1
Physical · Atoms, moles & gases

Atomic Structure

Planck's quantum theory

Energy of one photon

\[E=h\nu=\frac{hc}{\lambda}\]

Photoelectric effect

\[h\nu=h\nu_0+\tfrac12\,m_ev^2\]

\(h\nu_0\) = threshold energy (work function); \(\tfrac12 m_ev^2\) = kinetic energy of the ejected electron.

Bohr's model for hydrogen-like atoms

1. Quantization of angular momentum

\[mvr=n\,\frac{h}{2\pi}\]

2. Energy of the n-th orbit

\[E_n=-\frac{2\pi^2me^4}{h^2}\cdot\frac{Z^2}{n^2}=-2.178\times10^{-18}\,\frac{Z^2}{n^2}\ \mathrm{J/atom}=-13.6\,\frac{Z^2}{n^2}\ \mathrm{eV}\]

Writing \(E_1=\dfrac{2\pi^2me^4}{h^2}\) (the \(2.178\times10^{-18}\ \mathrm{J}\) constant), \(E_n=-E_1\dfrac{Z^2}{n^2}\).

3. Radius of the n-th orbit

\[r_n=\frac{n^2}{Z}\cdot\frac{h^2}{4\pi^2me^2}=0.529\,\frac{n^2}{Z}\ \mathring{\mathrm{A}}\]

4. Speed of the electron

\[v=\frac{2\pi Ze^2}{nh}=2.18\times10^{6}\,\frac{Z}{n}\ \mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\]

Wavelength of emitted photon

\[\bar\nu=\frac1\lambda=RZ^2\left(\frac{1}{n_1^2}-\frac{1}{n_2^2}\right)\]

\(R\) = Rydberg constant; transition from \(n_2\) to \(n_1\) (\(n_2\gt n_1\)).

Number of photons emitted

By a sample of H atoms (electrons de-exciting from level \(n\)):

\[\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\]

De Broglie wavelength

\[\lambda=\frac hp=\frac{h}{mv}\]

For a photon:

\[\lambda=\frac{h}{mc}\]

Heisenberg's uncertainty principle

\[\Delta x\cdot\Delta p\ge\frac{h}{4\pi}\]

or

\[m\,\Delta x\cdot\Delta v\ge\frac{h}{4\pi}\]

or

\[\Delta x\cdot\Delta v\ge\frac{h}{4\pi m}\]

Quantum numbers

  • Principal quantum number \(n=1,2,3,4,\dots\) to \(\infty\).
  • Orbital angular momentum of an electron in any (Bohr) orbit \(=\dfrac{nh}{2\pi}\).
  • Azimuthal quantum number \(l=0,1,\dots\) to \((n-1)\).
  • Number of orbitals in a subshell \(=2l+1\).
  • Maximum number of electrons in a particular subshell \(=2\times(2l+1)\).
  • Orbital angular momentum \(L=\dfrac{h}{2\pi}\sqrt{l(l+1)}=\sqrt{l(l+1)}\,\dfrac{h}{2\pi}\).
2
Physical · Atoms, moles & gases

Stoichiometry

Relative atomic mass

\[\text{R.A.M.}=\frac{\text{mass of one atom of an element}}{\frac{1}{12}\times\text{mass of one carbon atom}}\]

\(\approx\) total number of nucleons.

Y-map: mole, mass, volume and number

FromToOperation
MoleVolume at STP\(\times\ 22.4\ \mathrm{L}\)
Volume at STPMole\(\div\ 22.4\ \mathrm{L}\)
MoleNumber\(\times\ N_\mathrm{A}\)
NumberMole\(\div\ N_\mathrm{A}\)
MoleMass\(\times\) mol. wt. (molecule) or \(\times\) at. wt. (atom)
MassMole\(\div\) at. wt. or \(\div\) mol. wt.

Density and specific gravity

\[\text{Specific gravity}=\frac{\text{density of the substance}}{\text{density of water at }4^\circ\text{C}}\]

Density of gases

Absolute density (mass/volume):

\[\rho=\frac{\text{Molar mass of the gas}}{\text{Molar volume of the gas}}=\frac{PM}{RT}\]

Vapour density

\[\text{V.D.}=\frac{d_{\text{gas}}}{d_{\mathrm{H_2}}}=\frac{PM_{\text{gas}}/RT}{PM_{\mathrm{H_2}}/RT}=\frac{M_{\text{gas}}}{M_{\mathrm{H_2}}}=\frac{M_{\text{gas}}}{2}\]\[M_{\text{gas}}=2\times\text{V.D.}\]

Mole-mole analysis

FromToOperation
MassMole\(\div\) at. wt. / mol. wt.
Mole (of one species)Mole (of another)mole-mole relationship of the balanced equation
MoleVolume at STP\(\times\ 22.4\ \mathrm{L}\)
MoleMass\(\times\) mol. wt. / at. wt.

Molarity (M)

\[M=\frac{w/(\text{mol. wt. of solute})}{V\ (\text{in mL})}\times1000\]

\(w\) = mass of solute in g.

Molality (m)

\[m=\frac{\text{number of moles of solute}}{\text{mass of solvent in g}}\times1000=\frac{1000\,w_1}{M_1w_2}\]

\(w_1\), \(M_1\) = mass and molar mass of solute; \(w_2\) = mass of solvent in g.

Mole fraction (x)

\(n\) = moles of solute, \(N\) = moles of solvent.

\[x_{\text{solute}}=\frac{n}{n+N},\qquad x_{\text{solvent}}=\frac{N}{n+N}\]\[x_{\text{solute}}+x_{\text{solvent}}=1\]

Percentage calculations

  1. \(\%\ w/w=\dfrac{\text{mass of solute in g}}{\text{mass of solution in g}}\times100\)
  2. \(\%\ w/v=\dfrac{\text{mass of solute in g}}{\text{volume of solution in mL}}\times100\)
  3. \(\%\ v/v=\dfrac{\text{volume of solute in mL}}{\text{volume of solution}}\times100\)

Conversion between concentration terms

  1. Mole fraction of solute into molarity of solution: \(M=\dfrac{1000\,x_2\rho}{x_1M_1+x_2M_2}\)
  2. Molarity into mole fraction: \(x_2=\dfrac{MM_1}{1000\rho-MM_2+MM_1}\)
  3. Mole fraction into molality: \(m=\dfrac{1000\,x_2}{x_1M_1}\)
  4. Molality into mole fraction: \(x_2=\dfrac{mM_1}{1000+mM_1}\)
  5. Molality into molarity: \(M=\dfrac{1000\,m\rho}{1000+mM_2}\)
  6. Molarity into molality: \(m=\dfrac{1000\,M}{1000\rho-MM_2}\)

\(M_1\) and \(M_2\) are the molar masses of solvent and solute; \(\rho\) is the density of solution (g/mL); \(M\) = molarity (mol/L), \(m\) = molality (mol/kg), \(x_1\) = mole fraction of solvent, \(x_2\) = mole fraction of solute.

Average atomic mass

\[A_x=\frac{a_1x_1+a_2x_2+\cdots+a_nx_n}{100}\]

\(a_i\) = atomic mass of the i-th isotope, \(x_i\) = its percentage abundance.

Mean molar mass

\[M_{\text{avg.}}=\frac{n_1M_1+n_2M_2+\cdots+n_kM_k}{n_1+n_2+\cdots+n_k}=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{k}n_jM_j}{\sum_{j=1}^{k}n_j}\]

Individual oxidation number

Oxidation number = number of electrons in the valence shell − number of electrons left after bonding.

Equivalent weight

  • For elements: \(E=\dfrac{\text{atomic weight}}{\text{valency factor}}\)
  • For an acid/base: \(E=\dfrac{M}{\text{acidity/basicity}}\), where \(M\) = molar mass.
  • For an oxidising/reducing agent: \(E=\dfrac{M}{\text{no. of moles of }e^-\text{ lost/gained}}\)
  • In general: \(E=\dfrac{\text{atomic or molecular weight}}{\text{v.f.}}\) (v.f. = valency factor).

Number of equivalents

\[\text{No. of equivalents of solute}=\frac{W}{M/n}=\frac{W}{E}=\frac{\text{Wt.}}{\text{Eq. wt.}}\]\[\text{No. of equivalents}=\text{no. of moles of solute}\times\text{v.f.}\]

Normality (N)

\[N=\frac{\text{number of equivalents of solute}}{\text{volume of solution (in litres)}}\]\[N=\text{Molarity}\times\text{v.f.}\]

Valency factor

  • n-factor of an acid = basicity = no. of \(\mathrm{H^+}\) ion(s) furnished per molecule of the acid.
  • n-factor of a base = acidity = no. of \(\mathrm{OH^-}\) ion(s) furnished by the base per molecule.

At the equivalence point

\[N_1V_1=N_2V_2\]\[n_1M_1V_1=n_2M_2V_2\]

Volume strength of H₂O₂

20 V \(\mathrm{H_2O_2}\) means one litre of this sample of \(\mathrm{H_2O_2}\) on decomposition gives 20 L of \(\mathrm{O_2}\) gas at STP.

\[N\ (\mathrm{H_2O_2})=\frac{\text{Volume strength}}{5.6}\qquad M\ (\mathrm{H_2O_2})=\frac{\text{Volume strength}}{11.2}\]

Measurement of hardness

\[\text{Hardness in ppm}=\frac{\text{mass of }\mathrm{CaCO_3}}{\text{total mass of water}}\times10^{6}\]

Available chlorine in bleaching powder

\[\%\text{ of }\mathrm{Cl_2}=\frac{3.55\times x\times V\ (\mathrm{mL})}{W\ (\mathrm{g})}\]

\(x\) = molarity of hypo solution; \(V\) = volume (mL) of hypo solution used in titration; \(W\) = mass of bleaching powder sample.

3
Physical · Atoms, moles & gases

Gaseous State

Temperature scales

\[\frac{^\circ\mathrm{C}-0}{100}=\frac{^\circ\mathrm{F}-32}{212-32}=\frac{\mathrm{K}-273}{373-273}=\frac{R-R_0}{R_{100}-R_0}\]

\(R\) = temperature on the unknown scale; \(R_0\) and \(R_{100}\) are its readings at \(0^\circ\text{C}\) and \(100^\circ\text{C}\).

Boyle's law

At constant temperature, \(V\propto\dfrac1P\).

\[P_1V_1=P_2V_2\]

Charles' law

At constant pressure, \(V\propto T\).

\[\frac{V_1}{T_1}=\frac{V_2}{T_2}\]

Gay-Lussac's law

At constant volume, \(P\propto T\).

\[\frac{P_1}{T_1}=\frac{P_2}{T_2}\]

Temperature on the absolute scale.

Ideal gas equation

\[PV=nRT\]\[PV=\frac wm\,RT\qquad\text{or}\qquad P=\frac dm\,RT\qquad\text{or}\qquad Pm=dRT\]

Dalton's law of partial pressures

\[P_1=\frac{n_1RT}{V},\quad P_2=\frac{n_2RT}{V},\quad P_3=\frac{n_3RT}{V},\ \dots\]\[\text{Total pressure}=P_1+P_2+P_3+\cdots\]\[\text{Partial pressure}=\text{mole fraction}\times\text{total pressure}\]

Amagat's law of partial volumes

\[V=V_1+V_2+V_3+\cdots\]

Average molecular mass of a gaseous mixture

\[M_{\text{mix}}=\frac{\text{total mass of mixture}}{\text{total no. of moles in mixture}}=\frac{n_1M_1+n_2M_2+n_3M_3}{n_1+n_2+n_3}\]

Graham's law

Rate of diffusion \(r\propto\dfrac{1}{\sqrt d}\), where \(d\) = density of the gas.

\[\frac{r_1}{r_2}=\sqrt{\frac{d_2}{d_1}}=\sqrt{\frac{M_2}{M_1}}=\sqrt{\frac{\text{V.D.}_2}{\text{V.D.}_1}}\]

Kinetic theory of gases

Kinetic equation of gases

\[PV=\frac13\,mN\,\overline{u^2}\]

\(m\) = mass of one molecule, \(N\) = number of molecules, \(\overline{u^2}\) = mean square speed.

Average kinetic energy for one mole

\[N_\mathrm{A}\cdot\tfrac12\,m\overline{u^2}=\frac32\,k\,N_\mathrm{A}T=\frac32\,RT\]

Molecular speeds

Root mean square speed

\[u_{\text{rms}}=\sqrt{\frac{3RT}{M}}\]

Molar mass must be in kg/mol.

Average speed

\[u_{\text{av}}=\frac{u_1+u_2+u_3+\cdots+u_N}{N}=\sqrt{\frac{8RT}{\pi M}}=\sqrt{\frac{8kT}{\pi m}}\]

Most probable speed

\[u_{\text{mps}}=\sqrt{\frac{2RT}{M}}=\sqrt{\frac{2kT}{m}}\]

\(k\) is the Boltzmann constant.

Van der Waals equation

\[\left(P+\frac{an^2}{V^2}\right)(V-nb)=nRT\]

Critical constants

\[V_c=3b,\qquad P_c=\frac{a}{27b^2},\qquad T_c=\frac{8a}{27Rb}\]
4
Physical · Energetics & equilibrium

Thermodynamics

Thermodynamic processes

ProcessConditionConsequence
1. Isothermal\(T=\text{constant}\)\(dT=0,\ \Delta T=0\)
2. Isochoric\(V=\text{constant}\)\(dV=0,\ \Delta V=0\)
3. Isobaric\(P=\text{constant}\)\(dP=0,\ \Delta P=0\)
4. Adiabatic\(q=0\)no heat exchange with the surroundings (heat exchange is zero)

IUPAC sign convention for heat and work

  • Work done on the system = positive.
  • Work done by the system = negative.

First law of thermodynamics

\[\Delta U=U_2-U_1=q+w\]

Law of equipartition of energy

\[U=\frac f2\,nRT\quad\text{(only for an ideal gas)}\]\[\Delta E=\frac f2\,nR\,(\Delta T)\]

where \(f\) = degrees of freedom of the gas (translational + rotational):

Gas\(f\)
Monoatomic3
Diatomic or linear polyatomic5
Non-linear polyatomic6

Heat capacities and specific heat

QuantityDefinitionUnit
Total heat capacity\(C_T=\dfrac{dq}{dT}=\dfrac{q}{\Delta T}\)\(\mathrm{J\,{}^\circ C^{-1}}\)
Molar heat capacity\(C=\dfrac{dq}{n\,dT}=\dfrac{q}{n\,\Delta T}\)\(\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}\)
Specific heat capacity\(s=\dfrac{dq}{m\,dT}=\dfrac{q}{m\,\Delta T}\)\(\mathrm{J\,g^{-1}\,K^{-1}}\)
\[C_P=\frac{\gamma R}{\gamma-1},\qquad C_V=\frac{R}{\gamma-1}\]

Work: isothermal reversible process

Reversible isothermal expansion or compression of an ideal gas:

\[w=-nRT\ln\frac{V_f}{V_i}\]

Work: isochoric and isobaric processes

Reversible and irreversible isochoric process: since \(dV=0\),

\[dw=-P_{\text{ext}}\,dV=0\]

Reversible isobaric process:

\[w=-P\,(V_f-V_i)\]

Work: adiabatic reversible expansion

\[T_2V_2^{\,\gamma-1}=T_1V_1^{\,\gamma-1}\]

Reversible work:

\[w=\frac{P_2V_2-P_1V_1}{\gamma-1}=\frac{nR\,(T_2-T_1)}{\gamma-1}\]

Work: adiabatic irreversible process

Irreversible work:

\[w=\frac{P_2V_2-P_1V_1}{\gamma-1}=\frac{nR\,(T_2-T_1)}{\gamma-1}=nC_V\,(T_2-T_1)=-P_{\text{ext}}\,(V_2-V_1)\]

and use

\[\frac{P_1V_1}{T_1}=\frac{P_2V_2}{T_2}\]

Free expansion

Free expansion is always irreversible, and since \(P_{\text{ext}}=0\):

\[dw=-P_{\text{ext}}\,dV=0\]

If no heat is supplied, \(q=0\), then \(\Delta E=0\) so \(\Delta T=0\).

Application of the first law

\[\Delta U=Q+W,\qquad W=-P\,\Delta V\]\[\Delta U=Q-P\,\Delta V\]

Constant volume process

Heat given at constant volume = change in internal energy.

\[dU=(dq)_V,\qquad dU=nC_V\,dT\]\[C_V=\frac{dU}{dT}\cdot\frac1n=\frac f2\,R\]

Constant pressure process: enthalpy

\(H\) = enthalpy (a state function and an extensive property).

\[H=U+PV\]\[C_P-C_V=R\quad\text{(only for an ideal gas)}\]

Second law and entropy

For a spontaneous process:

\[\Delta S_{\text{universe}}=\Delta S_{\text{system}}+\Delta S_{\text{surroundings}}\gt 0\]

Entropy change of the system:

\[\Delta S_{\text{system}}=\int_A^B\frac{dq_{\text{rev}}}{T}\]

Entropy change for an ideal gas undergoing a process (state A \((P_1,V_1,T_1)\) to state B \((P_2,V_2,T_2)\), by an irreversible or reversible path):

\[\Delta S_{\text{system}}=nC_V\ln\frac{T_2}{T_1}+nR\ln\frac{V_2}{V_1}\quad\text{(only for an ideal gas)}\]

Third law of thermodynamics

The entropy of perfect crystals of all pure elements and compounds is zero at the absolute zero of temperature.

Gibbs free energy and spontaneity

\(G\) is a state function and an extensive property.

\[\Delta G_{\text{system}}=\Delta H_{\text{system}}-T\,\Delta S_{\text{system}}\]

Criteria of spontaneity:

  1. If \(\Delta G_{\text{system}}\lt 0\) (negative), the process is spontaneous.
  2. If \(\Delta G_{\text{system}}\gt 0\), the process is non-spontaneous.
  3. If \(\Delta G_{\text{system}}=0\), the system is at equilibrium.

Physical interpretation of ΔG

\(\Delta G\) is the maximum amount of non-expansional (non-compression) work that can be performed.

\[\Delta G=dw_{\text{non-exp}}=dH-T\,dS\]

Standard free energy change (ΔG°)

  1. \(\Delta G^\circ=-2.303\,RT\log_{10}K\)
  2. At equilibrium \(\Delta G=0\).
  3. The decrease in free energy is the net work: \(-\Delta G=W_{\text{net}}=2.303\,nRT\log_{10}\dfrac{V_2}{V_1}\)
  4. \(\Delta G^\circ_f\) for an element in its standard state \(=0\).
  5. \(\Delta G^\circ_{\text{reaction}}=\sum\Delta G^\circ_f(\text{products})-\sum\Delta G^\circ_f(\text{reactants})\)

Thermochemistry: standard enthalpy change and endo/exothermic reactions

Change in standard enthalpy = heat added at constant pressure:

\[\Delta H^\circ=H^\circ_{2,m}-H^\circ_{1,m}=C_P\,\Delta T\]
  • If \(H_{\text{products}}\gt H_{\text{reactants}}\), the reaction is endothermic, since extra heat has to be given to the reactants to convert them into products.
  • If \(H_{\text{products}}\lt H_{\text{reactants}}\), the reaction is exothermic, since the extra heat content of the reactants is released during the reaction.

Enthalpy change of a reaction

\[\Delta H_{\text{reaction}}=H_{\text{products}}-H_{\text{reactants}}\]\[\Delta H^\circ_{\text{reaction}}=H^\circ_{\text{products}}-H^\circ_{\text{reactants}}\]
  • Positive \(\Delta H\): endothermic.
  • Negative \(\Delta H\): exothermic.

Kirchhoff's equation

Temperature dependence of \(\Delta H\). For a constant pressure reaction:

\[\Delta H_2^\circ=\Delta H_1^\circ+\Delta C_P\,(T_2-T_1)\]

where \(\Delta C_P=\sum C_P(\text{products})-\sum C_P(\text{reactants})\).

For a constant volume reaction:

\[\Delta E_2^\circ=\Delta E_1^\circ+\int_{T_1}^{T_2}\Delta C_V\,dT\]

Enthalpy of reaction from enthalpies of formation

\[\Delta H^\circ_r=\sum_B\nu_B\,\Delta H^\circ_{f,\text{products}}-\sum_B\nu_B\,\Delta H^\circ_{f,\text{reactants}}\]

\(\nu_B\) = stoichiometric coefficient.

Enthalpy of reaction from bond enthalpies

\[\Delta H=\left(\begin{array}{c}\text{enthalpy required to break}\\\text{reactants into gaseous atoms}\end{array}\right)-\left(\begin{array}{c}\text{enthalpy released to form}\\\text{products from the gaseous atoms}\end{array}\right)\]

Resonance energy

\[\Delta H^\circ_{\text{resonance}}=\Delta H^\circ_{f,\text{experimental}}-\Delta H^\circ_{f,\text{calculated}}\]\[=\Delta H^\circ_{c,\text{calculated}}-\Delta H^\circ_{c,\text{experimental}}\]
5
Physical · Energetics & equilibrium

Chemical Equilibrium

At equilibrium

  1. Rate of forward reaction = rate of backward reaction.
  2. Concentrations (mol/L) of reactants and products become constant.
  3. \(\Delta G=0\).
  4. \(Q=K_{eq}\).

Equilibrium constant (K)

\[K=\frac{\text{rate constant of forward reaction}}{\text{rate constant of backward reaction}}=\frac{K_f}{K_b}\]

For \(\mathrm{aA+bB\rightleftharpoons cC+dD}\):

In terms of concentration:

\[K_C=\frac{[\mathrm{C}]^c[\mathrm{D}]^d}{[\mathrm{A}]^a[\mathrm{B}]^b}\]

In terms of partial pressure:

\[K_P=\frac{P_{\mathrm C}^{\,c}\,P_{\mathrm D}^{\,d}}{P_{\mathrm A}^{\,a}\,P_{\mathrm B}^{\,b}}\]

In terms of mole fraction:

\[K_x=\frac{x_{\mathrm C}^{\,c}\,x_{\mathrm D}^{\,d}}{x_{\mathrm A}^{\,a}\,x_{\mathrm B}^{\,b}}\]

Relations between Kp, Kc and Kx

\[K_p=K_c\,(RT)^{\Delta n}\]\[K_P=K_x\,(P)^{\Delta n}\]

\(\Delta n\) = moles of gaseous products − moles of gaseous reactants; \(P\) = total pressure.

Effect of temperature on K

\[\log\frac{K_2}{K_1}=\frac{\Delta H}{2.303\,R}\left(\frac1{T_1}-\frac1{T_2}\right)\]

\(\Delta H\) = enthalpy of reaction.

K and standard free energy change

\[\Delta G^\circ=-2.303\,RT\log K\]

Reaction quotient (Q)

The value of the expression (concentrations at any instant):

\[Q=\frac{[\mathrm{C}]^c[\mathrm{D}]^d}{[\mathrm{A}]^a[\mathrm{B}]^b}\]

At equilibrium \(Q=K_{eq}\).

Degree of dissociation (α)

\[\alpha=\frac{\text{no. of moles dissociated}}{\text{initial no. of moles taken}}\]

= fraction of moles dissociated out of 1 mole.

% dissociation = \(\alpha\times100\).

Observed molecular weight and vapour density of the mixture

For \(\mathrm{A_n(g)\rightleftharpoons nA(g)}\) with degree of dissociation \(\alpha\):

\[M_{\text{obs}}=\frac{\text{mass of equilibrium mixture}}{\text{total no. of moles}}=\frac{M_{\text{th}}}{1+(n-1)\alpha}\]\[D_{\text{obs}}=\frac{d_{\text{th}}}{1+(n-1)\alpha}\]

\(M_{\text{th}}\), \(d_{\text{th}}\) = theoretical molecular weight and vapour density of \(\mathrm{A_n}\).

Le Chatelier's principle

If a system at equilibrium is subjected to a disturbance or stress that changes any of the factors that determine the state of equilibrium, the system will react in such a way as to minimize the effect of the disturbance.

Effect of concentration

  • If the concentration of a reactant is increased at equilibrium, the reaction shifts in the forward direction.
  • If the concentration of a product is increased, the equilibrium shifts in the backward direction.

Effect of volume

  • If volume is increased, pressure decreases, hence the reaction shifts in the direction in which pressure increases, that is, the direction in which the number of moles of gases increases (and vice versa).
  • If volume is increased, then for \(\Delta n\gt 0\) the reaction shifts forward.
  • \(\Delta n\lt 0\): the reaction shifts backward.
  • \(\Delta n=0\): the reaction does not shift.

Effect of pressure

If pressure is increased at equilibrium, the reaction tries to decrease the pressure, hence it shifts in the direction in which fewer moles of gas are formed.

Effect of inert gas addition

(i) Constant pressure: on adding inert gas, the volume is increased to keep the pressure constant. Hence the equilibrium shifts in the direction in which a larger number of moles of gas is formed.

  • \(\Delta n\gt 0\): shifts forward.
  • \(\Delta n\lt 0\): shifts backward.
  • \(\Delta n=0\): no shift.

(ii) Constant volume: inert gas addition has no effect.

Effect of temperature

The equilibrium constant depends only on temperature. A plot of \(\ln K\) vs \(\dfrac1T\) is a straight line with

\[\text{slope}=-\frac{\Delta H}{R},\qquad\text{intercept}=\frac{\Delta S}{R}\]
  • Endothermic reaction (\(\Delta H\gt 0\)): the value of \(K\) increases with rise in temperature.
  • Exothermic reaction (\(\Delta H\lt 0\)): the value of \(K\) decreases with rise in temperature.
  • For \(\Delta H\gt 0\), the reaction shifts in the forward direction with increase in temperature.
  • For \(\Delta H\lt 0\), the reaction shifts in the backward direction with increase in temperature.

Vapour pressure of liquid: relative humidity

\[\text{Relative humidity}=\frac{\text{partial pressure of }\mathrm{H_2O}\text{ vapours}}{\text{vapour pressure of }\mathrm{H_2O}\text{ at that temperature}}\]

Thermodynamics of equilibrium

\[\Delta G=\Delta G^0+2.303\,RT\log_{10}Q\]

Van't Hoff equation:

\[\log\frac{K_1}{K_2}=\frac{\Delta H^0}{2.303\,R}\left(\frac1{T_2}-\frac1{T_1}\right)\]
6
Physical · Energetics & equilibrium

Ionic Equilibrium

Ostwald dilution law

Dissociation constant of a weak acid:

\[K_a=\frac{[\mathrm{H^+}][\mathrm{A^-}]}{[\mathrm{HA}]}=\frac{C\alpha\cdot C\alpha}{C(1-\alpha)}=\frac{C\alpha^2}{1-\alpha}\]

If \(\alpha\ll1\), then \(1-\alpha\approx1\), so

\[K_a=C\alpha^2\quad\text{or}\quad\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{K_a}{C}}=\sqrt{K_aV}\]

Similarly for a weak base, \(\alpha=\sqrt{K_b/C}\). The higher the value of \(K_a/K_b\), the stronger is the acid/base.

Acidity and the pH scale

\(\mathrm{pH}=-\log a_{\mathrm H^+}\), where \(a_{\mathrm H^+}\) is the activity of \(\mathrm{H^+}\) ions (= molar concentration for dilute solutions).

\[\mathrm{pH}=-\log[\mathrm{H^+}];\quad[\mathrm{H^+}]=10^{-\mathrm{pH}}\]\[\mathrm{pOH}=-\log[\mathrm{OH^-}];\quad[\mathrm{OH^-}]=10^{-\mathrm{pOH}}\]\[\mathrm{p}K_a=-\log K_a;\quad K_a=10^{-\mathrm{p}K_a}\]\[\mathrm{p}K_b=-\log K_b;\quad K_b=10^{-\mathrm{p}K_b}\]

pH can also be negative or greater than 14.

Properties of water

  1. In pure water \([\mathrm{H^+}]=[\mathrm{OH^-}]\), so it is neutral.
  2. Molar concentration (molarity) of water = 55.56 M.
  3. Ionic product of water: \(K_w=[\mathrm{H^+}][\mathrm{OH^-}]=10^{-14}\) at \(25^\circ\text{C}\) (experimentally).
    • \(\mathrm{pH}=7=\mathrm{pOH}\): neutral
    • \(\mathrm{pH}\lt 7\) or \(\mathrm{pOH}\gt 7\): acidic
    • \(\mathrm{pH}\gt 7\) or \(\mathrm{pOH}\lt 7\): basic
  4. Degree of dissociation of water: \(\alpha=\dfrac{\text{no. of moles dissociated}}{\text{total no. of moles initially taken}}=\dfrac{10^{-7}}{55.55}=1.8\times10^{-9}\) or \(1.8\times10^{-7}\%\)
  5. Absolute dissociation constant of water: \(K_a=K_b=\dfrac{[\mathrm{H^+}][\mathrm{OH^-}]}{[\mathrm{H_2O}]}=\dfrac{10^{-7}\times10^{-7}}{55.55}=1.8\times10^{-16}\)
    \(\mathrm{p}K_a=\mathrm{p}K_b=-\log(1.8\times10^{-16})=16-\log1.8=15.74\)

Conjugate acid-base pairs

\[K_a\times K_b=[\mathrm{H^+}][\mathrm{OH^-}]=K_w\]\[\mathrm{p}K_a+\mathrm{p}K_b=\mathrm{p}K_w=14\ \text{ at }25^\circ\text{C}\]
  • \(\mathrm{p}K_a\) of \(\mathrm{H_3O^+}\) ions \(=-1.74\)
  • \(\mathrm{p}K_b\) of \(\mathrm{OH^-}\) ions \(=-1.74\)

pH of strong acid and strong base solutions

(a) Strong acid solution

  • If the concentration is greater than \(10^{-6}\,\mathrm{M}\): \(\mathrm{H^+}\) ions coming from water can be neglected, so \([\mathrm{H^+}]=C\) and \(\mathrm{pH}=-\log C\).
  • If the concentration is less than \(10^{-6}\,\mathrm{M}\): \(\mathrm{H^+}\) ions coming from water cannot be neglected, so \([\mathrm{H^+}]=C+\dfrac{K_w}{[\mathrm{H^+}]}\).

(b) Strong base solution

Using a similar method as in (a), first calculate \([\mathrm{OH^-}]\) and then use \([\mathrm{H^+}]\times[\mathrm{OH^-}]=10^{-14}\).

pH of mixtures of strong acids and bases

Number of \(\mathrm{H^+}\) ions from solution 1 \(=N_1V_1\); from solution 2 \(=N_2V_2\).

(c) Mixture of two strong acids

\[[\mathrm{H^+}]=N=\frac{N_1V_1+N_2V_2}{V_1+V_2}\]

(d) Mixture of two strong bases

\[[\mathrm{OH^-}]=N=\frac{N_1V_1+N_2V_2}{V_1+V_2}\]

(e) Mixture of a strong acid and a strong base

If \(N_1V_1\gt N_2V_2\), the solution is acidic and

\[[\mathrm{H^+}]=N=\frac{N_1V_1-N_2V_2}{V_1+V_2}\]

If \(N_2V_2\gt N_1V_1\), the solution is basic and

\[[\mathrm{OH^-}]=N=\frac{N_2V_2-N_1V_1}{V_1+V_2}\]

pH of a weak (monoprotic) acid solution

\[K_a=\frac{[\mathrm{H^+}][\mathrm{A^-}]}{[\mathrm{HA}]}=\frac{C\alpha^2}{1-\alpha}\]

If \(\alpha\ll1\), \(1-\alpha\approx1\) and \(K_a\approx C\alpha^2\), so

\[\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{K_a}{C}},\qquad[\mathrm{H^+}]=C\alpha\]

(valid if \(\alpha\lt0.1\), i.e. 10%).

On increasing the dilution: \(C\downarrow\), \(\alpha\uparrow\), \([\mathrm{H^+}]\downarrow\), \(\mathrm{pH}\uparrow\).

Relative strength of two acids

\[\frac{[\mathrm{H^+}]\text{ furnished by acid 1}}{[\mathrm{H^+}]\text{ furnished by acid 2}}=\frac{c_1\alpha_1}{c_2\alpha_2}=\sqrt{\frac{c_1K_{a1}}{c_2K_{a2}}}\]

Salt hydrolysis

Salt ofType of hydrolysis\(K_h\)\(h\)pH
(a) weak acid & strong baseanionic\(\dfrac{K_w}{K_a}\)\(\sqrt{\dfrac{K_w}{K_a\,c}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{K_h}{c}}\)\(7+\dfrac12\mathrm{p}K_a+\dfrac12\log c\)
(b) strong acid & weak basecationic\(\dfrac{K_w}{K_b}\)\(\sqrt{\dfrac{K_w}{K_b\,c}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{K_h}{c}}\)\(7-\dfrac12\mathrm{p}K_b-\dfrac12\log c\)
(c) weak acid & weak baseboth\(\dfrac{K_w}{K_aK_b}\)\(\sqrt{\dfrac{K_w}{K_aK_b}}\)\(7+\dfrac12\mathrm{p}K_a-\dfrac12\mathrm{p}K_b\)
(d) strong acid & strong basedo not hydrolyse\(\mathrm{pH}=7\)

Hydrolysis of polyvalent anions or cations

For \(\mathrm{Na_3PO_4}\) of concentration \(C\):

\[K_{a1}\times K_{h3}=K_w,\quad K_{a2}\times K_{h2}=K_w,\quad K_{a3}\times K_{h1}=K_w\]

Generally pH is calculated using only the first step of hydrolysis.

\[K_{h1}=\frac{Ch^2}{1-h}\approx Ch^2,\qquad h=\sqrt{\frac{K_{h1}}{C}}\]\[[\mathrm{OH^-}]=Ch=\sqrt{K_{h1}\,C},\qquad[\mathrm{H^+}]=\sqrt{\frac{K_wK_{a3}}{C}}\]\[\mathrm{pH}=\frac12\left[\mathrm{p}K_w+\mathrm{p}K_{a3}+\log C\right]\]

Buffer solution

(a) Acidic buffer, e.g. \(\mathrm{CH_3COOH}\) and \(\mathrm{CH_3COONa}\) (weak acid and a salt of its conjugate base):

\[\mathrm{pH}=\mathrm{p}K_a+\log\frac{[\text{Salt}]}{[\text{Acid}]}\quad\text{[Henderson's equation]}\]

(b) Basic buffer, e.g. \(\mathrm{NH_4OH}\) and \(\mathrm{NH_4Cl}\) (weak base and a salt of its conjugate acid):

\[\mathrm{pOH}=\mathrm{p}K_b+\log\frac{[\text{Salt}]}{[\text{Base}]}\]

Solubility product and precipitation

For the salt \(\mathrm{A_xB_y}\) with solubility \(s\):

\[K_{sp}=(xs)^x(ys)^y=x^x\,y^y\,(s)^{x+y}\]

Condition for precipitation:

  • If the ionic product \(K_{I.P.}\gt K_{sp}\), precipitation occurs.
  • If \(K_{I.P.}=K_{sp}\), the solution is saturated (precipitation just begins or is just prevented).

Common ion effect, selective precipitation and indicators

  • Common ion effect: adding \(\mathrm{A^-}\) to a weak acid HA suppresses its dissociation (\(\alpha\) falls, pH rises); the same logic reduces solubility: for AgCl in NaCl of concentration \(c\), \(s=\dfrac{K_{sp}}{c}\); for \(\mathrm{A_xB_y}\) in a common ion, solve \(K_{sp}=(xs)^x(ys+c)^y\).
  • Selective precipitation: the ion needing the smaller concentration of reagent to reach its \(K_{sp}\) precipitates first; the first is considered completely removed when its concentration falls to \(10^{-5}\) M. Hydroxide precipitation gives \(\mathrm{pH}\) for \(\mathrm{M(OH)_n}\): \([\mathrm{OH^-}]=\left(\dfrac{K_{sp}}{[\mathrm M^{n+}]}\right)^{1/n}\).
  • Indicators: \(\mathrm{HIn\rightleftharpoons H^++In^-}\); \(\mathrm{pH}=\mathrm{p}K_{In}+\log\dfrac{[\mathrm{In^-}]}{[\mathrm{HIn}]}\); colour change range \(\mathrm{p}K_{In}\pm1\) (colour of one form shows when it is 10× the other). Phenolphthalein 8.3–10, methyl orange 3.1–4.4.
  • Buffer capacity is maximum when \([\mathrm{salt}]=[\mathrm{acid}]\), i.e. \(\mathrm{pH}=\mathrm{p}K_a\); useful range \(\mathrm{p}K_a\pm1\). Titration curve midpoint of a weak acid has \(\mathrm{pH}=\mathrm{p}K_a\).
7
Physical · Energetics & equilibrium

Electrochemistry

Electrode potential

  • For any electrode: oxidation potential \(=-\) reduction potential.
  • \(E_{\text{cell}}=\text{R.P. of cathode}-\text{R.P. of anode}\)
  • \(E_{\text{cell}}=\text{R.P. of cathode}+\text{O.P. of anode}\)
  • \(E_{\text{cell}}\) is always a positive quantity, and the anode is the electrode of lower R.P.
  • \(E^\circ_{\text{cell}}=\text{SRP of cathode}-\text{SRP of anode}\)
  • The greater the SRP value, the greater the oxidising power.

Gibbs free energy change

\[\Delta G=-nFE_{\text{cell}}\]\[\Delta G^\circ=-nFE^\circ_{\text{cell}}\]

Nernst equation

Effect of concentration and temperature on the emf of a cell.

\[\Delta G=\Delta G^\circ+RT\ln Q\quad(Q=\text{reaction quotient}),\qquad\Delta G^\circ=-RT\ln K_{eq}\]\[E_{\text{cell}}=E^\circ_{\text{cell}}-\frac{RT}{nF}\ln Q=E^\circ_{\text{cell}}-\frac{2.303\,RT}{nF}\log Q\]\[E_{\text{cell}}=E^\circ_{\text{cell}}-\frac{0.0591}{n}\log Q\quad[\text{at }298\ \mathrm{K}]\]

For an electrode \(\mathrm{M(s)/M^{n+}}\):

\[E_{\mathrm{M^{n+}/M}}=E^\circ_{\mathrm{M^{n+}/M}}-\frac{2.303\,RT}{nF}\log\frac1{[\mathrm{M^{n+}}]}\]

At chemical equilibrium

\(\Delta G=0\); \(E_{\text{cell}}=0\).

\[\log K_{eq}=\frac{nE^\circ_{\text{cell}}}{0.0591}\]\[E^\circ_{\text{cell}}=\frac{0.0591}{n}\log K_{eq}\]

Concentration cell

A cell in which both electrodes are made of the same material. For all concentration cells \(E^\circ_{\text{cell}}=0\).

(a) Electrolyte concentration cell, e.g. \(\mathrm{Zn(s)\,|\,Zn^{2+}}(c_1)\,\|\,\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}(c_2)\,|\,\mathrm{Zn(s)}\)

\[E=\frac{0.0591}{2}\log\frac{c_2}{c_1}\]

(b) Electrode concentration cell, e.g. \(\mathrm{Pt,H_2}(P_1\ \text{atm})\,|\,\mathrm{H^+}(1\,\mathrm{M})\,|\,\mathrm{H_2}(P_2\ \text{atm}),\mathrm{Pt}\)

\[E=\frac{0.0591}{2}\log\frac{P_1}{P_2}\]

Different types of electrodes

TypeElectrodeReduction reactionElectrode potential
1. Metal–metal ion electrode\(\mathrm{M(s)/M^{n+}}\)\(\mathrm{M^{n+}+ne^-\to M(s)}\)\(E=E^\circ+\dfrac{0.0591}{n}\log[\mathrm{M^{n+}}]\)
2. Gas–ion electrode (as a reduction electrode)\(\mathrm{Pt\,/\,H_2}(P\ \text{atm})\,/\,\mathrm{H^+}(X\,\mathrm{M})\)\(\mathrm{H^+(aq)+e^-\to\tfrac12H_2}(P\ \text{atm})\)\(E=E^\circ-0.0591\log\dfrac{P_{\mathrm{H_2}}^{1/2}}{[\mathrm{H^+}]}\)
3. Oxidation–reduction electrode (as a reduction electrode)\(\mathrm{Pt\,/\,Fe^{2+},Fe^{3+}}\)\(\mathrm{Fe^{3+}+e^-\to Fe^{2+}}\)\(E=E^\circ-0.0591\log\dfrac{[\mathrm{Fe^{2+}}]}{[\mathrm{Fe^{3+}}]}\)
4. Metal–metal insoluble salt electrode (as a reduction electrode)\(\mathrm{Ag/AgCl/Cl^-}\)\(\mathrm{AgCl(s)+e^-\to Ag(s)+Cl^-}\)\(E_{\mathrm{Cl^-/AgCl/Ag}}=E^\circ_{\mathrm{Cl^-/AgCl/Ag}}-0.0591\log[\mathrm{Cl^-}]\)

Electrolysis: order of deposition

(a) Cations, increasing order of deposition:

\[\mathrm{K^+\lt Ca^{2+}\lt Na^+\lt Mg^{2+}\lt Al^{3+}\lt Zn^{2+}\lt Fe^{2+}\lt H^+\lt Cu^{2+}\lt Ag^+\lt Au^{3+}}\]

(b) Anions: similarly, the anion which is the stronger reducing agent (low value of SRP) is liberated first at the anode. Increasing order of deposition:

\[\mathrm{SO_4^{2-}\lt NO_3^-\lt OH^-\lt Cl^-\lt Br^-\lt I^-}\]

Faraday's laws of electrolysis

First law:

\[w=Zq=Zit\]

\(Z\) = electrochemical equivalent of the substance.

Second law: \(W\propto E\), i.e.

\[\frac WE=\text{constant},\qquad\frac{W_1}{E_1}=\frac{W_2}{E_2}\]\[\frac WE=\frac{it}{96500}\times\text{current efficiency factor}\]\[\text{Current efficiency}=\frac{\text{actual mass deposited/produced}}{\text{theoretical mass deposited/produced}}\times100\]

Simultaneous deposition of Cu and Fe at the cathode

Condition for the simultaneous deposition of Cu and Fe on the cathode (equal reduction potentials):

\[E^\circ_{\mathrm{Cu^{2+}/Cu}}-\frac{0.0591}{2}\log\frac1{[\mathrm{Cu^{2+}}]}=E^\circ_{\mathrm{Fe^{2+}/Fe}}-\frac{0.0591}{2}\log\frac1{[\mathrm{Fe^{2+}}]}\]

Conductance and conductivity

\[\text{Conductance}=\frac1{\text{Resistance}}\]

Specific conductance or conductivity (reciprocal of specific resistance): \(\kappa=\dfrac1\rho\)

\[\text{specific conductance}=\text{conductance}\times\frac la\]

Equivalent conductance: \(\Lambda_E=\dfrac{1000\,\kappa}{\text{Normality}}\); unit \(\mathrm{ohm^{-1}\,cm^2\,eq^{-1}}\).

Molar conductance: \(\Lambda_m=\dfrac{1000\,\kappa}{\text{Molarity}}\); unit \(\mathrm{ohm^{-1}\,cm^2\,mol^{-1}}\).

Kohlrausch's law

Variation of \(\Lambda_{eq}/\Lambda_M\) of a solution with concentration:

(i) Strong electrolyte

\[\Lambda_M^c=\Lambda_M^\infty-b\sqrt c\]

(ii) Weak electrolyte

\[\Lambda_M^\infty=n_+\lambda_+^\infty+n_-\lambda_-^\infty\]

where \(\lambda\) is the molar conductivity of the ion, \(n_+\) = number of cations obtained after dissociation per formula unit, \(n_-\) = number of anions obtained after dissociation per formula unit.

Applications of Kohlrausch's law

  1. Calculation of \(\Lambda_M^\infty\) of weak electrolytes:\[\Lambda_M^\infty(\mathrm{CH_3COOH})=\Lambda_M^\infty(\mathrm{CH_3COONa})+\Lambda_M^\infty(\mathrm{HCl})-\Lambda_M^\infty(\mathrm{NaCl})\]
  2. Degree of dissociation of a weak electrolyte:\[\alpha=\frac{\Lambda_m^c}{\Lambda_m^\infty};\qquad K_{eq}=\frac{C\alpha^2}{1-\alpha}\]
  3. Solubility (\(S\)) of a sparingly soluble salt and its \(K_{sp}\):\[\Lambda_M^c=\Lambda_M^\infty=\frac{\kappa\times1000}{\text{solubility}},\qquad K_{sp}=S^2\]

Transport number

\[t_c=\frac{u_c}{u_c+u_a},\qquad t_a=\frac{u_a}{u_c+u_a}\]

\(t_c\) = transport number of the cation, \(t_a\) = transport number of the anion (\(u\) = ionic mobility).

Commercial cells, fuel cells and corrosion

  • Dry (Leclanché) cell: Zn anode, graphite cathode, \(\mathrm{MnO_2+NH_4Cl}\) paste; 1.5 V, not rechargeable. Mercury cell: constant 1.35 V. Lead storage battery: \(\mathrm{Pb}\) anode, \(\mathrm{PbO_2}\) cathode, 38% \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\); \(\mathrm{Pb+PbO_2+2H_2SO_4\rightleftharpoons2PbSO_4+2H_2O}\), \(E\approx2\) V per cell (12 V from six). Ni–Cd cell: \(\mathrm{Cd+2Ni(OH)_3\to CdO+2Ni(OH)_2+H_2O}\), 1.4 V.
  • H₂–O₂ fuel cell: anode \(\mathrm{2H_2+4OH^-\to4H_2O+4e^-}\); cathode \(\mathrm{O_2+2H_2O+4e^-\to4OH^-}\); overall \(\mathrm{2H_2+O_2\to2H_2O}\), \(E^\circ=1.23\) V, efficiency about 70%.
  • Corrosion of iron: anode \(\mathrm{Fe\to Fe^{2+}+2e^-}\) (\(E^\circ=-0.44\) V); cathode \(\mathrm{O_2+4H^++4e^-\to2H_2O}\) (\(E^\circ=1.23\) V); rust \(\mathrm{Fe_2O_3\cdot xH_2O}\). Prevention: galvanisation, sacrificial anode (Mg, Zn), painting, cathodic protection.
8
Physical · Solutions, solids & kinetics

Solution & Colligative Properties

Osmotic pressure

(i) Equilibrium height method: \(\pi=\rho gh\), where \(\rho\) = density of the solution and \(h\) = equilibrium height.

(ii) van't Hoff formula (for calculation of osmotic pressure):

\[\pi=CST=CRT=\frac nV\,RT\]

(just like the ideal gas equation; \(S=R\)). \(C\) = total concentration of all types of particles:

\[C=C_1+C_2+C_3+\dots,\qquad \pi=\frac{(n_1+n_2+n_3+\dots)}{V}\,RT\]

Osmotic pressure on mixing solutions

If \(V_1\) mL of concentration \(C_1\) and \(V_2\) mL of concentration \(C_2\) are mixed:

\[\pi=\frac{C_1V_1+C_2V_2}{V_1+V_2}\,RT=\frac{\pi_1V_1+\pi_2V_2}{V_1+V_2}\]

Types of solutions

  • (a) Isotonic solutions: two solutions having the same osmotic pressure, \(\pi_1=\pi_2\) (at the same temperature).
  • (b) Hypertonic: if \(\pi_1\gt\pi_2\), the 1st solution is hypertonic with respect to the 2nd solution.
  • (c) Hypotonic: the 2nd solution is hypotonic with respect to the 1st solution.

Abnormal colligative properties: van't Hoff correction factor (i)

For association or dissociation of the solute:

Quantity compared\(i\)
Colligative property\(\dfrac{\text{observed (experimental / actual / abnormal) value}}{\text{theoretical value}}\)
Number of particles / concentration\(\dfrac{\text{observed (experimental) no. of particles}}{\text{theoretical no. of particles}}\)
Molality\(\dfrac{\text{observed molality}}{\text{theoretical molality}}\)
Molar mass\(\dfrac{\text{theoretical molar mass (formula mass)}}{\text{observed / experimental (apparent) molar mass}}\)
  • \(i\gt1\): dissociation.
  • \(i\lt1\): association.

Osmotic pressure with the correction factor:

\[\pi=iCRT=(i_1C_1+i_2C_2+i_3C_3+\dots)\,RT\]

Relation between i and degree of dissociation or association (α)

Dissociation: for \(\mathrm{A_xB_y\to xA^{y+}+yB^{x-}}\)

\[i=1+(n-1)\,\alpha,\qquad n=x+y\]

Association (\(n\) molecules associate into one):

\[i=1+\left(\frac1n-1\right)\alpha\]

Relative lowering of vapour pressure (RLVP): Raoult's law for non-volatile solutes

Vapour pressure of the solution \(P_s\lt P^\circ\) (pure solvent). Lowering in vapour pressure \(=P^\circ-P_s\).

\[\text{RLVP}=\frac{P^\circ-P_s}{P^\circ}\]

Raoult's law (for non-volatile solutes): experimentally, the relative lowering in vapour pressure equals the mole fraction of the non-volatile solute in the solution.

\[\frac{P^\circ-P_s}{P^\circ}=X_{\text{solute}}=\frac{n}{n+N}\]\[\frac{P^\circ-P_s}{P_s}=\frac nN\]\[\frac{P^\circ-P_s}{P_s}=(\text{molality})\times\frac{M}{1000}\]

\(n\) = moles of solute, \(N\) = moles of solvent, \(M\) = molar mass of solvent.

If the solute gets associated or dissociated:

\[\frac{P^\circ-P_s}{P^\circ}=\frac{i\,n}{N+i\,n},\qquad\frac{P^\circ-P_s}{P_s}=\frac{i\,n}{N}\]\[\frac{P^\circ-P_s}{P_s}=i\times(\text{molality})\times\frac{M}{1000}\]

Raoult's law (solvent form)

(i) Vapour pressure of the solvent:

\[p_1=p_1^\circ\,X_1\]

where \(X_1\) is the mole fraction of the solvent (liquid).

(ii) An alternate form:

\[\frac{p_1^\circ-p_1}{p_1^\circ}=X_2\]

Elevation in boiling point

\[\Delta T_b=i\times K_b\,m\]\[K_b=\frac{RT_b^2}{1000\,L_{vap}}\quad\text{or}\quad K_b=\frac{M\,RT_b^2}{1000\,\Delta H_{vap}},\qquad L_{vap}=\frac{\Delta H_{vap}}{M}\]

\(K_b\) = molal elevation constant; \(T_b\) = boiling point of the pure solvent (K); \(M\) = molar mass of solvent.

Depression in freezing point

\[\Delta T_f=i\times K_f\,m\]

\(K_f\) = molal depression constant:

\[K_f=\frac{RT_f^2}{1000\,L_{fusion}}=\frac{M\,RT_f^2}{1000\,\Delta H_{fusion}}\]

\(T_f\) = freezing point of the pure solvent (K); \(M\) = molar mass of solvent.

Raoult's law for a binary (ideal) mixture of volatile liquids

\[P_A=X_AP_A^\circ,\qquad P_B=X_BP_B^\circ\]
  • If \(P_A^\circ\gt P_B^\circ\), then A is more volatile than B.
  • The B.P. of A is lower than the B.P. of B.

Dalton's law and composition of the vapour

\[P_T=P_A+P_B=X_AP_A^\circ+X_BP_B^\circ\]

\(x_A'\) = mole fraction of A in the vapour above the liquid/solution; \(x_B'\) = mole fraction of B in the vapour.

\[P_A=X_AP_A^\circ=x_A'\,P_T,\qquad P_B=X_BP_B^\circ=x_B'\,P_T\]\[1=P_T\left(\frac{x_A'}{P_A^\circ}+\frac{x_B'}{P_B^\circ}\right)\]

Graphical representation: vapour pressure against composition (ideal mixture)

Plot of vapour pressure against \(X_A\) for A more volatile than B (\(P_A^\circ\gt P_B^\circ\)); all three curves are straight lines.

CurveAt \(X_A=0\) (pure B)At \(X_A=1\) (pure A)
\(P_A\)0\(P_A^\circ\)
\(P_B\)\(P_B^\circ\)0
\(P_T=P_A+P_B\)\(P_B^\circ\)\(P_A^\circ\)

Ideal solutions (mixtures)

Mixtures which follow Raoult's law at all temperatures (and concentrations). The A–A and B–B interactions are of the same strength as the A–B interaction.

PropertyValue
\(\Delta H_{mix}\)0
\(\Delta V_{mix}\)0
\(\Delta S_{mix}\)positive (as for the process to proceed)
\(\Delta G_{mix}\)negative

Examples: (1) benzene + toluene; (2) hexane + heptane; (3) \(\mathrm{C_2H_5Br+C_2H_5I}\).

Non-ideal solutions: positive deviation

Non-ideal solutions do not obey Raoult's law.

  • (i) \(P_{T,\,\exp}\gt(X_AP_A^\circ+X_BP_B^\circ)\).
  • (ii) Force of attraction A–A, B–B \(\gt\) A–B (the A–B attraction is weaker).
  • (iii) \(\Delta H_{mix}=\)positive (energy absorbed).
  • (iv) \(\Delta V_{mix}=\)positive (1 L + 1 L \(\gt\) 2 L).
  • (v) \(\Delta S_{mix}=\)positive.
  • (vi) \(\Delta G_{mix}=\)negative.

Examples: \(\mathrm{H_2O+CH_3OH}\); \(\mathrm{H_2O+C_2H_5OH}\); \(\mathrm{C_2H_5OH}\) + hexane; \(\mathrm{C_2H_5OH}\) + cyclohexane; \(\mathrm{CHCl_3+CCl_4}\) (dipole–dipole interaction becomes weak).

On the \(P\)–\(X\) graph (\(P_A^\circ\gt P_B^\circ\)) the \(P_T\) curve lies above the straight line joining \(P_B^\circ\) and \(P_A^\circ\).

Non-ideal solutions: negative deviation

  • (i) \(P_{T,\,\exp}\lt X_AP_A^\circ+X_BP_B^\circ\).
  • (ii) Force of attraction A–A, B–B \(\lt\) A–B (the A–B attraction is stronger).
  • (iii) \(\Delta H_{mix}=\)negative.
  • (iv) \(\Delta V_{mix}=\)negative (1 L + 1 L \(\lt\) 2 L).
  • (v) \(\Delta S_{mix}=\)positive.
  • (vi) \(\Delta G_{mix}=\)negative.

Examples: \(\mathrm{H_2O+HCOOH}\); \(\mathrm{H_2O+CH_3COOH}\); \(\mathrm{H_2O+HNO_3}\); \(\mathrm{CHCl_3+CH_3COCH_3}\) (hydrogen bond \(\mathrm{C{=}O\cdots H{-}CCl_3}\)).

On the \(P\)–\(X\) graph (\(P_A^\circ\gt P_B^\circ\)) the \(P_T\) curve lies below the straight line joining \(P_B^\circ\) and \(P_A^\circ\).

Immiscible liquids

  1. \(P_{total}=P_A+P_B\)
  2. \(P_A=P_A^\circ X_A=P_A^\circ\) [since \(X_A=1\)]
  3. \(P_B=P_B^\circ X_B=P_B^\circ\) [since \(X_B=1\)]
  4. \(P_{total}=P_A^\circ+P_B^\circ\)
  5. \(\dfrac{P_A^\circ}{P_B^\circ}=\dfrac{n_A}{n_B}\)
  6. \(\dfrac{P_A^\circ}{P_B^\circ}=\dfrac{W_A\,M_B}{M_A\,W_B}\)
\[P_A^\circ=\frac{n_ART}{V};\qquad P_B^\circ=\frac{n_BRT}{V}\]

\(T_{soln}\lt T_A,\ T_B\): the boiling point of the solution (mixture) is less than the individual boiling points of both the liquids.

Henry's law

This law deals with the dissolution of a gas in a liquid: the mass of any gas dissolved in any solvent per unit volume is proportional to the pressure of the gas in equilibrium with the liquid.

\[m\propto p\quad\Rightarrow\quad m=kp\]\[m=\frac{\text{weight of gas}}{\text{volume of liquid}}\]

Azeotropes and reverse osmosis

  • Minimum-boiling azeotrope (positive deviation): ethanol–water, 95.6% ethanol by mass, b.p. 351.1 K. Maximum-boiling azeotrope (negative deviation): \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\)–water, 68% \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\), b.p. 393.5 K. An azeotrope boils at constant composition and cannot be separated by fractional distillation.
  • Reverse osmosis: applying an external pressure greater than \(\pi\) on the solution side forces the solvent through a semipermeable membrane towards pure solvent; used for desalination. Osmosis is spontaneous flow from lower to higher concentration; isotonic saline is 0.9% NaCl.
  • Molar mass from colligative properties: \(M=\dfrac{1000\,K_f\,w_2}{\Delta T_f\,w_1}\) (similarly for \(K_b\)); osmotic pressure is preferred for macromolecules since it is measurable at room temperature and for dilute solutions.
9
Physical · Solutions, solids & kinetics

Solid State

Classification of crystals into seven systems

Crystal systemUnit cell dimensions and anglesBravais latticesExample
Cubic\(a=b=c;\ \alpha=\beta=\gamma=90^\circ\)SC, BCC, FCCNaCl
Orthorhombic\(a\ne b\ne c;\ \alpha=\beta=\gamma=90^\circ\)SC, BCC, end-centred and FCCRhombic sulphur (\(\mathrm{S_R}\))
Tetragonal\(a=b\ne c;\ \alpha=\beta=\gamma=90^\circ\)SC, BCCSn, \(\mathrm{SnO_2}\)
Monoclinic\(a\ne b\ne c;\ \alpha=\gamma=90^\circ\ne\beta\)SC, end-centredMonoclinic sulphur (\(\mathrm{S_M}\))
Rhombohedral\(a=b=c;\ \alpha=\beta=\gamma\ne90^\circ\)SCQuartz
Triclinic\(a\ne b\ne c;\ \alpha\ne\beta\ne\gamma\ne90^\circ\)SC\(\mathrm{H_3BO_3}\)
Hexagonal\(a=b\ne c;\ \alpha=\beta=90^\circ,\ \gamma=120^\circ\)SCGraphite

Density of lattice matter

\[d=\frac{Z\,M}{N_A\,a^3}\]

\(N_A\) = Avogadro's number; \(M\) = atomic mass or molecular mass.

Examples of ionic crystals

StructureCoordination numberEdge length of unit cell
(a) Rock salt (NaCl)6 : 6—
(b) CsCl8 : 8\(a_{sc}=\dfrac{2}{\sqrt3}\left(r_{\mathrm{Cs^+}}+r_{\mathrm{Cl^-}}\right)\)
(c) Zinc blende (ZnS)4 : 4\(a_{fcc}=\dfrac{4}{\sqrt3}\left(r_{\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}}+r_{\mathrm{S^{2-}}}\right)\)
(d) Fluorite structure (\(\mathrm{CaF_2}\))8 : 4\(a_{fcc}=\dfrac{4}{\sqrt3}\left(r_{\mathrm{Ca^{2+}}}+r_{\mathrm{F^-}}\right)\)

Bragg's law (X-ray diffraction)

\[n\lambda=2d\sin\theta\]
  • \(n\) = order of diffraction (\(1,2,3,\dots\))
  • \(\lambda\) = wavelength of the X-rays
  • \(d\) = spacing between the parallel lattice planes
  • \(\theta\) = glancing (Bragg) angle, between the incident ray and the planes; the diffracted beam is deviated by \(2\theta\)

For a cubic crystal the spacing of the planes with Miller indices \((hkl)\) is

\[d_{hkl}=\frac{a}{\sqrt{h^2+k^2+l^2}}\]

Since \(\sin\theta\le1\), diffraction is possible only if \(\lambda\le\dfrac{2d}{n}\).

Applications: finding the interplanar spacing \(d\) and so the unit-cell edge \(a\) of a crystal (and hence its structure and density), or finding \(\lambda\) of X-rays from a crystal of known \(d\).

Added at your request; this card is not in the original booklet.

Crystal defects (imperfections): classification

Defects are irregularities in the ideal arrangement of the constituent particles of a crystal. Point defects are irregularities around a point (an atom or ion); line defects are irregularities along whole rows of lattice points.

  • Point defects
    • Stoichiometric defects (the ratio of cations to anions is unchanged)
      • Non-ionic solids: vacancy defect, interstitial defect
      • Ionic solids: Schottky defect, Frenkel defect
    • Non-stoichiometric defects
      • Metal excess defect
      • Metal deficiency defect
    • Impurity defects
  • Line defects
  • Vacancy defect: some lattice sites are vacant, so the density decreases.
  • Interstitial defect: some particles occupy interstitial sites, so the density increases.

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Schottky and Frenkel defects

FeatureSchottky defectFrenkel defect
NatureEqual numbers of cations and anions are missing from their lattice sites (vacancies)The smaller ion (usually the cation) leaves its lattice site and occupies an interstitial site (vacancy + interstitial; a dislocation defect)
Electrical neutrality and stoichiometryMaintainedMaintained
DensityDecreasesUnchanged
Favoured byHigh coordination number; cation and anion of similar sizeLow coordination number; large difference in size of cation and anion
ExamplesNaCl, KCl, CsCl, AgBrZnS, AgCl, AgBr, AgI

AgBr shows both Schottky and Frenkel defects. Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Non-stoichiometric defects

Metal excess defect

  • Anionic vacancies (F-centres): when NaCl is heated in sodium vapour, \(\mathrm{Na}\) atoms deposit on the surface, \(\mathrm{Cl^-}\) ions diffuse to the surface, and the electron released by \(\mathrm{Na\to Na^++e^-}\) diffuses into the crystal and occupies an anionic vacancy. These trapped electrons are F-centres (colour centres) and make the crystal coloured and paramagnetic: NaCl turns yellow, KCl violet (lilac), LiCl pink.
  • Extra cations in interstitial sites: on heating, ZnO loses oxygen and becomes yellow; the excess \(\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}\) ions sit in interstitial sites with electrons trapped nearby.
\[\mathrm{ZnO\xrightarrow{\Delta}Zn^{2+}+\tfrac12O_2\uparrow+2e^-}\]

Metal deficiency defect

  • A cation is missing from its lattice site, and the charge is balanced by an adjacent metal ion having a higher oxidation state (e.g. \(\mathrm{Fe^{2+}}\) replaced in part by \(\mathrm{Fe^{3+}}\)).
  • Example: FeO is actually \(\mathrm{Fe_{0.95}O}\) (composition \(\mathrm{Fe_{0.93}O}\) to \(\mathrm{Fe_{0.96}O}\)); similarly NiO and FeS.

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Impurity defects

Introduced by adding an aliovalent (different-valence) impurity ion to an ionic crystal.

  • NaCl doped with \(\mathrm{SrCl_2}\): each \(\mathrm{Sr^{2+}}\) ion takes the place of two \(\mathrm{Na^+}\) ions; one site is occupied by \(\mathrm{Sr^{2+}}\) and the other becomes a cationic vacancy.
  • The concentration of cationic vacancies equals the concentration of the \(\mathrm{Sr^{2+}}\) impurity.
  • Similarly \(\mathrm{CdCl_2}\) in AgCl.

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Semiconductors and magnetic properties

  • n-type: group 14 doped with group 15 (P, As): extra electron in conduction band. p-type: doped with group 13 (B, Al, Ga): positive holes. 12–16 compounds (ZnS, CdS, CdSe) and 13–15 compounds (InSb, GaAs) are also semiconductors; conductivity of intrinsic semiconductors rises with T, that of metals falls.
  • Conductivity order: metals (\(10^{4}\)–\(10^{7}\ \mathrm{ohm^{-1}m^{-1}}\)) \(\gt\) semiconductors (\(10^{-6}\)–\(10^{4}\)) \(\gt\) insulators (\(10^{-20}\)–\(10^{-10}\)).
  • Magnetism: diamagnetic (all electrons paired, weakly repelled: NaCl, \(\mathrm{H_2O}\)); paramagnetic (unpaired e⁻, weakly attracted: \(\mathrm{O_2,Cu^{2+}}\)); ferromagnetic (domains aligned parallel; Fe, Co, Ni, \(\mathrm{CrO_2}\); lose it above the Curie temperature); antiferromagnetic (equal opposite alignment, net zero: MnO); ferrimagnetic (unequal opposite alignment, net moment: \(\mathrm{Fe_3O_4}\), ferrites).
10
Physical · Solutions, solids & kinetics

Chemical Kinetics & Radioactivity

Rate (velocity) of a chemical reaction

\[\text{Rate}=\frac{\Delta c}{\Delta t}=\frac{\mathrm{mol/lit}}{\mathrm{sec}}=\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,time^{-1}}=\mathrm{mol\,dm^{-3}\,time^{-1}}\]

Types of rates of chemical reaction, for a reaction \(\mathrm{R\to P}\):

\[\text{Average rate}=\frac{\text{total change in concentration}}{\text{total time taken}}\]\[r_{\text{instantaneous}}=\lim_{\Delta t\to0}\frac{\Delta c}{\Delta t}=\frac{dc}{dt}=-\frac{d[\mathrm R]}{dt}=\frac{d[\mathrm P]}{dt}\]

Rate law: dependence of rate on concentration of reactants

\[\text{Rate}=K\,(\text{conc.})^{\text{order}}\qquad\text{(differential rate equation or rate expression)}\]

\(K\) = rate constant = specific reaction rate = rate of reaction when the concentration is unity.

\[\text{unit of }K=(\text{conc})^{1-\text{order}}\ \text{time}^{-1}\]

Order of reaction

\[m_1\mathrm{A}+m_2\mathrm{B}\to\text{products},\qquad R\propto[\mathrm{A}]^p[\mathrm{B}]^q\]

Here \(p\) may or may not be equal to \(m_1\), and similarly \(q\) may or may not be equal to \(m_2\).

  • \(p\) is the order of reaction with respect to reactant A.
  • \(q\) is the order of reaction with respect to reactant B.
  • \((p+q)\) is the overall order of the reaction.

Integrated rate laws: zero order reactions

\(C_0\) or \(a\) = initial concentration; \(C_t\) or \(a-x\) = concentration at time \(t\).

\[\text{Rate}=k\,[\text{conc.}]^0=\text{constant}\]\[\text{Rate}=k=\frac{C_0-C_t}{t}\quad\text{or}\quad C_t=C_0-kt\]

Unit of \(k=\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}\). Time for completion \(=\dfrac{C_0}{k}\).

At \(t_{1/2}\), \(C_t=\dfrac{C_0}{2}\), so

\[kt_{1/2}=\frac{C_0}{2}\ \Rightarrow\ t_{1/2}=\frac{C_0}{2k}\ \Rightarrow\ t_{1/2}\propto C_0\]

Integrated rate laws: first order reactions

Let a 1st order reaction be \(\mathrm{A\to products}\).

\[t=\frac{2.303}{k}\log\frac{a}{a-x}\quad\text{or}\quad k=\frac{2.303}{t}\log\frac{C_0}{C_t}\]\[t_{1/2}=\frac{\ln2}{k}=\frac{0.693}{k}\quad\text{(independent of initial concentration)}\]\[t_{avg}=\frac1k=1.44\,t_{1/2}\]

First order reactions: graphical representation

\[t=\frac{2.303}{k}\log C_0-\frac{2.303}{k}\log C_t\]
PlotShapeSlope
\(\log\dfrac{C_0}{C_t}\) (or \(\log\dfrac{a}{a-x}\)) against \(t\)Straight line through the origin\(\tan\theta=\dfrac{k}{2.303}\)
\(\log C_t\) against \(t\)Straight line with intercept \(\log C_0\)\(\tan\theta=-\dfrac{k}{2.303}\)

Integrated rate laws: second order reactions

Two types:

\(\mathrm{A+A\to products}\)\(\mathrm{A+B\to products}\)
At \(t=0\)\(a\)\(a,\ b\)
At time \(t\)\((a-x)\)\((a-x),\ (b-x)\)
Rate\(\dfrac{dx}{dt}=k\,(a-x)^2\)\(\dfrac{dx}{dt}=k\,(a-x)(b-x)\)
Integrated form\(\dfrac1{a-x}-\dfrac1a=kt\)\(k=\dfrac{2.303}{t\,(a-b)}\log\dfrac{b\,(a-x)}{a\,(b-x)}\)

Methods to determine the order of a reaction

  1. Initial rate method: for \(r=k[\mathrm A]^a[\mathrm B]^b[\mathrm C]^c\), if \([\mathrm B]\) and \([\mathrm C]\) are constant, then for two different initial concentrations of A:\[r_{01}=k\,[\mathrm A_0]_1^{\,a},\qquad r_{02}=k\,[\mathrm A_0]_2^{\,a}\]\[\frac{r_{01}}{r_{02}}=\left(\frac{[\mathrm A_0]_1}{[\mathrm A_0]_2}\right)^{a}\]
  2. Using the integrated rate law: a method of trial and error.
  3. Method of half-lives: for an \(n\)th order reaction,\[t_{1/2}\propto\frac1{[\mathrm R_0]^{\,n-1}}\]
  4. Ostwald isolation method:\[\text{rate}=k[\mathrm A]^a[\mathrm B]^b[\mathrm C]^c=k_0[\mathrm A]^a\]

Methods to monitor the progress of the reaction

(a) Gaseous reactions can be monitored by measuring the total pressure at fixed volume and temperature, or by measuring the total volume of the mixture at constant pressure and temperature. For \(\mathrm{A(g)\to nB(g)}\) (first order), with \(P_0\) the initial pressure of A and \(P_t\) the total pressure at time \(t\):

\[k=\frac{2.303}{t}\log\frac{(n-1)\,P_0}{nP_0-P_t}\]

This formula is not applicable when \(n=1\); the value of \(n\) can also be fractional.

(b) Titration method

  1. \(a\propto V_0\), \(a-x\propto V_t\):\[k=\frac{2.303}{t}\log\frac{V_0}{V_t}\]
  2. Study of acid hydrolysis of an ester:\[k=\frac{2.303}{t}\log\frac{V_\infty-V_0}{V_\infty-V_t}\]

(c) By measuring the optical rotation produced by the reaction mixture:

\[k=\frac{2.303}{t}\log\frac{\theta_0-\theta_\infty}{\theta_t-\theta_\infty}\]

Effect of temperature: temperature coefficient

\[\text{T.C.}=\frac{K_{t+10}}{K_t}\approx2\ \text{to}\ 3\]

(for most of the reactions).

Arrhenius theory: energy profile of a reaction

Enthalpy (\(H\)) is plotted against the progress of the reaction (reaction coordinate); the reactants and products lie on either side of the threshold (activated complex) energy.

SymbolMeaning
\(H_R\) (or \(E_R\))Summation of enthalpies of reactants
\(H_P\) (or \(E_P\))Summation of enthalpies of products
\(\Delta H\)Enthalpy change during the reaction
\(E_{a1}\) (\(E_{af}\))Energy of activation of the forward reaction
\(E_{a2}\) (\(E_{ab}\))Energy of activation of the backward reaction
\[\Delta H=H_P-H_R=E_P-E_R=E_{af}-E_{ab}\]\[E_{\text{threshold}}=E_{af}+E_R=E_{ab}+E_P\]
  • \(E_P\gt E_R\): endothermic.
  • \(E_P\lt E_R\): exothermic.

Arrhenius equation

\[k=Ae^{-E_a/RT},\qquad r=k\,[\text{conc.}]^{\text{order}}\]\[\frac{d\ln k}{dT}=\frac{E_a}{RT^2}\]\[\log k=\log A-\frac{E_a}{2.303\,R}\cdot\frac1T\]

If \(k_1\) and \(k_2\) are the rate constants of a reaction at two different temperatures \(T_1\) and \(T_2\) respectively, then

\[\log\frac{k_2}{k_1}=\frac{E_a}{2.303\,R}\left(\frac1{T_1}-\frac1{T_2}\right)\]\[\ln k=\ln A-\frac{E_a}{RT}\]

A plot of \(\ln k\) against \(1/T\) is a straight line with slope \(=-\dfrac{E_a}{R}\) and intercept \(\ln A\).

As \(E_a\to0\) or \(T\to\infty\), \(k\to A\).

Collision theory, complex reactions and catalysis

\[k=PZ_{AB}\,e^{-E_a/RT}\]

\(Z\) = collision frequency, \(P\) = steric (orientation) factor, \(e^{-E_a/RT}\) = fraction of collisions with \(E\ge E_a\). Rate rises roughly 2–3 times per 10 K rise (temperature coefficient).

  • Molecularity is a whole number (1, 2, 3), applies only to elementary steps; order may be fractional or zero. The rate-determining step (slowest) fixes the rate law; intermediates are eliminated using steady-state or fast-equilibrium approximations.
  • Parallel \(\mathrm{A\xrightarrow{k_1}B,\ A\xrightarrow{k_2}C}\): \(k_{\text{eff}}=k_1+k_2\), \(\dfrac{[B]}{[C]}=\dfrac{k_1}{k_2}\); \(E_{\text{eff}}=\dfrac{k_1E_1+k_2E_2}{k_1+k_2}\). Consecutive \(\mathrm{A\xrightarrow{k_1}B\xrightarrow{k_2}C}\): \([B]\) rises to a maximum at \(t_{\max}=\dfrac{\ln(k_1/k_2)}{k_1-k_2}\). Reversible (opposing) first-order: \(k_f+k_b\) governs approach to equilibrium, \(K=k_f/k_b\).
  • Catalyst lowers \(E_a\) of both forward and backward steps equally, unchanged \(\Delta H\) and \(K\). Photochemical reactions: quantum yield \(\Phi=\dfrac{\text{molecules reacted}}{\text{photons absorbed}}\). Pseudo-first-order: ester hydrolysis, inversion of cane sugar.
11
Inorganic · Periodicity & bonding

Periodic Table & Periodicity

Döbereiner and Newlands

  • Döbereiner's triads: similar elements were arranged in groups of three, called triads.
  • Newlands' law of octaves: he was the first to correlate the chemical properties of the elements with their atomic masses.

Lothar Meyer's classification

He plotted a graph of atomic masses against the respective atomic volumes for a number of elements and observed that:

  1. elements with similar properties occupied similar positions on the curve;
  2. alkali metals, having larger atomic volumes, occupied the crests;
  3. transition elements occupied the troughs;
  4. the halogens occupied the ascending portions of the curve before the inert gases;
  5. alkaline earth metals occupied positions at about the mid-points of the descending portions of the curve.

He concluded that the atomic volumes (a physical property) of the elements are periodic functions of their atomic masses.

Mendeleev's periodic law

The physical and chemical properties of the elements are periodic functions of their atomic masses.

Merits

  • It simplified and systematised the study of elements and their compounds.
  • It helped in predicting the discovery of new elements on the basis of the blank spaces left in the table.

Demerits of Mendeleev's table

  • Position of hydrogen is uncertain; it has been placed in groups IA and VIIA.
  • No separate positions were given to isotopes.
  • Anomalous positions of lanthanides and actinides.
  • The order of increasing atomic weights is not strictly followed in the arrangement of elements.
  • Similar elements were placed in different groups.
  • It did not explain the cause of periodicity.

Lengths of the periods

PeriodPrincipal quantum numberNumber of elementsCalled as
1st\(n=1\)2Very short period
2nd\(n=2\)8Short period
3rd\(n=3\)8Short period
4th\(n=4\)18Long period
5th\(n=5\)18Long period
6th\(n=6\)32Very long period
7th\(n=7\)19Incomplete period

The count of 19 for the 7th period is the older value; with elements up to \(Z=118\) the 7th period is now complete (32 elements).

Modern periodic law (Moseley)

If the elements are arranged in order of their increasing atomic number, after a regular interval elements with similar properties are repeated.

Periodicity: the repetition of the properties of elements after regular intervals, when the elements are arranged in order of increasing atomic number.

Cause of periodicity: the periodic repetition of properties is due to the recurrence of similar valence-shell electronic configurations after certain regular intervals.

The long form of the table (Moseley's periodic table) consists of horizontal rows (periods) and vertical columns (groups).

Periods

There are seven periods, numbered 1 to 7.

  • Each period consists of a series of elements having the same valence shell.
  • Each period corresponds to a particular principal quantum number of the valence shell present in it.
  • Each period starts with an alkali metal having outermost configuration \(ns^1\).
  • Each period ends with a noble gas with outermost configuration \(ns^2np^6\), except helium (\(1s^2\)).
  • Each period starts with the filling of a new energy level.
  • The number of elements in each period is twice the number of atomic orbitals available in the energy level being filled.

Groups

There are eighteen groups, numbered 1 to 18.

A group consists of a series of elements having similar valence-shell electronic configurations.

Long-form periodic table at a glance

GroupFamilyBlockValence configurationElements
1Alkali metalss\(ns^1\)Li, Na, K, Rb, Cs, Fr (H placed above)
2Alkaline earth metalss\(ns^2\)Be, Mg, Ca, Sr, Ba, Ra
3 to 12Transition elementsd\((n-1)d^{1-10}ns^{0-2}\)3d: Sc to Zn; 4d: Y to Cd; 5d: La, Hf to Hg; 6d: Ac, Rf to Cn
13Boron familyp\(ns^2np^1\)B, Al, Ga, In, Tl, Nh
14Carbon familyp\(ns^2np^2\)C, Si, Ge, Sn, Pb, Fl
15Nitrogen family (pnictogens)p\(ns^2np^3\)N, P, As, Sb, Bi, Mc
16Oxygen family (chalcogens)p\(ns^2np^4\)O, S, Se, Te, Po, Lv
17Halogensp\(ns^2np^5\)F, Cl, Br, I, At, Ts
18Noble gasesp\(ns^2np^6\) (He: \(1s^2\))He, Ne, Ar, Kr, Xe, Rn, Og
Group 3 (placed separately below the table)Lanthanoids and actinoidsf\((n-2)f^{1-14}(n-1)d^{0-1}ns^2\)4f series: Ce to Lu; 5f series: Th to Lr
PeriodOrbitals filledElementsAtomic numbers
1\(1s\)21 to 2

Classification of the elements into blocks

BlockGroupsGeneral electronic configuration
s-block1 and 2 (extreme left of the table)\([\text{inert gas}]\,ns^{1-2}\)
p-block13 to 18\([\text{inert gas}]\,ns^2np^{1-6}\)
d-block3 to 12\([\text{inert gas}]\,(n-1)d^{1-10}ns^{1-2}\)
f-blockall belong to the 3rd group\((n-2)f^{1-14}(n-1)d^{0-1}ns^2\)

The f-block elements are classified into two series:

  • 1st inner transition or 4f series: 14 elements, \({}_{58}\mathrm{Ce}\) to \({}_{71}\mathrm{Lu}\).
  • 2nd inner transition or 5f series: 14 elements, \({}_{90}\mathrm{Th}\) to \({}_{103}\mathrm{Lr}\).

Predicting period, group and block

  • Period = principal quantum number of the valence shell.
  • Block = type of subshell that receives the last electron.
  • Group is predicted from the number of electrons in the valence shell and/or penultimate shell:
\[\begin{aligned}\text{s-block: }&\ \text{Group no.}=\text{no. of valence electrons}\\ \text{p-block: }&\ \text{Group no.}=10+\text{no. of valence electrons}\\ \text{d-block: }&\ \text{Group no.}=\text{no. of electrons in }(n-1)d+\text{no. of electrons in valence shell}\end{aligned}\]

Metals, non-metals and metalloids

Metals readily give up electron(s) and have a shining lustre. They comprise more than 78% of all known elements and lie on the left-hand side of the table. They are usually solids at room temperature (except mercury, gallium), have high melting and boiling points and are good conductors of heat and electricity. Oxides of metals are generally basic (some metals in a higher oxidation state form acidic oxides, e.g. \(\mathrm{CrO_3}\)).

Non-metals do not lose electrons but take up electrons to form anions. They are located at the top right-hand side of the table, are solids, liquids or gases at room temperature with low melting and boiling points, and are poor conductors of heat and electricity. Oxides of non-metals are generally acidic.

Metalloids (semimetals): B, Si, Ge, As, Sb and Te.

Diagonal relationship

2nd periodLiBeBC
3rd periodNaMgAlSi

The diagonal pairs are Li–Mg, Be–Al and B–Si. The relationship arises because:

  1. On descending a group, atoms and ions increase in size; on moving left to right, size decreases. On moving diagonally the size remains nearly the same: \(\mathrm{Li}=1.23\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}\) and \(\mathrm{Mg}=1.36\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}\); \(\mathrm{Li^+}=0.76\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}\) and \(\mathrm{Mg^{2+}}=0.72\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}\).
  2. It is sometimes suggested that it arises because of diagonal similarity in electronegativity: \(\mathrm{Li}=1.0\) and \(\mathrm{Mg}=1.2\); \(\mathrm{Be}=1.5\) and \(\mathrm{Al}=1.5\); \(\mathrm{B}=2.0\) and \(\mathrm{Si}=1.8\).

Effective nuclear charge

\[Z_{\mathrm{eff}}=Z-\sigma\]

\(Z\) is the actual nuclear charge (atomic number) and \(\sigma\) is the shielding (screening) constant. The shielding effect can be determined using Slater's rules.

Atomic radius

  • Covalent radius: one-half of the distance between the centres of two nuclei (of like atoms) bonded by a single covalent bond. Generally used for non-metals.
  • Van der Waals radius (collision radius): one-half of the internuclear distance between two adjacent atoms in two nearest neighbouring molecules of the substance in the solid state.
  • Metallic radius (crystal radius): one-half of the distance between the nuclei of two adjacent metal atoms in the metallic crystal lattice.
\[r_{\text{covalent}}\lt r_{\text{crystal}}\lt r_{\text{van der Waals}}\]

Variation of atomic radius

In a period (left to right)In a group (top to bottom)
Nuclear charge \(Z\)increases by one unitincreases by more than one unit
Effective nuclear charge \(Z_{\mathrm{eff}}\)also increasesalmost remains constant because of the increased screening effect of inner-shell electrons
Number of shells \(n\)remains constantincreases
Resultelectrons are pulled closer to the nucleus by the increased \(Z_{\mathrm{eff}}\), \(r_n\propto\dfrac{1}{Z^*}\)the effect of the increased number of shells outweighs the effect of the increased nuclear charge
Atomic radiusdecreases with increase in atomic numberincreases

Ionic radius

The effective distance from the centre of the nucleus of the ion up to which it has an influence in the ionic bond is called the ionic radius.

CationAnion
Formed by the loss of one or more electrons from the valence shell of an atom.Formed by the gain of one or more electrons in the valence shell of an atom.
Cations are smaller than the parent atomsAnions are larger than the parent atoms
(i) The whole of the outer shell of electrons is usually removed.(i) Electrons increase on gaining electrons, but the magnitude of nuclear charge remains the same.
(ii) The number of positive charges on the nucleus is greater than the number of orbital electrons, so the inward pull on the remaining electrons increases, causing contraction in size.(ii) Nuclear charge per electron is reduced, so the electron cloud is held less tightly by the nucleus, leading to expansion of the outer shell and increase in size.

Ionisation energy

Ionisation energy (IE) is the amount of energy required to remove the most loosely bound electron from an isolated gaseous atom to form a cation.

\[\begin{aligned}\mathrm{M(g)}+\mathrm{IE_1}&\to\mathrm{M^+(g)}+e^-\\ \mathrm{M^+(g)}+\mathrm{IE_2}&\to\mathrm{M^{2+}(g)}+e^-\\ \mathrm{M^{2+}(g)}+\mathrm{IE_3}&\to\mathrm{M^{3+}(g)}+e^-\end{aligned}\]

\(\mathrm{IE_1}\), \(\mathrm{IE_2}\) and \(\mathrm{IE_3}\) are the 1st, 2nd and 3rd ionisation energies, to remove an electron from the neutral atom, the monovalent cation and the divalent cation respectively. In general:

\[(\mathrm{IE})_1\lt(\mathrm{IE})_2\lt(\mathrm{IE})_3\lt\cdots\]

Factors influencing ionisation energy

  • Size of the atom: ionisation energy decreases with increase in atomic size.
  • Nuclear charge: ionisation energy increases with increase in nuclear charge.
  • Shielding (screening) effect: the larger the number of electrons in the inner shells, the greater the screening effect, the smaller the force of attraction, and hence the lower the IE.
  • Penetration effect: penetration of electrons follows the order \(s\gt p\gt d\gt f\) for the same energy level. The higher the penetration of the electron, the higher the ionisation energy.
  • Electronic configuration: an atom with exactly half-filled or completely filled orbitals has extra stability.

Electron gain enthalpy

The electron gain enthalpy \(\Delta_{eg}H\) is the change in standard molar enthalpy when a neutral gaseous atom gains an electron to form an anion.

\[\mathrm{X(g)}+e^-\to\mathrm{X^-(g)}\]

The second electron gain enthalpy (addition of a second electron to the uninegative ion) is invariably positive, because electron repulsion outweighs nuclear attraction.

Group 17 elements (halogens) have very high negative electron gain enthalpies (high electron affinity) because they attain the stable noble gas configuration by picking up an electron.

Trends in electron gain enthalpy

  • Across a period, with increase in atomic number, electron gain enthalpy becomes more negative.
  • Down a group, electron gain enthalpy becomes less negative.
  • Noble gases have large positive electron gain enthalpies.
  • The negative electron gain enthalpy of O or F is less than that of S or Cl.
  • Electron gain enthalpies of alkaline earth metals are very small or positive.
  • Nitrogen has very low electron affinity.
\[\text{Electron affinity}\propto\frac{1}{\text{atomic size}}\qquad \text{Electron affinity}\propto Z_{\mathrm{eff}}\qquad \text{Electron affinity}\propto\frac{1}{\text{screening effect}}\]

Half-filled and completely filled subshells are comparatively more stable, so adding an extra electron to such a system is difficult and the electron affinity value decreases.

Electronegativity: Pauling scale

Electronegativity is a measure of the tendency of an element to attract shared electrons towards itself in a covalently bonded molecule.

\[X_A-X_B=0.208\sqrt{E_{A-B}-\sqrt{E_{A-A}\times E_{B-B}}}\quad(\text{bond energies in kcal}\,\mathrm{mol^{-1}})\]\[X_A-X_B=0.1017\sqrt{E_{A-B}-\sqrt{E_{A-A}\times E_{B-B}}}\quad(\text{bond energies in kJ}\,\mathrm{mol^{-1}})\]

\(E_{A-B}\) = bond enthalpy (bond energy) of the A–B bond; \(E_{A-A}\) = bond energy of the A–A bond; \(E_{B-B}\) = bond energy of the B–B bond.

Electronegativity: Mulliken scale

\[\chi_M=\frac{\mathrm{IE}+\mathrm{EA}}{2}\]

Pauling's electronegativity \(\chi_P\) is related to Mulliken's \(\chi_M\) by

\[\chi_P=1.35\,(\chi_M)^{1/2}-1.37\]

Mulliken's values are about 2.8 times larger than Pauling's values.

Valence or oxidation states

Many elements exhibit variable valence. This is particularly characteristic of transition elements and actinoids.

Periodic trends and chemical reactivity

  • In a group, the basic nature of oxides increases (acidic nature decreases).
  • Oxides of metals are generally basic and oxides of non-metals are acidic. Oxides of metalloids are generally amphoteric.
  • The oxides of Be, Al, Zn, Sn, As, Pb and Sb are amphoteric.
  • In a period, the nature of the oxides varies from basic to acidic.
Oxide\(\mathrm{Na_2O}\)\(\mathrm{MgO}\)\(\mathrm{Al_2O_3}\)\(\mathrm{SiO_2}\)\(\mathrm{P_4O_{10}}\)\(\mathrm{SO_3}\)\(\mathrm{Cl_2O_7}\)
NatureStrongly basicBasicAmphotericWeakly acidicAcidicAcidicStrongly acidic
