12
Inorganic · Periodicity & bonding

Chemical Bonding

Chemical bond

In the process of bond formation each atom attains a stable outer electronic configuration of the inert gases.

Ionic (electrovalent) bond

The formation of an ionic compound depends primarily upon:

  • the ease of formation of the positive and negative ions from the respective neutral atoms;
  • the arrangement of the positive and negative ions in the solid, that is, the lattice of the crystalline compound.

Conditions for the formation of ionic compounds

  1. The electronegativity difference between the two combining elements must be large.
  2. The ionisation enthalpy (\(\mathrm{M(g)\to M^+(g)}+e^-\)) of the electropositive element must be low.
  3. The negative value of the electron gain enthalpy (\(\mathrm{X(g)}+e^-\to\mathrm{X^-(g)}\)) of the electronegative element should be high.
  4. The lattice enthalpy (\(\mathrm{M^+(g)+X^-(g)\to MX(s)}\)) of the ionic solid must be high.

Lattice enthalpy

The lattice enthalpy of an ionic solid is the energy required to completely separate one mole of a solid ionic compound into its gaseous constituent ions.

Factors affecting lattice energy

\[U\propto\frac{1}{r_++r_-}\qquad(r_++r_-=\text{inter-ionic distance})\]\[U\propto Z_+\,Z_-\]

\(Z_+\) = charge on the cation and \(Z_-\) = charge on the anion, in terms of electronic charge.

Born-Haber cycle

It interrelates the various energy terms involved during the formation of an ionic compound. It is a thermochemical cycle based on Hess's law of constant heat summation.

Hydration

All the simple salts dissolve in water producing ions, and consequently the solution conducts electricity. Since \(\mathrm{Li^+}\) is very small, it is heavily hydrated. This makes the radius of the hydrated \(\mathrm{Li^+}\) ion large, and hence it moves only slowly. In contrast, \(\mathrm{Cs^+}\) is the least hydrated because of its bigger size, so the radius of the hydrated \(\mathrm{Cs^+}\) is smaller than that of hydrated \(\mathrm{Li^+}\); hydrated \(\mathrm{Cs^+}\) moves faster and conducts electricity more readily.

Hydrolysis

Hydrolysis means reaction with water molecules, ultimately leading to breaking of the O–H bond of water into \(\mathrm{H^+}\) and \(\mathrm{OH^-}\) ions. In covalent compounds it takes place generally by two mechanisms:

  • By coordinate bond formation: generally in halides of atoms having vacant d-orbitals, or halides of atoms having vacant orbitals.
  • By H-bond formation: for example in nitrogen trihalides.

General properties of ionic compounds

  • Physical state: at room temperature ionic compounds exist either in the solid state or in solution, but not in the gaseous state.
  • Simple ionic compounds do not show isomerism, but isomorphism is their important characteristic, e.g. \(\mathrm{FeSO_4\cdot7H_2O}\) and \(\mathrm{MgSO_4\cdot7H_2O}\).
  • Electrical conductivity: all ionic solids are good conductors in the molten state as well as in aqueous solution, because their ions are free to move.
  • Solubility: soluble in polar solvents like water, which have a high dielectric constant.

Covalent character in ionic compounds (Fajans' rules)

The greater the polarisation of the anion in a molecule, the more covalent character it has. More distortion of the anion means more polarisation, and so covalent character increases.

  1. Size of cation: \(\text{size of cation}\propto\dfrac{1}{\text{polarisation}}\).
  2. Size of anion: \(\text{size of anion}\propto\text{polarisation}\).
  3. Charge on cation: \(\text{charge on cation}\propto\text{polarisation}\).
  4. Charge on anion: \(\text{charge on anion}\propto\text{polarisation}\).
  5. Pseudo inert gas configuration of the cation (18-electron outer shell) polarises the anion more than an inert gas configuration of comparable size and charge, e.g. \(\mathrm{CuCl}\) is more covalent than \(\mathrm{NaCl}\).

Covalent bond

It is formed by sharing of valence electrons between atoms to form molecules, e.g. formation of the \(\mathrm{Cl_2}\) molecule:

\[\mathrm{Cl}\cdot+\cdot\mathrm{Cl}\to\mathrm{Cl{-}Cl}\]

Each chlorine atom attains 8 electrons in its outer shell (covalent bond between the two Cl atoms). The important conditions are:

  1. Each bond is formed as a result of sharing of an electron pair between the atoms.
  2. Each combining atom contributes at least one electron to the shared pair.
  3. The combining atoms attain outer-shell noble gas configurations as a result of the sharing of electrons.

Coordinate (dative) bond

The bond formed between two atoms in which the contribution of the electron pair is made by one of them (donor) while the sharing is done by both (the other is the acceptor).

  • \(\mathrm{NH_4^+}\) (ammonium ion): \(\mathrm{NH_3+H^+\to NH_4^+}\); N is the donor and \(\mathrm{H^+}\) is the acceptor.
  • \(\mathrm{O_3}\) (ozone): \(\mathrm{O{=}O\to O}\).

Other examples: \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\), \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\), \(\mathrm{H_3O^+}\), \(\mathrm{N_2O}\), \(\mathrm{[Cu(NH_3)_4]^{2+}}\).

Formal charge

\[\text{FC}=V-L-\tfrac12B\]

\(V\) = valence electrons of the free atom, \(L\) = non-bonding (lone-pair) electrons, \(B\) = bonding electrons.

Formal charges help in the selection of the lowest-energy structure from a number of possible Lewis structures for a given species.

Limitations of the octet rule

  1. Incomplete octet of the central atom: \(\mathrm{LiCl}\), \(\mathrm{BeH_2}\), \(\mathrm{BCl_3}\), \(\mathrm{AlCl_3}\) and \(\mathrm{BF_3}\).
  2. Odd-electron molecules: nitric oxide \(\mathrm{NO}\) and nitrogen dioxide \(\mathrm{NO_2}\).
  3. Expanded octet: \(\mathrm{PF_5}\) (10 electrons around the P atom), \(\mathrm{SF_6}\) (12 electrons around the S atom), \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) (12 electrons around the S atom).
  4. Other drawbacks of the octet theory:
    • some noble gases (for example xenon and krypton) also combine with oxygen and fluorine to form compounds like \(\mathrm{XeF_2}\), \(\mathrm{KrF_2}\), \(\mathrm{XeOF_2}\) etc.;
    • it does not account for the shape of molecules;
    • it does not explain the relative stability of molecules, being totally silent about the energy of a molecule.

Valence bond theory: orbital overlap

\[\mathrm{H_2(g)}+435.8\ \mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\to\mathrm{H(g)}+\mathrm{H(g)}\]

According to the orbital overlap concept, the formation of a covalent bond between two atoms results from pairing of electrons present in the valence shell having opposite spins.

Depending on the type of overlapping, covalent bonds are of two types: sigma (\(\sigma\)) bond and pi (\(\pi\)) bond.

Sigma and pi bonds

  • Sigma (\(\sigma\)) bond: formed by the end-to-end (head-on) overlap of bonding orbitals along the internuclear axis. Types: \(s\)–\(s\) overlap, \(s\)–\(p\) overlap, and \(p\)–\(p\) overlap (between half-filled \(p\)-orbitals of the two approaching atoms).
  • Pi (\(\pi\)) bond: the atomic orbitals overlap in such a way that their axes remain parallel to each other and perpendicular to the internuclear axis.
  • Strength: in a sigma bond the overlapping of orbitals takes place to a larger extent, so it is stronger than the pi bond, where the extent of overlapping is smaller.

VSEPR theory

The main postulates are:

  1. The shape of a molecule depends upon the number of valence-shell electron pairs (bonded or non-bonded) around the central atom.
  2. Pairs of electrons in the valence shell repel one another since their electron clouds are negatively charged.
  3. These pairs tend to occupy positions in space that minimise repulsion and thus maximise the distance between them.
  4. The valence shell is taken as a sphere with the electron pairs localised on the spherical surface at maximum distance from one another.
  5. A multiple bond is treated as if it is a single electron pair, and the two or three electron pairs of a multiple bond are treated as a single super pair.
  6. Where two or more resonance structures can represent a molecule, the VSEPR model is applicable to any such structure.

The repulsive interaction of electron pairs decreases in the order:

\[\text{lp–lp}\gt\text{lp–bp}\gt\text{bp–bp}\]

lp = lone pair, bp = bond pair.

Hybridisation: salient features

  1. The number of hybrid orbitals is equal to the number of atomic orbitals that get hybridised.
  2. The hybridised orbitals are always equivalent in energy and shape.
  3. Hybrid orbitals are more effective in forming stable bonds than pure atomic orbitals.
  4. These hybrid orbitals are directed in space in some preferred direction to have minimum repulsion between electron pairs, so a stable arrangement is obtained. Therefore, the type of hybridisation indicates the geometry of the molecule.

Conditions for hybridisation

  1. The orbitals present in the valence shell of the atom are hybridised.
  2. The orbitals undergoing hybridisation should have almost equal energy.
  3. Promotion of an electron is not an essential condition prior to hybridisation.
  4. It is the orbitals that undergo hybridisation and not the electrons.

Steric number and hybridisation (Gillespie)

\[\text{Steric number}=\text{no. of atoms bonded to the atom}+\text{no. of lone pair(s) left on that atom}\]
Steric numberHybridisationGeometry
2\(sp\)Linear
3\(sp^2\)Trigonal planar
4\(sp^3\)Tetrahedral
5\(sp^3d\)Trigonal bipyramidal
6\(sp^3d^2\)Octahedral
7\(sp^3d^3\)Pentagonal bipyramidal

Hybridisation involving d-orbitals

HybridisationType of d-orbital involved
\(sp^3d\)\(d_{z^2}\)
\(sp^3d^2\)\(d_{x^2-y^2}\) and \(d_{z^2}\)
\(sp^3d^3\)\(d_{x^2-y^2}\), \(d_{z^2}\) and \(d_{xy}\)
\(dsp^2\)\(d_{x^2-y^2}\)

Molecular orbital theory (MOT)

Developed by F. Hund and R. S. Mulliken in 1932.

  1. Molecular orbitals are formed by the combination of atomic orbitals of comparable energies and proper symmetry.
  2. An electron in an atomic orbital is influenced by one nucleus, while in a molecular orbital it is influenced by two or more nuclei depending upon the number of atoms in the molecule. Thus an atomic orbital is monocentric while a molecular orbital is polycentric.
  3. The number of molecular orbitals formed is equal to the number of combining atomic orbitals. When two atomic orbitals combine, two molecular orbitals, called the bonding molecular orbital and the anti-bonding molecular orbital, are formed.
  4. Molecular orbitals, like atomic orbitals, are filled in accordance with the Aufbau principle, obeying the Pauli exclusion principle and Hund's rule of maximum multiplicity. But the filling order of these molecular orbitals is always experimentally decided; there is no rule like the \((n+l)\) rule for atomic orbitals.

Conditions for combination of atomic orbitals

  1. The combining atomic orbitals must have the same or nearly the same energy.
  2. The combining atomic orbitals must have the same symmetry about the molecular axis.
  3. The combining atomic orbitals must overlap to the maximum extent.

Energy level order of molecular orbitals

Increasing order of energies of the molecular orbitals for \(\mathrm{O_2}\) and \(\mathrm{F_2}\):

\[\sigma1s\lt\sigma^*1s\lt\sigma2s\lt\sigma^*2s\lt\sigma2p_z\lt(\pi2p_x=\pi2p_y)\lt(\pi^*2p_x=\pi^*2p_y)\lt\sigma^*2p_z\]

For the molecules up to \(\mathrm{N_2}\) (\(\mathrm{Li_2}\) to \(\mathrm{N_2}\)) the important characteristic feature is that the energy of the \(\sigma2p_z\) molecular orbital is higher than that of the \(\pi2p_x\) and \(\pi2p_y\) molecular orbitals:

\[\sigma1s\lt\sigma^*1s\lt\sigma2s\lt\sigma^*2s\lt(\pi2p_x=\pi2p_y)\lt\sigma2p_z\lt(\pi^*2p_x=\pi^*2p_y)\lt\sigma^*2p_z\]

Bond order

\[\text{b.o.}=\tfrac12\,(N_b-N_a)\]

\(N_b\) and \(N_a\) are the numbers of electrons in bonding and anti-bonding molecular orbitals.

  • A positive bond order (\(N_b\gt N_a\)) means a stable molecule; a negative (\(N_b\lt N_a\)) or zero (\(N_b=N_a\)) bond order means an unstable molecule.
  • Nature of the bond: integral bond orders of 1, 2 or 3 correspond to single, double or triple bonds respectively.
  • Bond length: the bond order may be taken as an approximate measure of bond length; bond length decreases as bond order increases.

Magnetic nature

If all the molecular orbitals in a molecule are doubly occupied, the substance is diamagnetic (repelled by a magnetic field), e.g. the \(\mathrm{N_2}\) molecule.

Dipole moment

\[\mu=q\times d\]

Dipole moment is the magnitude of charge (\(q\)) times the distance of separation (\(d\)). It is usually expressed in debye (D).

\[1\ \mathrm{D}=3.33564\times10^{-30}\ \mathrm{C\,m}\qquad 1\ \text{debye}=1\times10^{-18}\ \text{esu cm}\]

The shift in electron density, for example in HF, is represented by a crossed arrow above the Lewis structure indicating the direction of the shift. A molecule will have a dipole moment if the vector sum of all the individual bond moments is non-zero. For two dipole moments \(P\) and \(Q\) inclined at an angle \(\theta\), the resultant dipole moment is

\[\mu_R=\sqrt{P^2+Q^2+2PQ\cos\theta}\]

Resonance

Definition: resonance is the phenomenon in which two or more structures, involving identical positions of atoms, can be written for a particular compound.

For example, the ozone molecule can be equally represented by two structures, I and II:

\[\mathrm{O{=}O\to O}\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad\mathrm{O\leftarrow O{=}O}\]

Resonance hybrid: the actual structure of the molecule, which is the hybrid of all the different possible structures that can be written for it without violating the rules of maximum covalence for the atoms.

Hydrogen bond

\[\cdots\mathrm{H^{\delta+}{-}F^{\delta-}}\cdots\mathrm{H^{\delta+}{-}F^{\delta-}}\cdots\mathrm{H^{\delta+}{-}F^{\delta-}}\]

Conditions required for H-bonding

  1. The molecule should have a more electronegative atom (F, O, N) linked to the H atom.
  2. The size of the electronegative atom should be small.
  3. A lone pair should be present on the electronegative atom.

Order of H-bond strength

\[\mathrm{F{-}H\cdots{:}F}\gt\mathrm{O{-}H\cdots{:}O}\gt\mathrm{N{-}H\cdots{:}N}\gt\mathrm{N{-}H\cdots{:}O}\]

Intramolecular H-bonding

It is formed when a hydrogen atom is present between two highly electronegative (F, O, N) atoms within the same molecule. A compound with intramolecular H-bonding has a lower boiling point (is more volatile) than its para-derivative.

Necessary conditions

  1. The ring formed as a result of hydrogen bonding should be planar.
  2. A 5- or 6-membered ring should be formed.
  3. The interacting atoms should be placed in such a way that there is minimum strain during ring closure.

Intermolecular H-bonding

It is formed between two different molecules of the same or different compounds.

  • In water molecules.
  • The hydrogen bonds in HF link the F atom of one molecule with the H atom of another molecule, thus forming a zig-zag chain \((\mathrm{HF})_n\) in both the solid and the liquid.

Intermolecular forces (van der Waals forces)

Intermolecular attractions hold two or more molecules together. These are the weakest chemical forces and can be of the following types:

  • Ion–dipole attraction
  • Dipole–dipole attraction
  • Ion–induced dipole attraction
  • Dipole–induced dipole attraction
  • Instantaneous dipole–instantaneous induced dipole attraction (dispersion force or London forces)
\[\text{Strength of van der Waals force}\propto\text{molecular mass}\qquad\text{van der Waals force}\propto\text{boiling point}\]

Metallic bond

Two models are considered to explain metallic bonding:

  • Electron-sea model
  • Band model

Some special bonding situations

Electron-deficient bonding: there are many compounds in which some electron-deficient bonds are present apart from normal covalent or coordinate bonds. These bonds have fewer electrons than expected, such as the three-centre two-electron (3c–2e) bonds present in diborane \(\mathrm{B_2H_6}\), \(\mathrm{Al_2(CH_3)_6}\), \(\mathrm{BeH_2(s)}\) and bridging metal carbonyls.

Back bonding: it generally takes place when, out of two bonded atoms, one atom has vacant orbitals (generally this atom is from the second or third period) and the other bonded atom has some non-bonded electron pair (generally this atom is from the second period). Back bonding increases the bond strength and decreases the bond length. For example, the extent of back bonding in boron trihalides:

\[\mathrm{BF_3}\gt\mathrm{BCl_3}\gt\mathrm{BBr_3}\]

Bent’s rule and back bonding

  • Bent’s rule: more electronegative substituents prefer hybrid orbitals of lower s-character (so they have smaller bond angles); lone pairs and electropositive groups take more s-character. Example: in \(\mathrm{CH_3F}\) the C–F bond has more p-character and the H–C–H angle exceeds the tetrahedral value.
  • \(p\pi\)–\(p\pi\) back bonding: lone pair of the halogen donates into the empty p orbital of B: Lewis acidity \(\mathrm{BF_3\lt BCl_3\lt BBr_3\lt BI_3}\) (strongest overlap, hence weakest acid, for F). \(d\pi\)–\(p\pi\) back bonding: lone pair on N or O donates into empty 3d orbitals of Si, P, S, so \(\mathrm{(SiH_3)_3N}\) is planar and a weaker base than \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_3N}\); \(\mathrm{(SiH_3)_2O}\) has a wide Si–O–Si angle.
  • Bridge bonds: in \(\mathrm{B_2H_6}\) the two 3c–2e (banana) B–H–B bonds hold 4 electrons; 4 terminal 2c–2e bonds; the B–H–B angle is about \(83^\circ\).
13
Inorganic · Periodicity & bonding

Coordination Compounds

Addition compounds

Addition compounds are formed by the combination of two or more stable compounds in stoichiometric ratio. They are of two kinds: (1) double salts and (2) coordination compounds.

  • Double salts lose their identity in solution, e.g. \(\mathrm{K_2SO_4\cdot Al_2(SO_4)_3\cdot24H_2O}\).
  • Coordination compounds retain their identity in solution:
\[\mathrm{Fe(CN)_2+4KCN\to Fe(CN)_2\cdot4KCN}\ \ \text{or}\ \ \mathrm{K_4[Fe(CN)_6]}\]\[\mathrm{K_4[Fe(CN)_6](aq)\to4K^+(aq)+[Fe(CN)_6]^{4-}(aq)}\]

Basic terms

  • Central atom/ion: in a coordination entity, the atom/ion to which a fixed number of ligands are bound in a definite geometrical arrangement around it.
  • Ligands: the neutral molecules, anions or cations which are directly linked with the central metal atom or ion in the coordination entity.
  • Chelate ligand: a di- or polydentate ligand which uses its two or more donor atoms to bind a single metal ion, producing a ring.
  • Ambidentate ligand: a ligand which can ligate through two different atoms present in it.
  • Coordination number: the number of ligand donor atoms to which the metal is directly attached.
  • Oxidation number of the central atom: the charge it would carry if all the ligands are removed along with the electron pairs that are shared with the central atom. In \(\mathrm{[Fe(CN)_6]^{3-}}\) it is +3, written Fe(III).

Ambidentate ligands

Mode of bindingName
\(\mathrm{M\leftarrow NO_2}\)nitrito-N
\(\mathrm{M\leftarrow O{-}N{=}O}\)nitrito-O
\(\mathrm{M\leftarrow SCN}\)thiocyanato or thiocyanato-S
\(\mathrm{M\leftarrow NCS}\)isothiocyanato or thiocyanato-N

Homoleptic and heteroleptic complexes

  • Homoleptic: a metal is bound to only one type of donor group, e.g. \(\mathrm{[Cr(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}\).
  • Heteroleptic: a metal is bound to more than one type of donor group, e.g. \(\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_4Br_2]^+}\).

Writing the formulae of mononuclear coordination entities

  1. The central atom is placed first.
  2. The ligands are then placed in alphabetical order. The placement of a ligand in the list does not depend on its charge.
  3. Polydentate ligands are also placed alphabetically. For an abbreviated ligand, the first letter of the abbreviation decides its position in the alphabetical order.
  4. The formula for the entire coordination entity, whether charged or not, is enclosed in square brackets. When ligands are polyatomic, their formulae are enclosed in parentheses. Ligand abbreviations are also enclosed in parentheses.
  5. There should be no space between the ligands and the metal within a coordination sphere.
  6. When the formula of a charged coordination entity is written without that of the counter ion, the charge is indicated outside the square brackets as a right superscript with the number before the sign, e.g. \(\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}\), \(\mathrm{[Fe(CN)_6]^{3-}}\).
  7. The charge of the cation(s) is balanced by the charge of the anion(s).

Naming mononuclear coordination compounds

  1. Like simple salts, the cation is named first, in both positively and negatively charged coordination entities.
  2. The ligands are named in alphabetical order (according to the name of the ligand, not the prefix) before the name of the central atom/ion.
  3. Names of anionic ligands end in -o; those of neutral ligands are the same as the molecule, except aqua for \(\mathrm{H_2O}\), ammine for \(\mathrm{NH_3}\), carbonyl for CO, thiocarbonyl for CS and nitrosyl for NO. Names of cationic ligands end in -ium.
  4. Prefixes mono, di, tri, etc. indicate the number of ligands of one kind in the coordination entity. When the names of the ligands include a numerical prefix, are complicated, or the normal prefixes would create confusion, the name is set off in parentheses and the second set of prefixes (bis, tris, …) is used.
  5. The oxidation state of the metal in a cation, anion or neutral coordination entity is indicated by a Roman numeral in parentheses after the name of the metal.
  6. If the complex ion is a cation, the metal is named the same as the element (Co is cobalt, Pt is platinum). If the complex ion is an anion, the name of the metal ends with the suffix -ate; e.g. Co in the complex anion \(\mathrm{[Co(SCN)_4]^{2-}}\) is called cobaltate. For some metals the Latin names are used in complex anions.
  7. The neutral complex molecule is named similarly to the complex cation.

Numerical prefixes

NumberSimple prefixComplex-ligand prefix

Werner's theory

According to Werner, most elements exhibit two types of valencies:

  • (a) Primary valency: corresponds to the oxidation state of the metal ion; also called principal, ionisable or ionic valency. It is satisfied by negative ions, and its attachment to the central metal ion is shown by dotted lines.
  • (b) Secondary or auxiliary valency: the coordination number (CN) of the central metal ion. It is non-ionic or non-ionisable (coordinate covalent bond type). In modern terminology such spatial arrangements are called coordination polyhedra.
C.N.Geometry
2linear
3triangular
4tetrahedral or square planar
6octahedral

Effective atomic number (EAN) rule — Sidgwick

\[\mathrm{EAN}=Z-(\text{oxidation state of central metal})+(\text{no. of electrons donated by ligands})\]

where \(Z\) = atomic number of the central metal.

Valence bond theory: hybridisation and shape

The model utilises hybridisation of \((n-1)d,\ ns,\ np\) or \(ns,\ np,\ nd\) orbitals of the metal atom or ion to yield a set of equivalent orbitals of definite geometry, to account for the observed structures (octahedral, square planar and tetrahedral) and the magnetic properties of complexes. The number of unpaired electrons, measured by the magnetic moment of the compound, determines which d-orbitals are used.

Coordination number of metalType of hybridisationShape of complex
4\(sp^3\)Tetrahedral
4\(dsp^2\)Square planar
5\(sp^3d\)Trigonal bipyramidal
6\(sp^3d^2\)Octahedral
6\(d^2sp^3\)Octahedral

VBT: coordination number six

  • In the diamagnetic octahedral complex \(\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}\), cobalt is in the +3 oxidation state (\(3d^6\)); the complex uses \(d^2sp^3\) hybrid orbitals.
  • The complex \(\mathrm{[FeF_6]^{4-}}\) is paramagnetic and uses the outer orbitals (4d) in hybridisation (\(sp^3d^2\)); it is thus called an outer orbital, high spin or spin free complex.

VBT: coordination number four

ComplexMetal ionHybridisationGeometryMagnetic behaviour
\(\mathrm{[NiCl_4]^{2-}}\)\(\mathrm{Ni^{2+}}\), \(3d^8\)\(sp^3\)tetrahedralparamagnetic
\(\mathrm{[Ni(CO)_4]}\)Ni(0)\(sp^3\)tetrahedraldiamagnetic (no unpaired electrons)
\(\mathrm{[Ni(CN)_4]^{2-}}\)\(\mathrm{Ni^{2+}}\), \(3d^8\)\(dsp^2\)square planardiamagnetic

Shortcomings of valence bond theory

  1. A number of assumptions are involved.
  2. There is no quantitative interpretation of magnetic data.
  3. It has nothing to say about the spectral (colour) properties of coordination compounds.
  4. It does not give a quantitative interpretation of the thermodynamic or kinetic stabilities of coordination compounds.
  5. It does not make exact predictions regarding the tetrahedral and square-planar structures of 4-coordinate complexes.
  6. It does not distinguish between strong and weak ligands.

Magnetic moment

\[\mu=\sqrt{n(n+2)}\ \text{Bohr magneton}\]

\(n\) = number of unpaired electrons.

Magnetic properties and d-orbital occupancy

  • For metal ions with up to three electrons in the d-orbitals, like \(\mathrm{Ti^{3+}}\) (\(d^1\)), \(\mathrm{V^{3+}}\) (\(d^2\)), \(\mathrm{Cr^{3+}}\) (\(d^3\)), two vacant d-orbitals are easily available for octahedral hybridisation. The magnetic behaviour of these free ions and of their coordination entities is similar.
  • When more than three 3d electrons are present, as in \(\mathrm{Cr^{2+}}\) and \(\mathrm{Mn^{3+}}\) (\(d^4\)); \(\mathrm{Mn^{2+}}\) and \(\mathrm{Fe^{3+}}\) (\(d^5\)); \(\mathrm{Fe^{2+}}\) and \(\mathrm{Co^{3+}}\) (\(d^6\)), the required two vacant orbitals for hybridisation are not directly available (a consequence of Hund's rule).
  • For \(d^4\), \(d^5\) and \(d^6\), two vacant d-orbitals become available for hybridisation only as a result of pairing of the 3d electrons, which leaves two, one and zero unpaired electrons respectively.
ConfigurationUnpaired electrons after pairing
\(d^4\)2
\(d^5\)1
\(d^6\)0

Crystal field theory: octahedral splitting

The crystal field theory (CFT) is an electrostatic model which considers the metal–ligand bond to be ionic, arising purely from the electrostatic interaction between the metal ion and the ligand.

(a) Crystal field splitting in octahedral coordination entities. The five d-orbitals split into a lower \(t_{2g}\) set (three orbitals) and a higher \(e_g\) set (two orbitals), separated by \(\Delta_0\):

\[E(t_{2g})=-0.4\,\Delta_0,\qquad E(e_g)=+0.6\,\Delta_0\]

The crystal field splitting \(\Delta_0\) depends upon the field produced by the ligand and the charge on the metal ion.

Spectrochemical series

Ligands arranged in order of increasing field strength:

\[\mathrm{I^-\lt Br^-\lt SCN^-\lt Cl^-\lt S^{2-}\lt F^-\lt OH^-\lt C_2O_4^{2-}\lt H_2O\lt NCS^-\lt edta^{4-}\lt NH_3\lt en\lt NO_2^-\lt CN^-\lt CO}\]

It is an experimentally determined series, based on the absorption of light by complexes with different ligands.

Crystal field stabilisation energy (CFSE)

\[\mathrm{CFSE}=\left[-0.4\,n_{t_{2g}}+0.6\,n_{e_g}\right]\Delta_0+n'P\]

\(n_{t_{2g}}\) and \(n_{e_g}\) are the numbers of electron(s) in the \(t_{2g}\) and \(e_g\) orbitals, and \(\Delta_0\) is the crystal field splitting energy for the octahedral complex. \(n'\) represents the number of extra electron pairs formed because of the ligands, in comparison to the normal degenerate configuration; \(P\) is the pairing energy.

Crystal field splitting in tetrahedral complexes

In a tetrahedral coordination entity the d-orbital splitting is inverted (the lower set is \(e\), the upper set \(t_2\)) and is smaller than the octahedral field splitting. For the same metal, the same ligands and the same metal–ligand distances,

\[\Delta_t=\frac49\,\Delta_0\]

with \(E(e)=-0.6\,\Delta_t\) and \(E(t_2)=+0.4\,\Delta_t\).

Colour in coordination compounds

According to the crystal field theory, colour is due to the d–d transition of an electron under the influence of the ligands. The colour of a substance is due to the absorption of light of a specific wavelength in the visible part of the electromagnetic spectrum (400 to 700 nm) and the transmission or reflection of the rest of the wavelengths.

Limitations of crystal field theory

  1. It considers only the metal-ion d-orbitals and gives no consideration at all to other metal orbitals (such as \(s\), \(p_x\), \(p_y\), \(p_z\)).
  2. It is unable to account satisfactorily for the relative strengths of ligands; e.g. it gives no explanation as to why \(\mathrm{H_2O}\) is a stronger ligand than \(\mathrm{OH^-}\) in the spectrochemical series.
  3. According to this theory, the bond between the metal and the ligands is purely ionic. It gives no account of the partly covalent nature of metal–ligand bonds.
  4. The CFT cannot account for \(\pi\)-bonding in complexes.

Stability of coordination compounds

The stability of a coordination compound \(\mathrm{[ML_n]}\) is measured in terms of the stability constant (equilibrium constant) \(\beta_n\) for the overall reaction:

\[\mathrm{M(H_2O)}_n+n\mathrm{L}\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{ML}_n+n\mathrm{H_2O},\qquad\beta_n=\frac{[\mathrm{ML}_n]}{[\mathrm{M(H_2O)}_n]\,[\mathrm{L}]^n}\]

By convention, the water displaced is ignored, as its concentration remains essentially constant. The overall reaction takes place in steps, with a stability (formation) constant \(K_1,K_2,K_3,\dots,K_n\) for each step:

\[\mathrm{M(H_2O)}_n+\mathrm{L}\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{ML(H_2O)}_{n-1}+\mathrm{H_2O},\qquad K_1=\frac{[\mathrm{ML(H_2O)}_{n-1}]}{[\mathrm{M(H_2O)}_n]\,[\mathrm{L}]}\]\[\mathrm{ML}_{n-1}\mathrm{(H_2O)}+\mathrm{L}\rightleftharpoons\mathrm{ML}_n+\mathrm{H_2O},\qquad K_n=\frac{[\mathrm{ML}_n]}{[\mathrm{ML}_{n-1}\mathrm{(H_2O)}]\,[\mathrm{L}]}\]\[\beta_n=K_1\times K_2\times K_3\times\cdots\times K_n\]

\(\beta_n\), the stability constant, is related to the thermodynamic stability when the system has reached equilibrium.

Isomerism: classification

  • (1) Structural isomerism
    • (A) Ionisation isomerism
    • (B) Solvate / hydrate isomerism
    • (C) Linkage isomerism
    • (D) Coordination isomerism
    • (E) Ligand isomerism
    • (F) Polymerisation isomerism
  • (2) Stereoisomerism
    • Geometrical isomerism
    • Optical isomerism

Ionisation isomerism

Occurs when the counter ion in a coordination compound is itself a potential ligand and can displace a ligand, which can then become the counter ion.

\[\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_5SO_4]NO_3\ \text{and}\ [Co(NH_3)_5NO_3]SO_4}\]

Solvate / hydrate isomerism

Occurs when water forms a part of the coordination entity or is outside it.

ComplexReaction with \(\mathrm{AgNO_3}\) (molar ratio \(\mathrm{AgNO_3}\) : complex)Reaction with conc. \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) (dehydrating agent)
\(\mathrm{[Cr(H_2O)_6]Cl_3}\)3 : 1no water molecule is lost, i.e. no reaction
\(\mathrm{[CrCl(H_2O)_5]Cl_2\cdot H_2O}\)2 : 1one mole of water is lost per mole of complex
\(\mathrm{[CrCl_2(H_2O)_4]Cl\cdot2H_2O}\)1 : 1two moles of water are lost per mole of complex

Linkage isomerism

Occurs in ligands, like ambidentate ligands, which have two possible coordination sites.

\[\mathrm{[Co(ONO)(NH_3)_5]Cl_2\ \text{and}\ [Co(NO_2)(NH_3)_5]Cl_2}\]

Coordination isomerism

Shown by compounds made up of cationic and anionic coordination entities, due to interchange of ligands between the cation and anion entities.

\[\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6][Cr(CN)_6]\ \text{and}\ [Cr(NH_3)_6][Co(CN)_6]}\]

Ligand and polymerisation isomerism

  • Ligand isomerism: since many ligands are organic compounds which can themselves show isomerism, the resulting complexes can show isomerism from this source.
  • Polymerisation isomerism: considered a special case of coordination isomerism; the various isomers differ in formula weight from one another, so they are not true isomers in the real sense.

Geometrical isomerism: coordination number four

Geometrical isomerism is common among coordination compounds with coordination numbers 4 and 6.

  • Tetrahedral complexes cannot show geometrical isomerism, since all four positions are equivalent in tetrahedral geometry.
  • Square planar complexes: \(\mathrm{Pt(NH_3)_2Cl_2}\) exists as cis and trans isomers. A square planar complex of the type \(\mathrm{Ma_2bc}\) (a, b, c unidentate) shows two geometrical isomers; a complex of the type \(\mathrm{Mabcd}\) (a, b, c, d unidentate) shows three geometrical isomers.

Geometrical isomerism: coordination number six (octahedral)

Example: cis and trans isomers of \(\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_4Cl_2]^+}\); also cis and trans isomers of \(\mathrm{[CoCl_2(en)_2]}\). Number of possible geometrical isomers:

Type of complexGeometrical isomers
(I) Only unidentate ligands
\(\mathrm{Ma_2b_4}\)2
\(\mathrm{Ma_4bc}\)2
\(\mathrm{Ma_3b_3}\)2 (facial and meridional)
(II) Bidentate ligand (AA) and unidentate ligands
\(\mathrm{M(AA)a_3b}\)2
\(\mathrm{M(AA)a_2b_2}\)3 (a trans a; a trans b; b trans b)
\(\mathrm{M(AA)b_4}\)only one form
\(\mathrm{M(AA)abcd}\)6
\(\mathrm{M(AA)_2a_2}\)2 (cis and trans)

Optical isomerism

A coordination compound which can rotate the plane of polarised light is said to be optically active.

  • Octahedral complexes: optical isomerism is common in octahedral complexes involving didentate ligands; e.g. \(\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}\) has d and l forms.
  • Square planar complexes: rarely show optical isomerism. The plane formed by the four ligating atoms and the metal ion is considered to be a mirror plane, which prevents the possibility of chirality.

Organometallic compounds: metal carbonyls

Compounds of metals with CO as a ligand are called metal carbonyls. They are of two types.

  • (a) Monomeric: contain only one metal atom per molecule, e.g. \(\mathrm{[Ni(CO)_4]}\) (\(sp^3\), tetrahedral) and \(\mathrm{[Fe(CO)_5]}\) (\(dsp^3\), trigonal bipyramidal).
  • (b) Polymeric: contain two or more metal atoms per molecule and have metal–metal bonds, e.g. \(\mathrm{Mn_2(CO)_{10}}\), \(\mathrm{Co_2(CO)_9}\), etc.

Synergic bonding in metal carbonyls

The M–C bond is formed by the donation of a pair of electrons from a filled d orbital of the metal into the vacant antibonding \(\pi^*\) orbital of carbon monoxide. Thus carbon monoxide acts as a \(\sigma\) donor (\(\mathrm{OC\to M}\)) and a \(\pi\) acceptor (\(\mathrm{OC\leftarrow M}\)); the two interactions create a synergic effect which strengthens the bond between CO and the metal.

Sigma (σ) bonded organometallic compounds

  • (a) Grignard's reagent \(\mathrm{R{-}Mg{-}X}\), where R is an alkyl or aryl group and X is a halogen.
  • (b) \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_4Sn}\), \(\mathrm{(C_2H_5)_4Pb}\), \(\mathrm{Al_2(CH_3)_6}\), \(\mathrm{Al_2(C_2H_5)_6}\), etc.

Pi (π) bonded organometallic compounds

These are compounds of a metal with alkenes, alkynes, benzene and other ring compounds.

  • Zeise's salt: \(\mathrm{K[PtCl_3(\eta^2{-}C_2H_4)]}\), a salt of the anion \(\mathrm{[PtCl_3(\eta^2{-}C_2H_4)]^-}\) with \(\mathrm{K^+}\); the ethene molecule is bonded to Pt through its \(\pi\) bond (side-on, \(\eta^2\)).
  • Ferrocene: \(\mathrm{Fe(\eta^5{-}C_5H_5)_2}\).
  • Bis(benzene)chromium: \(\mathrm{Cr(\eta^6{-}C_6H_6)_2}\).
14
Inorganic · Elements & compounds

Metallurgy

Minerals, ores and gangue

The compound of a metal found in nature is a mineral. Minerals from which a metal can be extracted economically and conveniently are ores. An ore is usually contaminated with earthy or undesired material called gangue.

  • (a) Native ores contain the metal in the free state, e.g. silver, gold, platinum.
  • (b) Oxidised ores consist of oxides or oxysalts (carbonates, phosphates, sulphates, silicates) of metals.
  • (c) Sulphurised ores consist of sulphides of metals such as iron, lead, zinc, mercury.
  • (d) Halide ores consist of halides of metals.

Important ores

MetalOreComposition
AluminiumBauxite\(\mathrm{AlO_x(OH)_{3-2x}}\) where \(0\lt x\lt1\) (mainly \(\mathrm{Al_2O_3}\))

Steps of extraction

Metallurgy is the scientific and technological process used for the extraction and isolation of a metal from its ore. The major steps are:

  1. (A) Crushing and grinding: the ore is crushed by jaw crushers and ground to a powder.
  2. (B) Concentration (dressing, benefaction): removal of unwanted impurities.
  3. (C) Extraction of the crude metal from the concentrated ore: (i) conversion to oxide, (ii) reduction of the metal oxide.
  4. (D) Purification (refining) of the metal.

(B) Concentration of the ore

  • (i) Hydraulic washing / gravity separation / levigation: based on the difference in densities of the gangue and ore particles. Generally used for oxide and native ores.
  • (ii) Electromagnetic separation: based on differences in magnetic properties of the ore components. Chromite ore \(\mathrm{(FeO\cdot Cr_2O_3)}\) is separated from non-magnetic siliceous impurities, and cassiterite \(\mathrm{(SnO_2)}\) is separated from magnetic wolframite \(\mathrm{(FeWO_4+MnWO_4)}\).
  • (iii) Froth flotation: commonly used for low-grade sulphide ores such as galena \(\mathrm{PbS}\), copper pyrites \(\mathrm{CuFeS_2}\) (\(\mathrm{Cu_2S\cdot Fe_2S_3}\)), zinc blende \(\mathrm{ZnS}\). It is based on the different wettability of gangue and ore: gangue particles are preferentially wetted by water, while ore particles are wetted by oil (pine oil). One or more chemical frothing agents are added.
  • (iv) Leaching: used if the ore is soluble in a suitable solvent, e.g. acids, bases and suitable chemical reagents.

(C)(i) Conversion to oxide: calcination and roasting

Calcination is heating the concentrated ore strongly in a limited supply of air or in the absence of air. It brings about the following changes:

  • (a) A carbonate ore decomposes to the oxide of the metal.
  • (b) Water of crystallisation of a hydrated ore is lost as moisture.
  • (c) Organic matter, if present, is expelled and the ore becomes porous; volatile impurities are removed.

Roasting is heating the concentrated ore (generally a sulphide ore) strongly in excess of air or \(\mathrm{O_2}\) below its melting point. Roasting is exothermic: once started it does not require additional heating.

Smelting and slag formation

In many extractions an oxide is added deliberately to combine with impurities and form a stable molten phase, immiscible with the molten metal, called a slag. The process is called smelting.

\[\text{non-metal oxide (acidic oxide)}+\text{metal oxide (basic oxide)}\to\text{fusible slag}\]

Removal of a basic oxide: \(\mathrm{FeO}\) is the impurity in the extraction of \(\mathrm{Cu}\) from copper pyrite.

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{2CuFeS_2+4O_2\to Cu_2S+2FeO+3SO_2}\qquad(\text{roasted pyrite})\\&\mathrm{Cu_2S+FeO+SiO_2\to FeSiO_3\ (\text{fusible slag, upper layer})+Cu_2S\ (\text{matte, lower layer})}\end{aligned}\]

Matte also contains a very small amount of iron(II) sulphide.

Removal of acidic impurities such as sand and \(\mathrm{P_4O_{10}}\): smelting is done in the presence of limestone.

\[\mathrm{CaCO_3\to CaO+CO_2}\qquad\mathrm{CaO+SiO_2\to CaSiO_3}\ (\text{fusible slag})\]\[\mathrm{6CaO+P_4O_{10}\to2Ca_3(PO_4)_2}\ (\text{fusible slag; Thomas slag})\]

(C)(ii) Reduction of a metal oxide

The free metal is obtained by reduction of a compound, using either a chemical reducing agent or electrolysis.

Reduction with carbon:

\[\mathrm{PbO+C\to Pb+CO}\qquad(\text{extraction of lead})\]

Reduction with CO: in some cases the CO produced in the furnace itself is the reducing agent.

\[\mathrm{Fe_2O_3+3CO\to2Fe+3CO_2}\]

Reduction by other metals (aluminothermic or Goldschmidt process). Oxides of Cr and Mn can be reduced by a highly electropositive metal such as aluminium, which liberates a large amount of energy (1675 kJ/mol) on oxidation to \(\mathrm{Al_2O_3}\). The reaction is called the thermite reaction.

\[\mathrm{Cr_2O_3+2Al\to2Cr\,(\ell)+Al_2O_3}\]

Magnesium (or sodium) reduction. Where the oxide is too stable to reduce, electropositive metals are used to reduce halides.

\[\mathrm{TiCl_4+2Mg\xrightarrow{1000-1150^\circ C}Ti+2MgCl_2}\quad(\text{Kroll process})\]\[\mathrm{TiCl_4+4Na\to Ti+4NaCl}\quad(\text{Mond-type / sodium process})\]

Self-reduction (auto-reduction, air reduction)

If the sulphide ore of a less electropositive metal such as Hg, Cu, Pb, Sb is heated in air, a part of it is changed into oxide or sulphate, which then reacts with the remaining sulphide to give the metal and \(\mathrm{SO_2}\).

\[\mathrm{2Cu_2S+3O_2\to2Cu_2O+2SO_2}\qquad\mathrm{2Cu_2O+Cu_2S\to6Cu+SO_2}\]

Electrolytic reduction

The most powerful method of reduction; it gives a very pure product. It is expensive compared with chemical methods, so it is used for very reactive metals such as Mg or Al, or for samples of high purity.

  1. In aqueous solution: convenient and cheap if the products do not react with water. Copper and zinc are obtained by electrolysis of aqueous solutions of their sulphates.
  2. In fused melts: aluminium is obtained by electrolysis of a fused mixture of \(\mathrm{Al_2O_3}\) and cryolite \(\mathrm{Na_3[AlF_6]}\).

Extraction of aluminium

(a) Purification of bauxite (leaching): bauxite is digested with concentrated \(\mathrm{NaOH}\) to give soluble sodium aluminate; \(\mathrm{CO_2}\) precipitates hydrated alumina, which is filtered and ignited to pure \(\mathrm{Al_2O_3}\).

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{Al_2O_3+2NaOH+3H_2O\to2Na[Al(OH)_4]}\\&\mathrm{2Na[Al(OH)_4]+CO_2\to Al_2O_3\cdot xH_2O\downarrow+2NaHCO_3}\\&\mathrm{Al_2O_3\cdot xH_2O\xrightarrow{1470\ K}Al_2O_3+xH_2O}\end{aligned}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a flow chart.

(b) Electrolytic reduction (Hall–Héroult process) of \(\mathrm{Al_2O_3}\) dissolved in molten cryolite:

\[\mathrm{2Al_2O_3+3C\to4Al+3CO_2}\]\[\text{Cathode: }\mathrm{Al^{3+}(melt)+3e^-\to Al(\ell)}\]\[\text{Anode: }\mathrm{C(s)+O^{2-}(melt)\to CO(g)+2e^-}\qquad\mathrm{C(s)+2O^{2-}(melt)\to CO_2(g)+4e^-}\]

1. Extraction of iron from haematite

At 500–800 K (lower temperature range in the blast furnace):

\[\mathrm{3Fe_2O_3+CO\to2Fe_3O_4+CO_2}\qquad\mathrm{Fe_3O_4+4CO\to3Fe+4CO_2}\qquad\mathrm{Fe_2O_3+CO\to2FeO+CO_2}\]

At 900–1500 K (higher temperature range):

\[\mathrm{C+CO_2\to2CO}\qquad\mathrm{FeO+CO\to Fe+CO_2}\]

Limestone is decomposed to \(\mathrm{CaO}\), which removes the silicate impurity of the ore as slag. The slag is molten and separates from the iron.

\[\mathrm{CaCO_3\to CaO+CO_2}\qquad\mathrm{CaO+SiO_2\to CaSiO_3}\]

2. Extraction of copper

From copper glance / copper pyrite (self-reduction):

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{2CuFeS_2+4O_2\to Cu_2S+2FeO+3SO_2}\\&\mathrm{Cu_2S+FeO+SiO_2\to FeSiO_3\ (\text{fusible slag})+Cu_2S\ (\text{matte})}\\&\mathrm{2FeS+3O_2\to2FeO+2SO_2}\qquad\mathrm{FeO+SiO_2\to FeSiO_3}\\&\mathrm{2Cu_2S+3O_2\to2Cu_2O+2SO_2}\qquad\mathrm{2Cu_2O+Cu_2S\to6Cu+SO_2}\ (\text{self-reduction})\end{aligned}\]

3. Extraction of lead

(i) Roasting, then reduction with carbon:

\[\mathrm{2PbS+3O_2\to2PbO+2SO_2}\]\[\mathrm{2PbO+C\to2Pb\,(\ell)+CO_2}\]

(ii) Self-reduction: partial roasting in air, then heating in absence of air:

\[\mathrm{3PbS+3O_2\to PbS+2PbO+2SO_2}\]\[\mathrm{PbS+2PbO\to3Pb\,(\ell)+SO_2}\]

4. Extraction of zinc from zinc blende

The ore is roasted in excess of air at about 1200 K.

\[\mathrm{2ZnS+3O_2\to2ZnO+2SO_2}\]

The oxide is reduced with coke:

\[\mathrm{ZnO+C\xrightarrow{\text{coke, }1673\ K}Zn+CO}\]

5. Extraction of tin from cassiterite

The concentrated ore is subjected to electromagnetic separation to remove magnetic wolframite. \(\mathrm{SnO_2}\) is reduced with carbon at 1200–1300 °C in an electric furnace. The product often contains traces of Fe, removed by blowing air through the molten mixture to oxidise it to \(\mathrm{FeO}\), which floats to the surface.

\[\mathrm{SnO_2+2C\to Sn+2CO}\qquad\mathrm{2Fe+O_2\to2FeO}\]

6. Extraction of magnesium from sea water (Dow's process)

Sea water contains 0.13% magnesium as chloride and sulphate.

  1. (a) Magnesium is precipitated as hydroxide by slaked lime: \(\mathrm{Mg^{2+}+Ca(OH)_2\to Mg(OH)_2\downarrow+Ca^{2+}}\).
  2. (b) Preparation of hexahydrated magnesium chloride: the hydroxide is converted to chloride; concentration and crystallisation give \(\mathrm{MgCl_2\cdot6H_2O}\).
  3. (c) Preparation of anhydrous magnesium chloride (dehydration, in a current of dry HCl).
  4. (d) Electrolysis of fused anhydrous \(\mathrm{MgCl_2}\) in the presence of \(\mathrm{NaCl}\):
\[\mathrm{MgCl_2\to Mg^{2+}+2Cl^-}\]\[\text{Cathode: }\mathrm{Mg^{2+}+2e^-\to Mg}\ (99\%\ \text{pure})\qquad\text{Anode: }\mathrm{2Cl^-\to Cl_2+2e^-}\]

7. Extraction of gold and silver (MacArthur–Forrest cyanide process)

(a) From native ores by leaching the metal with \(\mathrm{CN^-}\):

\[\mathrm{4Au/Ag(s)+8CN^-(aq)+2H_2O(aq)+O_2(g)\to4[Au/Ag(CN)_2]^-(aq)+4OH^-(aq)}\]\[\mathrm{2[Au/Ag(CN)_2]^-(aq)+Zn(s)\to2Au/Ag(s)+[Zn(CN)_4]^{2-}(aq)}\]

(b) From argentite ore:

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{Ag_2S\ (\text{conc. ore})+2NaCN\to2AgCN+Na_2S}\\&\mathrm{4Na_2S+5O_2+2H_2O\to2Na_2SO_4+4NaOH+2S}\\&\mathrm{AgCN+NaCN\to Na[Ag(CN)_2]}\ (\text{soluble complex})\\&\mathrm{2Na[Ag(CN)_2]+Zn\ (\text{dust})\to2Ag+Na_2[Zn(CN)_4]}\end{aligned}\]

(D) Refining of metals: physical methods

  • (I) Liquation: for a metal that is readily fusible while the impurities are not; used for purification of Sn and Zn, and for removing Pb from a Zn–Ag alloy.
  • (II) Fractional distillation: for metals that are themselves volatile with non-volatile impurities (and vice versa). Zn, Cd and Hg are purified this way.
  • (III) Zone refining (fractional crystallisation): used when metals are required in very high purity, e.g. pure Si and Ge for semiconductors.

(D) Refining of metals: chemical methods

  • (I) Oxidative refining: usually for Pb, Ag, Cu, Fe etc.; the molten impure metal is subjected to oxidation in various ways.
  • (II) Poling: purification of copper and tin containing their own oxides as impurities. Green wood poles release hydrocarbons such as \(\mathrm{CH_4}\), which reduce the oxide: \(\mathrm{4CuO+CH_4\to4Cu\,(pure)+CO_2+2H_2O}\).
  • (III) Electrolytic refining: metals such as Cu, Ni and Al are refined electrolytically.
  • (IV) Vapour phase refining:
    (i) Mond's process (nickel): \(\mathrm{H_2O(g)+C\to CO(g)+H_2}\); then \(\mathrm{Ni(s)+4CO(g)\xrightarrow{50^\circ C}[Ni(CO)_4](g)}\), and \(\mathrm{[Ni(CO)_4](g)\xrightarrow{200^\circ C}Ni+4CO(g)}\).
    (ii) Van Arkel–de Boer process: \(\mathrm{Ti\,(impure)+2I_2\xrightarrow{50-250^\circ C}TiI_4}\); on a tungsten filament at 1400 °C, \(\mathrm{TiI_4\to Ti+2I_2}\).

Ellingham diagram

\[\Delta G^\circ=\Delta H^\circ-T\Delta S^\circ,\qquad\text{slope of the line}=-\Delta S^\circ\]
  • Metal + \(\mathrm{O_2}\to\) oxide: gas consumed, \(\Delta S^\circ\lt0\), so the line rises with T. \(\mathrm{C+O_2\to CO_2}\) is almost horizontal (\(\Delta S^\circ\approx0\)); \(\mathrm{2C+O_2\to2CO}\) slopes downwards (\(\Delta S^\circ\gt0\)).
  • A metal (or carbon) can reduce the oxide of any metal whose line lies above it, because the combined \(\Delta G^\circ\) becomes negative. Above about 1073 K carbon reduces FeO (CO line below Fe line), which is why blast-furnace reduction occurs in the hot zone. Al reduces \(\mathrm{Cr_2O_3}\) (thermite).
  • Lines meet at a change of state (kink at the melting/boiling point of the metal). Oxides of Hg, Ag, Cu\(_2\)O decompose on heating because \(\Delta G^\circ\) of formation turns positive at moderate T.
15
Inorganic · Elements & compounds

s-Block Elements

Members of the s-block

GroupElements
Group 1 (alkali metals)Lithium (Li), sodium (Na), potassium (K), rubidium (Rb), caesium (Cs) and francium (Fr)
Group 2 (alkaline earth metals)Beryllium (Be), magnesium (Mg), calcium (Ca), strontium (Sr), barium (Ba) and radium (Ra)

Group 1: hydration enthalpy

The hydration enthalpies of alkali metal ions decrease with increase in ionic size.

\(\mathrm{Li^+}\) has the maximum degree of hydration, and for this reason lithium salts are mostly hydrated, e.g. \(\mathrm{LiCl\cdot 2H_2O}\).

Group 1: physical properties and flame colours

All the alkali metals are silvery white, soft and light metals. Because of the larger size, these elements have low density. The melting and boiling points of the alkali metals are low, indicating weak metallic bonding. Alkali metals and their salts impart characteristic colour to an oxidising flame.

MetalLiNaKRbCs
Flame colourCrimson redYellowViolet / lilacRed violetBlue

Group 1: reactivity towards air

The alkali metals are highly reactive due to their larger size and low ionisation enthalpy. They burn vigorously in oxygen forming oxides: lithium forms the monoxide, sodium forms the peroxide, and the other metals form superoxides.

\[\mathrm{4Li + O_2 \to 2Li_2O}\]\[\mathrm{2Na + O_2 \to Na_2O_2}\]\[\mathrm{M + O_2 \to MO_2}\qquad(\mathrm{M = K,\ Rb,\ Cs})\]

Group 1: reducing nature

The alkali metals are strong reducing agents, lithium being the most and sodium the least powerful.

Group 1: solution in liquid ammonia

The alkali metals dissolve in liquid ammonia giving deep blue solutions which are conducting in nature.

\[\mathrm{M + (x+y)NH_3 \to [M(NH_3)_x]^+ + [e(NH_3)_y]^-}\]

The blue colour of the solution is due to the ammoniated electron, and the solution is paramagnetic. On standing, the solution slowly liberates hydrogen with formation of amide:

\[\mathrm{M^+(am) + e^- + NH_3(l) \xrightarrow{\text{on standing}} MNH_2(am) + \tfrac12H_2(g)}\]

In concentrated solution the blue colour changes to bronze colour and the solution becomes diamagnetic.

Anomalous properties of lithium

The anomalous behaviour of lithium is due to (i) the exceptionally small size of its atom and ion, and (ii) its high polarising power (i.e. charge/radius ratio).

The similarity between lithium and magnesium (diagonal relationship) is particularly striking and arises because of their similar size:

LiMg
Atomic radius152 pm160 pm
Ionic radius\(\mathrm{Li^+}\) = 76 pm\(\mathrm{Mg^{2+}}\) = 72 pm

Group 2: alkaline earth metals

The first element, beryllium, differs from the rest of the members and shows a diagonal relationship to aluminium.

Group 2: hydration enthalpies

Hydration enthalpies of alkaline earth metal ions:

\[\mathrm{Be^{2+} \gt Mg^{2+} \gt Ca^{2+} \gt Sr^{2+} \gt Ba^{2+}}\]

The hydration enthalpies of alkaline earth metal ions are larger than those of alkali metal ions. Thus the compounds of alkaline earth metals are more extensively hydrated than those of alkali metals, e.g. \(\mathrm{MgCl_2}\) and \(\mathrm{CaCl_2}\) exist as \(\mathrm{MgCl_2\cdot6H_2O}\) and \(\mathrm{CaCl_2\cdot6H_2O}\), while \(\mathrm{NaCl}\) and \(\mathrm{KCl}\) do not form such hydrates.

Group 2: physical properties and flame colours

The alkaline earth metals, in general, are silvery white, lustrous and relatively soft, but harder than the alkali metals. Their melting and boiling points are higher due to smaller sizes. Because of the low ionisation enthalpies they are strongly electropositive in nature.

The electrons in beryllium and magnesium are too strongly bound to get excited by flame, hence these elements do not impart any colour to the flame. Calcium, strontium and barium impart characteristic colour to the flame.

MetalBeMgCaSrBa
Flame colourNo colourNo colourBrick redCrimsonApple green

Group 2: reactivity towards air and water; reducing nature

  • Beryllium and magnesium are inert to oxygen and water.
  • Magnesium is more electropositive and burns with dazzling brilliance in air to give \(\mathrm{MgO}\) and \(\mathrm{Mg_3N_2}\).
  • Calcium, strontium and barium are readily attacked by air to form the oxide and nitride.
  • The alkaline earth metals are strong reducing agents. This is indicated by the large negative values of their reduction potentials.

Group 2: solution in liquid ammonia

The alkaline earth metals dissolve in liquid ammonia to give deep blue-black solutions forming ammoniated ions.

\[\mathrm{M + (x+y)NH_3 \to [M(NH_3)_x]^{2+} + 2[e(NH_3)_y]^-}\]

From these solutions, the ammoniates \(\mathrm{[M(NH_3)_6]^{2+}}\) can be recovered.

Anomalous behaviour of beryllium: diagonal relationship with aluminium

Beryllium, the first member of the Group 2 metals, shows anomalous behaviour as compared to magnesium and the rest of the members. Further, it shows a diagonal relationship to aluminium.

The ionic radius of \(\mathrm{Be^{2+}}\) is estimated to be 31 pm; the charge/radius ratio is nearly the same as that of the \(\mathrm{Al^{3+}}\) ion. Hence beryllium resembles aluminium in some ways.

Sodium oxide, Na2O

Preparation:

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{4Na + O_2 \to 2Na_2O}\\&\mathrm{2Na + Na_2O_2 \to 2Na_2O}\\&\mathrm{6Na + 2NaNO_2 \to 4Na_2O + N_2}\end{aligned}\]

Properties: white solid, basic oxide; reacts violently with water and with acids and acidic oxides.

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{Na_2O + H_2O \to 2NaOH}\\&\mathrm{Na_2O + 2HCl \to 2NaCl + H_2O}\\&\mathrm{Na_2O + CO_2 \to Na_2CO_3}\end{aligned}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Sodium peroxide, Na2O2

Preparation: heating sodium in excess air or oxygen (at about 570 K).

\[\mathrm{2Na + O_2 \xrightarrow{\Delta} Na_2O_2}\]

Properties: pale yellow solid containing the peroxide ion \(\mathrm{O_2^{2-}}\) (diamagnetic); a strong oxidising agent.

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{Na_2O_2 + 2H_2O \xrightarrow{\text{cold}} 2NaOH + H_2O_2}\\&\mathrm{Na_2O_2 + H_2SO_4\ (\text{dil., cold}) \to Na_2SO_4 + H_2O_2}\\&\mathrm{2Na_2O_2 + 2H_2O \xrightarrow{\text{warm}} 4NaOH + O_2}\\&\mathrm{2Na_2O_2 + 2CO_2 \to 2Na_2CO_3 + O_2}\\&\mathrm{Na_2O_2 + CO \to Na_2CO_3}\\&\mathrm{2Cr(OH)_3 + 3Na_2O_2 \to 2Na_2CrO_4 + 2NaOH + 2H_2O}\end{aligned}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Sodium hydroxide, NaOH

Preparation. (i) Castner–Kellner process: electrolysis of brine with a mercury cathode and graphite anode.

\[\begin{aligned}&\text{Cathode: }\mathrm{Na^+ + e^- \to Na(Hg)}\\&\text{Anode: }\mathrm{Cl^- \to \tfrac12Cl_2 + e^-}\\&\mathrm{2Na(Hg) + 2H_2O \to 2NaOH + H_2 + 2Hg}\end{aligned}\]

(ii) Causticisation of sodium carbonate:

\[\mathrm{Na_2CO_3 + Ca(OH)_2 \to 2NaOH + CaCO_3\downarrow}\]

Properties. White, deliquescent solid, strongly basic and soluble in water; absorbs \(\mathrm{CO_2}\) from air.

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{2NaOH + CO_2 \to Na_2CO_3 + H_2O}\\&\mathrm{2Al + 2NaOH + 6H_2O \to 2Na[Al(OH)_4] + 3H_2}\\&\mathrm{Zn + 2NaOH \to Na_2ZnO_2 + H_2}\\&\mathrm{2NaOH + Cl_2 \to NaCl + NaOCl + H_2O}\quad(\text{cold, dil.})\\&\mathrm{6NaOH + 3Cl_2 \to 5NaCl + NaClO_3 + 3H_2O}\quad(\text{hot, conc.})\\&\mathrm{P_4 + 3NaOH + 3H_2O \to PH_3 + 3NaH_2PO_2}\end{aligned}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Sodium carbonate, Na2CO3 (Solvay process)

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{NaCl + NH_3 + CO_2 + H_2O \to NaHCO_3\downarrow + NH_4Cl}\\&\mathrm{2NaHCO_3 \xrightarrow{\Delta} Na_2CO_3 + H_2O + CO_2}\\&\mathrm{CaCO_3 \xrightarrow{\Delta} CaO + CO_2}\\&\mathrm{CaO + H_2O \to Ca(OH)_2}\\&\mathrm{2NH_4Cl + Ca(OH)_2 \to 2NH_3 + CaCl_2 + 2H_2O}\end{aligned}\]

Overall: \(\mathrm{2NaCl + CaCO_3 \to Na_2CO_3 + CaCl_2}\) (ammonia is recovered and re-used).

Properties. Washing soda is \(\mathrm{Na_2CO_3\cdot10H_2O}\); on heating it loses water of crystallisation and finally gives anhydrous \(\mathrm{Na_2CO_3}\). Its aqueous solution is alkaline due to hydrolysis:

\[\mathrm{CO_3^{2-} + H_2O \rightleftharpoons HCO_3^- + OH^-}\]\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{Na_2CO_3 + 2HCl \to 2NaCl + H_2O + CO_2\uparrow}\\&\mathrm{Na_2CO_3 + H_2O + CO_2 \to 2NaHCO_3}\end{aligned}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Quick lime, slaked lime and lime water

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{CaCO_3 \xrightarrow{1070-1270\ K} CaO + CO_2}\qquad(\text{quick lime})\\&\mathrm{CaO + H_2O \to Ca(OH)_2}\qquad(\text{slaked lime})\end{aligned}\]

Lime water is the clear aqueous solution of \(\mathrm{Ca(OH)_2}\). It turns milky with \(\mathrm{CO_2}\) (due to \(\mathrm{CaCO_3}\)), and the milkiness disappears with excess \(\mathrm{CO_2}\):

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{Ca(OH)_2 + CO_2 \to CaCO_3\downarrow + H_2O}\\&\mathrm{CaCO_3 + H_2O + CO_2 \to Ca(HCO_3)_2}\end{aligned}\]

Quick lime is a basic oxide and a flux: \(\mathrm{CaO + SiO_2 \to CaSiO_3}\).

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Bleaching powder

Formed when slaked lime absorbs chlorine:

\[\mathrm{3Ca(OH)_2 + 2Cl_2 \to Ca(OCl)_2\cdot Ca(OH)_2\cdot CaCl_2\cdot 2H_2O}\]

It is commonly written as \(\mathrm{CaOCl_2}\); with dilute acid it liberates chlorine (the source of its bleaching and oxidising action):

\[\mathrm{CaOCl_2 + H_2SO_4 \to CaSO_4 + Cl_2\uparrow + H_2O}\]

The reaction line is from the booklet; the properties line is a rebuilt addition.

Industrial compounds: processes and uses

  • Castner–Kellner: electrolysis of brine with a mercury cathode gives NaOH, \(\mathrm{Cl_2}\), \(\mathrm{H_2}\). Downs process: electrolysis of fused \(\mathrm{NaCl+CaCl_2}\) (about 873 K) gives Na.
  • Baking soda \(\mathrm{NaHCO_3}\) (Solvay): \(\mathrm{2NaHCO_3\xrightarrow{\Delta}Na_2CO_3+H_2O+CO_2}\); mild antacid. Washing soda \(\mathrm{Na_2CO_3\cdot10H_2O}\).
  • Gypsum \(\mathrm{CaSO_4\cdot2H_2O}\) loses water on heating at 393 K to give plaster of Paris \(\mathrm{CaSO_4\cdot\tfrac12H_2O}\); above 473 K it becomes anhydrous (dead burnt plaster). Plaster sets back to gypsum with water (expansion).
  • Portland cement: \(\mathrm{Ca_3SiO_5}\) (alite), \(\mathrm{Ca_2SiO_4}\), \(\mathrm{Ca_3Al_2O_6}\); gypsum (2–3%) is added to slow setting. Lime: \(\mathrm{CaCO_3\xrightarrow{1070-1270\,K}CaO+CO_2}\).

Inorganic · Elements & compounds

p-Block Elements

Trends in properties of p-block elements

IncreasesDecreases
Down a groupCovalent radius, van der Waals' radius, metallic characterElectronegativity, ionisation enthalpy, oxidising power, enthalpy of atomisation (except for \(\mathrm{N_2}\), \(\mathrm{O_2}\), \(\mathrm{F_2}\))
Across a period (left to right)Electronegativity, ionisation enthalpy, oxidising power; enthalpy of atomisation (up to group 14)Covalent radius, van der Waals' radius, metallic character

Group 13: oxidation state and chemical reactivity

General oxidation state \(=+3\).

Reactivity towards acids and alkalies:

\[\mathrm{2Al(s)+6HCl(aq)\to2Al^{3+}(aq)+6Cl^-(aq)+3H_2(g)}\]\[\mathrm{2Al(s)+2NaOH(aq)+6H_2O(\ell)\to2Na^+[Al(OH)_4]^-(aq)+3H_2(g)}\]

Na[Al(OH)4] is sodium tetrahydroxoaluminate(III).

Reactivity towards halogens:

\[\mathrm{2E(s)+3X_2(g)\to2EX_3(s)}\qquad(\mathrm{X=F,Cl,Br,I})\]

Boron and its compounds: important reactions

  • Borax on heating loses water and fuses to a transparent glassy bead: \(\mathrm{Na_2B_4O_7\cdot10H_2O\xrightarrow{\Delta}Na_2B_4O_7\to2NaBO_2+B_2O_3}\). The metal oxides dissolve in the bead to give coloured metaborates (borax bead test).
  • Boric acid from borax: \(\mathrm{Na_2B_4O_7+2HCl+5H_2O\to4H_3BO_3+2NaCl}\). \(\mathrm{H_3BO_3}\) is a weak monobasic Lewis acid: \(\mathrm{B(OH)_3+2H_2O\rightleftharpoons[B(OH)_4]^-+H_3O^+}\). On heating: \(\mathrm{H_3BO_3\xrightarrow{370\,K}HBO_2\xrightarrow{\gt370\,K}B_2O_3}\).
  • Diborane, \(\mathrm{B_2H_6}\): \(\mathrm{4BF_3+3LiAlH_4\to2B_2H_6+3LiF+3AlF_3}\); \(\mathrm{2NaBH_4+I_2\to B_2H_6+2NaI+H_2}\).
  • \(\mathrm{B_2H_6+3O_2\to B_2O_3+3H_2O}\) (highly exothermic); \(\mathrm{B_2H_6+6H_2O\to2H_3BO_3+6H_2}\); \(\mathrm{B_2H_6+2NaH\to2Na[BH_4]}\).

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a reaction scheme.

Cleavage of diborane

Small amines such as \(\mathrm{NH_3}\), \(\mathrm{CH_3NH_2}\) and \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_2NH}\) give unsymmetrical cleavage of diborane:

\[\mathrm{B_2H_6+2NH_3\to[H_2B(NH_3)_2]^++[BH_4]^-}\]

Large amines such as \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_3N}\) and pyridine give symmetrical cleavage:

\[\mathrm{B_2H_6+2(CH_3)_3N\to2H_3B\leftarrow N(CH_3)_3}\]\[\mathrm{B_2H_6+2CO\xrightarrow{200^\circ C,\ 20\ atm}2BH_3CO}\ (\text{borane carbonyl})\]

Group 14 (carbon family): oxidation states and reactivity

Carbon (C), silicon (Si), germanium (Ge), tin (Sn) and lead (Pb). Electronic configuration \(ns^2np^2\).

Common oxidation states are \(+4\) and \(+2\); carbon also shows negative oxidation states. In the heavier members the tendency to show the \(+2\) state increases in the sequence \(\mathrm{Ge\lt Sn\lt Pb}\).

  • (i) Towards oxygen: all members form oxides on heating in oxygen: the monoxide \(\mathrm{MO}\) and the dioxide \(\mathrm{MO_2}\).
  • (ii) Towards water: tin decomposes steam to give the dioxide and dihydrogen.
  • (iii) Towards halogens: halides \(\mathrm{MX_2}\) and \(\mathrm{MX_4}\) (\(\mathrm{X=F,Cl,Br,I}\)). The stability of the dihalides increases down the group.

Anomalous behaviour of carbon: catenation

Order of catenation: \(\mathrm{C\gg Si\gt Ge\approx Sn}\). Lead does not show catenation. Because of catenation and \(p\pi\!-\!p\pi\) bond formation, carbon shows allotropic forms.

BondBond enthalpy (\(\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\))BondBond enthalpy (\(\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\))
C–C348Si–Si297
Ge–Ge260Sn–Sn240

Allotropes of carbon

  • Diamond: crystalline lattice; each carbon is \(sp^3\) hybridised and linked tetrahedrally to four other carbon atoms. C–C bond length is 154 pm; a rigid three-dimensional network.
  • Graphite: layered structure; layers are held by van der Waals forces and the distance between two layers is 340 pm. Each layer is made of planar hexagonal rings; the C–C bond length within a layer is 141.5 pm. Each carbon in the hexagonal ring is \(sp^2\) hybridised. Graphite conducts electricity along the sheet. It cleaves easily between the layers, so it is very soft and slippery and is used as a dry lubricant in machines running at high temperature.
  • Fullerenes: the \(\mathrm{C_{60}}\) molecule is shaped like a soccer ball and is called buckminsterfullerene. It contains twenty six-membered rings and twelve five-membered rings. The ball has 60 vertices, each occupied by one carbon atom, and contains both single and double bonds, with C–C distances of 143.5 pm and 138.3 pm respectively.

Important reactions of CO, CO2 and metal carbides

\[\mathrm{HCOOH\xrightarrow{\text{conc. }H_2SO_4,\ \Delta}CO+H_2O}\qquad\mathrm{H_2C_2O_4\xrightarrow{\text{conc. }H_2SO_4}CO+CO_2+H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{Fe_2O_3+3CO\to2Fe+3CO_2}\qquad\mathrm{CO+Cl_2\to COCl_2}\ (\text{phosgene})\]\[\mathrm{CaCO_3+2HCl\to CaCl_2+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\qquad\mathrm{CO_2+Ca(OH)_2\to CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{CaC_2+2H_2O\to Ca(OH)_2+C_2H_2}\qquad\mathrm{Al_4C_3+12H_2O\to4Al(OH)_3+3CH_4}\qquad\mathrm{Mg_2C_3+4H_2O\to2Mg(OH)_2+C_3H_4}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a reaction scheme.

Classification of silicates

The basic unit is the \(\mathrm{SiO_4^{4-}}\) tetrahedron. The classification depends on how many oxygen atoms each tetrahedron shares with its neighbours.

TypeOxygens shared per \(\mathrm{SiO_4}\)AnionExamples
(A) Orthosilicates0 (discrete units)\(\mathrm{SiO_4^{4-}}\)Olivine \(\mathrm{Mg_2SiO_4}\), zircon \(\mathrm{ZrSiO_4}\), willemite \(\mathrm{Zn_2SiO_4}\)
(B) Pyrosilicates1 (two units share one O)\(\mathrm{Si_2O_7^{6-}}\)Thortveitite \(\mathrm{Sc_2Si_2O_7}\)
(C) Cyclic silicates2 (closed rings)\(\mathrm{(SiO_3)_n^{2n-}}\), e.g. \(\mathrm{Si_3O_9^{6-}}\), \(\mathrm{Si_6O_{18}^{12-}}\)Beryl \(\mathrm{Be_3Al_2Si_6O_{18}}\)
(D) Chain silicates2 (single chain); 2 and 3 alternately (double chain)Single chain \(\mathrm{(SiO_3)_n^{2n-}}\); double chain \(\mathrm{(Si_4O_{11})_n^{6n-}}\)Spodumene \(\mathrm{LiAl(SiO_3)_2}\); asbestos
(E) Two-dimensional sheet silicates3\(\mathrm{(Si_2O_5)_n^{2n-}}\)Talc, mica, kaolinite
(F) Three-dimensional silicates4\(\mathrm{SiO_2}\) frameworkQuartz, feldspar, zeolites

In a sheet silicate three oxygen atoms of each tetrahedron are shared with adjacent \(\mathrm{SiO_4^{4-}}\) tetrahedra, forming a two-dimensional sheet of general formula \(\mathrm{(Si_2O_5)_n^{2n-}}\). In a three-dimensional silicate all four oxygen atoms of each tetrahedron are shared with adjacent \(\mathrm{SiO_4^{4-}}\) units.

Structures are drawings in the original; the table of types and examples is a standard textbook rebuild.

Silicones

Silicones are prepared only from chlorosilanes of the types \(\mathrm{R_3SiCl}\), \(\mathrm{R_2SiCl_2}\) and \(\mathrm{RSiCl_3}\), by hydrolysis and condensation.

  • From \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_3SiCl}\): \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_3SiCl+H_2O\to(CH_3)_3SiOH+HCl}\); then \(\mathrm{2(CH_3)_3SiOH\to(CH_3)_3Si{-}O{-}Si(CH_3)_3+H_2O}\) (a chain-terminating unit).
  • From \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_2SiCl_2}\): \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_2SiCl_2+2H_2O\to(CH_3)_2Si(OH)_2+2HCl}\); condensation gives long linear chains \(\mathrm{[-(CH_3)_2Si{-}O-]_n}\).
  • Hydrolysis of a mixture of \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_3SiCl}\) and \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_2SiCl_2}\) gives chains of controlled length, terminated by \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_3Si{-}}\) groups.
  • \(\mathrm{CH_3SiCl_3}\) on hydrolysis gives a complex cross-linked polymer.

The hydrocarbon layer along the silicon–oxygen chain makes silicones water-repellent.

Group 15 (nitrogen family): general properties

Electronic configuration \(ns^2np^3\). Covalent and ionic radii (in a particular state) increase down the group.

Physical properties: all the elements are polyatomic. Metallic character increases down the group. Boiling points in general increase from top to bottom, but the melting point increases up to arsenic and then decreases up to bismuth. Except nitrogen, all the elements show allotropy.

Oxidation states: the common states are \(-3\), \(+3\) and \(+5\). The stability of \(+5\) decreases and that of \(+3\) increases down the group (inert-pair effect):

\[\mathrm{Bi^{3+}\gt Sb^{3+}\gt As^{3+}}\qquad\mathrm{Bi^{5+}\lt Sb^{5+}\lt As^{5+}}\]

Nitrogen also exhibits \(+1\), \(+2\) and \(+4\) when it reacts with oxygen.

Anomalous properties of nitrogen

  • (i) Hydrides: the stability of the hydrides decreases from \(\mathrm{NH_3}\) to \(\mathrm{BiH_3}\) (seen from the bond dissociation enthalpies), so the reducing character of the hydrides increases. Basicity decreases in the order \(\mathrm{NH_3\gt PH_3\gt AsH_3\gt SbH_3\ge BiH_3}\).
  • (ii) Oxides: the oxide of the element in the higher oxidation state is more acidic than that of the lower oxidation state, and acidic character decreases down the group. The \(\mathrm{E_2O_3}\) oxides of N and P are purely acidic, those of As and Sb amphoteric, and that of Bi predominantly basic.
  • (iii) Halides: nitrogen does not form a pentahalide because of the non-availability of d orbitals in its valence shell. Pentahalides are more covalent than trihalides. Halides hydrolyse in water forming oxyacids or oxychlorides:\[\mathrm{PCl_3+3H_2O\to H_3PO_3+3HCl}\]\[\mathrm{SbCl_3+H_2O\to SbOCl\downarrow\,(\text{orange})+2HCl}\]\[\mathrm{BiCl_3+H_2O\to BiOCl\downarrow\,(\text{white})+2HCl}\]
  • (iv) Binary compounds with metals show the \(-3\) oxidation state, e.g. \(\mathrm{Ca_3N_2}\) (calcium nitride), \(\mathrm{Ca_3P_2}\) (calcium phosphide) and \(\mathrm{Na_3As}\) (sodium arsenide).

Nitrogen and its compounds

  • Ammonia: Haber process \(\mathrm{N_2+3H_2\rightleftharpoons2NH_3}\) (Fe catalyst, Mo promoter, about 200 atm, 700 K); lab: \(\mathrm{2NH_4Cl+Ca(OH)_2\to CaCl_2+2NH_3\uparrow+2H_2O}\). It is a Lewis base: \(\mathrm{Cu^{2+}+4NH_3\to[Cu(NH_3)_4]^{2+}}\) (deep blue).
  • Nitric acid (Ostwald process):\[\mathrm{4NH_3+5O_2\xrightarrow{Pt/Rh,\ 500\,K,\ 9\,bar}4NO+6H_2O}\qquad\mathrm{2NO+O_2\to2NO_2}\qquad\mathrm{3NO_2+H_2O\to2HNO_3+NO}\]
  • Reaction with metals:\[\mathrm{Cu+4HNO_3(\text{conc.})\to Cu(NO_3)_2+2NO_2+2H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{3Cu+8HNO_3(\text{dil.})\to3Cu(NO_3)_2+2NO+4H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{4Zn+10HNO_3(\text{dil.})\to4Zn(NO_3)_2+N_2O+5H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{4Zn+10HNO_3(\text{very dil.})\to4Zn(NO_3)_2+NH_4NO_3+3H_2O}\]Cr and Al are rendered passive by conc. \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\).

Phosphorus and its compounds

  • White phosphorus is \(\mathrm{P_4}\) (tetrahedral, six P–P bonds, \(60^\circ\) strain), very reactive and glows in the dark; it is stored under water.
  • Phosphine: \(\mathrm{Ca_3P_2+6H_2O\to3Ca(OH)_2+2PH_3}\); \(\mathrm{P_4+3NaOH+3H_2O\to PH_3+3NaH_2PO_2}\); \(\mathrm{PH_3+HI\to PH_4I}\).
  • Chlorides: \(\mathrm{P_4+6Cl_2\to4PCl_3}\); \(\mathrm{P_4+10Cl_2\to4PCl_5}\); \(\mathrm{PCl_3+Cl_2\rightleftharpoons PCl_5}\). Solid \(\mathrm{PCl_5}\) is ionic, \(\mathrm{[PCl_4]^+[PCl_6]^-}\). Hydrolysis: \(\mathrm{PCl_5+H_2O\to POCl_3+2HCl}\), then \(\mathrm{POCl_3+3H_2O\to H_3PO_4+3HCl}\).
  • Oxides: \(\mathrm{P_4+3O_2\to P_4O_6}\) (limited air); \(\mathrm{P_4+5O_2\to P_4O_{10}}\) (excess air).

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a reaction scheme.

Allotropes of phosphorus

  • When white phosphorus is heated in an atmosphere of \(\mathrm{CO_2}\) or coal gas at 573 K, red phosphorus is produced.
  • \(\alpha\)-black phosphorus is formed when red phosphorus is heated in a sealed tube at 803 K.
  • \(\beta\)-black phosphorus is prepared by heating white phosphorus at 473 K under high pressure.

Order of thermodynamic stability:

\[\text{black}\gt\text{red}\gt\text{white}\]

Group 16 (oxygen family): general properties

Electronic configuration \(ns^2np^4\).

  • Atomic and ionic radii increase down the group because of the increase in number of shells. The oxygen atom is exceptionally small.
  • Physical properties: oxygen and sulphur are non-metals, selenium and tellurium are metalloids, polonium is a metal. Polonium is radioactive and short lived (half-life 13.8 days). Melting and boiling points increase with atomic number down the group.
  • Catenation: the tendency decreases down the group; it is prominent in sulphur (\(\mathrm{S_8}\)). The S–S bond is important in biological systems and is found in some proteins and enzymes such as cysteine.
  • Oxidation states: \(+2\), \(+4\), \(+6\), of which \(+4\) and \(+6\) are more common.

Anomalous behaviour of oxygen

It is due to its small size and high electronegativity. The absence of d orbitals in oxygen limits its covalency to four.

  • (i) Hydrides: acidic character increases from \(\mathrm{H_2O}\) to \(\mathrm{H_2Te}\), which follows from the decrease in H–E bond dissociation enthalpy down the group. For the same reason the thermal stability of the hydrides decreases from \(\mathrm{H_2O}\) to \(\mathrm{H_2Po}\). All the hydrides except water are reducing, and this property increases from \(\mathrm{H_2S}\) to \(\mathrm{H_2Te}\).
  • (ii) Dioxides: the reducing property decreases from \(\mathrm{SO_2}\) to \(\mathrm{TeO_2}\); \(\mathrm{SO_2}\) is reducing while \(\mathrm{TeO_2}\) is an oxidising agent. Oxides are generally acidic.
  • (iii) Halides: stabilities decrease in the order \(\mathrm{F\gt Cl\gt Br\gt I}\). \(\mathrm{SF_6}\) is exceptionally stable for steric reasons. The well-known monohalides are dimeric, e.g. \(\mathrm{S_2F_2}\), \(\mathrm{S_2Cl_2}\), \(\mathrm{S_2Br_2}\), \(\mathrm{Se_2Cl_2}\), \(\mathrm{Se_2Br_2}\); these undergo disproportionation:\[\mathrm{2Se_2Cl_2\to SeCl_4+3Se}\]

Oxygen and its compounds

  • Preparation of \(\mathrm{O_2}\): \(\mathrm{2KClO_3\xrightarrow{MnO_2,\ \Delta}2KCl+3O_2}\); \(\mathrm{2H_2O_2\to2H_2O+O_2}\); \(\mathrm{2HgO\xrightarrow{\Delta}2Hg+O_2}\).
  • Oxides: acidic (\(\mathrm{SO_2}\), \(\mathrm{CO_2}\), \(\mathrm{P_4O_{10}}\)), basic (\(\mathrm{Na_2O}\), \(\mathrm{CaO}\)), amphoteric (\(\mathrm{Al_2O_3}\), \(\mathrm{ZnO}\)) and neutral (\(\mathrm{CO}\), \(\mathrm{N_2O}\), \(\mathrm{NO}\)).
  • Ozone: \(\mathrm{3O_2\rightleftharpoons2O_3}\) (silent electric discharge, \(\Delta H=+142\ \mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\)); bent molecule. It is a strong oxidising agent: \(\mathrm{O_3\to O_2+[O]}\); \(\mathrm{2KI+H_2O+O_3\to2KOH+I_2+O_2}\); \(\mathrm{PbS+4O_3\to PbSO_4+4O_2}\).
  • Hydrogen peroxide: \(\mathrm{BaO_2+H_2SO_4\to BaSO_4+H_2O_2}\); it acts as both an oxidising and a reducing agent in acidic and in basic media.

Sulphur dioxide and sulphuric acid

  • \(\mathrm{S+O_2\to SO_2}\); \(\mathrm{4FeS_2+11O_2\to2Fe_2O_3+8SO_2}\). \(\mathrm{SO_2}\) is a reducing agent: \(\mathrm{SO_2+Cl_2\to SO_2Cl_2}\); it decolourises acidified \(\mathrm{KMnO_4}\) and turns acidified \(\mathrm{K_2Cr_2O_7}\) green.

Group 17 (halogen family): general properties

Fluorine, chlorine, bromine, iodine and astatine. Electronic configuration \(ns^2np^5\).

  • Atomic and ionic radii: the halogens have the smallest atomic radii in their respective periods because of the maximum effective nuclear charge.
  • Physical properties: fluorine and chlorine are gases, bromine is a liquid, iodine is a solid. Melting and boiling points increase steadily with atomic number. The X–X bond dissociation enthalpies from chlorine onwards show the expected trend:\[\mathrm{Cl-Cl\gt Br-Br\gt F-F\gt I-I}\]
  • Oxidation states: all the halogens exhibit \(-1\). Chlorine, bromine and iodine also exhibit \(+1\), \(+3\), \(+5\) and \(+7\).

Chemical reactivity of the halogens

\[\mathrm{2F_2(g)+2H_2O(\ell)\to4H^+(aq)+4F^-(aq)+O_2(g)}\]\[\mathrm{X_2(g)+H_2O(\ell)\to HX(aq)+HOX(aq)}\qquad(\mathrm{X=Cl\ or\ Br})\]\[\mathrm{4I^-(aq)+4H^+(aq)+O_2(g)\to2I_2(s)+2H_2O(\ell)}\]

Chlorine, hydrogen halides and oxoacids

  • Chlorine: \(\mathrm{MnO_2+4HCl\to MnCl_2+Cl_2+2H_2O}\); \(\mathrm{2KMnO_4+16HCl\to2KCl+2MnCl_2+8H_2O+5Cl_2}\); Deacon: \(\mathrm{4HCl+O_2\xrightarrow{CuCl_2,\ 723\,K}2Cl_2+2H_2O}\).
  • With alkali: \(\mathrm{Cl_2+2NaOH(\text{cold, dil.})\to NaCl+NaOCl+H_2O}\); \(\mathrm{3Cl_2+6NaOH(\text{hot, conc.})\to5NaCl+NaClO_3+3H_2O}\).
  • Hydrogen halides: \(\mathrm{NaCl+H_2SO_4\to NaHSO_4+HCl}\). Acid strength \(\mathrm{HF\lt HCl\lt HBr\lt HI}\); thermal stability decreases from HF to HI; HF is unusually weak and has a high boiling point because of hydrogen bonding.
  • Oxoacids: \(\mathrm{HOX}\) (hypohalous), \(\mathrm{HXO_2}\), \(\mathrm{HXO_3}\), \(\mathrm{HXO_4}\). For chlorine acid strength increases \(\mathrm{HOCl\lt HClO_2\lt HClO_3\lt HClO_4}\).

TypeExamplesStructure (hybridisation)
\(\mathrm{XX'}\)\(\mathrm{ClF}\), \(\mathrm{BrF}\), \(\mathrm{IF}\), \(\mathrm{BrCl}\), \(\mathrm{ICl}\), \(\mathrm{IBr}\)Linear
\(\mathrm{XX'_3}\)\(\mathrm{ClF_3}\), \(\mathrm{BrF_3}\), \(\mathrm{IF_3}\)Bent T-shaped (\(sp^3d\))

\(\mathrm{XX'_7}\)\(\mathrm{IF_7}\)Pentagonal bipyramidal (\(sp^3d^3\))

Interhalogens are more reactive than the halogens (except \(\mathrm{F_2}\)) because the X–X' bond is weaker than the X–X bond. They hydrolyse to give halide and oxohalide ions.

Group 18 (zero group): general properties

Helium, neon, argon, krypton, xenon and radon. The most abundant element in air is Ar; order of abundance in air: \(\mathrm{Ar\gt Ne\gt Kr\gt He\gt Xe}\). Electronic configuration \(ns^2np^6\).

  • Atomic radii increase down the group with increase in atomic number.
  • Physical properties: all the noble gases are monoatomic, colourless and tasteless, and sparingly soluble in water. They have very low melting and boiling points because the only interatomic interaction is weak dispersion forces.

Chemical properties of the noble gases

In general, noble gases are least reactive. Their inertness is attributed to:

  • (i) except helium (\(1s^2\)), they have a completely filled \(ns^2np^6\) valence shell;
  • (ii) they have high ionisation enthalpy and more positive electron gain enthalpy.

All attempts to force them to react were unsuccessful for years. In March 1962, Neil Bartlett (University of British Columbia) observed a reaction of a noble gas. He first prepared a red compound formulated as \(\mathrm{O_2^+PtF_6^-}\). He then realised that the first ionisation enthalpy of molecular oxygen (\(1175\ \mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\)) is almost identical to that of xenon (\(1170\ \mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\)), and prepared the same type of compound, \(\mathrm{Xe^+PtF_6^-}\), by mixing \(\mathrm{PtF_6}\) and xenon. After this a number of xenon compounds, mainly with the most electronegative elements fluorine and oxygen, have been synthesised.

Liquid helium and clathrate compounds

  • Compressed and liquefied helium forms \(\mathrm{He(I)}\) at 4.2 K, a normal liquid. Cooled further to 2.2 K it gives \(\mathrm{He(II)}\), a superfluid: it climbs the walls of its container and flows out, with very high thermal conductivity and very low viscosity.
  • Clathrate compounds: during the formation of ice, Xe atoms are trapped in the cavities (cages) formed by the water molecules in the crystal structure of ice. The compounds so obtained are clathrates. Clathrates provide a convenient way of storing radioactive isotopes of Kr and Xe produced in nuclear reactors.

Xenon compounds

CompoundPreparationShape (hybridisation)
\(\mathrm{XeF_2}\)\(\mathrm{Xe+F_2\xrightarrow{673\,K,\ 1\,bar}XeF_2}\) (Xe in excess)Linear (\(sp^3d\))

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Pseudohalogens and interhalogens

  • Pseudohalogens: \(\mathrm{(CN)_2,(SCN)_2}\) and ions \(\mathrm{CN^-,SCN^-,OCN^-,N_3^-}\); form halogen-like acids (HCN), insoluble silver salts, and add to alkenes. Cyanogen \(\mathrm{(CN)_2}\) is linear, \(\mathrm{N{\equiv}C{-}C{\equiv}N}\).
  • Interhalogens \(\mathrm{XX'_n}\) (n = 1, 3, 5, 7): \(\mathrm{ClF,BrF_3,IF_5,IF_7}\); larger halogen is central; more reactive than \(\mathrm{X_2}\) (except F₂); structures: \(\mathrm{XX'_3}\) T-shaped, \(\mathrm{XX'_5}\) square pyramidal, \(\mathrm{IF_7}\) pentagonal bipyramidal.
  • Isoelectronic species (same electron count): \(\mathrm{N_2,CO,CN^-,NO^+}\) (bond order 3); \(\mathrm{CO_2,N_2O,NO_2^+,N_3^-}\) (linear).
17
Inorganic · Elements & compounds

d-Block Elements

General electronic configuration

\[(n-1)\,d^{\,1-10}\,ns^{\,0-2}\]

where \(n\) is the outermost shell.

Metallic character

Nearly all the transition elements display typical metallic properties such as high tensile strength, ductility, malleability, high thermal and electrical conductivity and metallic lustre.

With the exceptions of Zn, Cd, Hg and Mn, they have one or more typical metallic structures at normal temperatures.

The transition elements (with the exception of Zn, Cd and Hg) are very hard and have low volatility.

Melting and boiling points

The melting and boiling points of the transition series elements are generally very high.

Density

The atomic volumes of the transition elements are low compared with the elements of group 1 and 2. This is because the increased nuclear charge is poorly screened, so the densities of the transition metals are high.

Oxidation states

Most transition elements show variable oxidation states: the inner \((n-1)d\) electrons participate in addition to the outer \(ns\) electrons, because the energies of the \(ns\) and \((n-1)d\) subshells are nearly the same.

Different oxidation states of the first transition series

ElementOuter electronic configurationOxidation states

Ti\(3d^{2}4s^{2}\)+2, +3, +4
V\(3d^{3}4s^{2}\)+2, +3, +4, +5
Cr\(3d^{5}4s^{1}\)+2, +3, (+4), (+5), +6
Mn\(3d^{5}4s^{2}\)+2, +3, +4, (+5), +6, +7

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Standard electrode potentials

The value of the ionisation enthalpies gives information regarding the thermodynamic stability of the transition metal compounds in different oxidation states. The smaller the ionisation enthalpy of the metal, the more stable is its compound.

In addition to ionisation enthalpy, other factors such as enthalpy of sublimation and hydration enthalpy determine the stability of a particular oxidation state in solution. The overall energy change is

\[\Delta H=\Delta_{\text{sub}}H+\mathrm{IE}+\Delta_{\text{hyd}}H\]
  • The smaller the value of the total energy change for a particular oxidation state in aqueous solution, the greater is the stability of that oxidation state.
  • The electrode potentials are a measure of the total energy change.
  • Qualitatively, the stability of the transition metal ions in different oxidation states can be determined on the basis of electrode potential data: the lower the electrode potential, i.e. the more negative the standard reduction potential of the electrode, the more stable is the oxidation state of the transition metal in aqueous solution.

Ti4696611310−1866−1.63
V5156481370−1895−1.18
