\(\Delta_aH^\circ\) = enthalpy of atomisation; \(\Delta_iH_1^\circ,\ \Delta_iH_2^\circ\) = first and second ionisation enthalpies; \(\Delta_{\text{hyd}}H^\circ\) = hydration enthalpy.

Formation of coloured ions

Most of the compounds of transition metals are coloured in the solid form or in solution. The colour of the compounds of transition metals may be attributed to the presence of an incomplete \((n-1)d\) subshell.

The excess of the other colours constituting white light are transmitted and the compound appears coloured. The observed colour of a substance is always the complementary colour of the colour which is absorbed by the substance.

Magnetic properties

  • Paramagnetic substances are attracted by a magnetic field.
  • Diamagnetic substances are repelled by a magnetic field.

The ‘spin only’ magnetic moment can be calculated from the relation

\[\mu=\sqrt{n(n+2)}\ \text{B.M.}\]

where \(n\) is the number of unpaired electrons and \(\mu\) is the magnetic moment in Bohr magneton (BM) units.

The paramagnetism first increases in any transition series and then decreases. The maximum paramagnetism is observed around the middle of the series (as it contains the maximum number of unpaired electrons).

Formation of interstitial compounds

Transition metals form interstitial compounds with elements such as hydrogen, boron, carbon and nitrogen.

Catalytic properties

Many transition metals and their compounds act as good catalysts for various reactions. The use of Fe, Co, Ni, V, Cr, Mn, Pt, etc. is very common.

  1. The catalytic property of transition metals is due to their tendency to form reaction intermediates with suitable reactants. These intermediates give reaction paths of lower activation energy and, therefore, increase the rate of the reaction.
  2. In some cases, the transition metal catalysts provide a suitable large surface area for the adsorption of the reactants. This increases the concentration of the reactants at the catalyst surface and also weakens the bonds in the reactant molecules. Consequently, the activation energy gets lowered.
  3. In some cases, the transition metal ions can change their oxidation states and become more effective as catalysts.

Alloy formation

Alloys are hard, have high melting points and are more resistant to corrosion than the parent metals.

Potassium permanganate, KMnO4: preparation

From pyrolusite ore (\(\mathrm{MnO_2}\)).

  1. Fuse \(\mathrm{MnO_2}\) with alkali in the presence of air or an oxidising agent such as \(\mathrm{KNO_3}\), giving green potassium manganate:\[\mathrm{2MnO_2+4KOH+O_2\xrightarrow{\Delta}2K_2MnO_4+2H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{MnO_2+2KOH+KNO_3\xrightarrow{\Delta}K_2MnO_4+KNO_2+H_2O}\]
  2. Oxidise the manganate (green) to permanganate (purple), either electrolytically in alkaline solution (anode reaction \(\mathrm{MnO_4^{2-}\to MnO_4^-+e^-}\)) or chemically:\[\mathrm{2K_2MnO_4+Cl_2\to2KMnO_4+2KCl}\]\[\mathrm{2MnO_4^{2-}+O_3+H_2O\to2MnO_4^-+2OH^-+O_2}\]or by making the solution acidic (disproportionation):\[\mathrm{3MnO_4^{2-}+4H^+\to2MnO_4^-+MnO_2+2H_2O}\]

From \(\mathrm{Mn^{2+}}\) salts (oxidation to permanganate):

\[\mathrm{2Mn^{2+}+5S_2O_8^{2-}+8H_2O\to2MnO_4^-+10SO_4^{2-}+16H^+}\]\[\mathrm{2Mn^{2+}+5PbO_2+4H^+\to2MnO_4^-+5Pb^{2+}+2H_2O}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

KMnO4: properties and structure

  • Dark purple (almost black) crystalline solid, moderately soluble in water giving an intense purple solution.
  • \(\mathrm{MnO_4^-}\) is tetrahedral (\(sp^3\)); Mn is in the \(+7\) state (\(d^0\)), so the ion is diamagnetic and its intense colour is due to charge transfer (\(\mathrm{O^{2-}\to Mn^{7+}}\)), not \(d\)–\(d\) transitions.
  • The manganate ion \(\mathrm{MnO_4^{2-}}\) is dark green, tetrahedral and paramagnetic (\(d^1\), one unpaired electron, \(\mu=1.73\) BM).

Heating:

\[\mathrm{2KMnO_4\xrightarrow{513\ K}K_2MnO_4+MnO_2+O_2\uparrow}\]

Cold concentrated \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) gives explosive green oily \(\mathrm{Mn_2O_7}\):

\[\mathrm{2KMnO_4+H_2SO_4\to Mn_2O_7+K_2SO_4+H_2O}\qquad\mathrm{2Mn_2O_7\to4MnO_2+3O_2}\]

Uses: strong oxidant in volumetric analysis (self-indicator: faint permanent pink colour at the end point), Baeyer's reagent (cold, dilute, alkaline \(\mathrm{KMnO_4}\), test for \(\mathrm{C{=}C}\) unsaturation), disinfectant and bleaching agent.

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Neutral or weakly alkaline\(\mathrm{MnO_4^-+2H_2O+3e^-\to MnO_2+4OH^-}\)\(\mathrm{MnO_2}\) (brown precipitate)3 (\(M/3\))
Strongly alkaline\(\mathrm{MnO_4^-+e^-\to MnO_4^{2-}}\)\(\mathrm{MnO_4^{2-}}\) (green)1 (\(M\))

Dilute \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) is used for acidification: \(\mathrm{HCl}\) is itself oxidised to \(\mathrm{Cl_2}\) and \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\) is an oxidant. Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Potassium dichromate, K2Cr2O7: preparation

From chromite ore (\(\mathrm{FeCr_2O_4}\))

  1. Fuse the finely powdered ore with sodium carbonate in excess air, giving yellow sodium chromate:\[\mathrm{4FeCr_2O_4+8Na_2CO_3+7O_2\to8Na_2CrO_4+2Fe_2O_3+8CO_2\uparrow}\]
  2. Extract with water and acidify with sulphuric acid, converting chromate to dichromate (orange):\[\mathrm{2Na_2CrO_4+H_2SO_4\to Na_2Cr_2O_7+Na_2SO_4+H_2O}\]
  3. Treat the sodium dichromate solution with \(\mathrm{KCl}\); the less soluble potassium dichromate crystallises:\[\mathrm{Na_2Cr_2O_7+2KCl\to K_2Cr_2O_7+2NaCl}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Chromate–dichromate interconversion

\[\mathrm{2CrO_4^{2-}+2H^+\rightleftharpoons Cr_2O_7^{2-}+H_2O}\]
  • In acidic solution (low pH) the equilibrium shifts right: orange dichromate \(\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}}\).
  • In alkaline solution (high pH) it shifts left: yellow chromate \(\mathrm{CrO_4^{2-}}\):\[\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+2OH^-\to2CrO_4^{2-}+H_2O}\]
  • No change in oxidation state (Cr is \(+6\) in both): the change is only acid–base (condensation/hydrolysis).
  • Structure: \(\mathrm{CrO_4^{2-}}\) is tetrahedral; \(\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}}\) is two \(\mathrm{CrO_4}\) tetrahedra sharing one corner oxygen, with a Cr–O–Cr angle of \(126^\circ\).
  • Cr(VI) is \(d^0\): both ions are diamagnetic and their colour arises from charge transfer.

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

K2Cr2O7 as an oxidant in acidic medium

\[\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+14H^++6e^-\to2Cr^{3+}+7H_2O}\qquad E^\circ=+1.33\ \mathrm{V}\]

Orange \(\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}}\) turns green (\(\mathrm{Cr^{3+}}\)); \(n\)-factor \(=6\), equivalent weight \(=M/6\).

ReductantIonic equation
\(\mathrm{Fe^{2+}}\to\mathrm{Fe^{3+}}\)\(\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+6Fe^{2+}+14H^+\to2Cr^{3+}+6Fe^{3+}+7H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}\to\mathrm{Sn^{4+}}\)\(\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+3Sn^{2+}+14H^+\to2Cr^{3+}+3Sn^{4+}+7H_2O}\)
Iodide\(\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+6I^-+14H^+\to2Cr^{3+}+3I_2+7H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{H_2S}\)\(\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+3H_2S+8H^+\to2Cr^{3+}+3S\downarrow+7H_2O}\)
\(\mathrm{SO_2}\)\(\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+2H^++3SO_2\to2Cr^{3+}+3SO_4^{2-}+H_2O}\)
Nitrite\(\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+3NO_2^-+8H^+\to2Cr^{3+}+3NO_3^-+4H_2O}\)

Hot conc. \(\mathrm{HCl}\)\(\mathrm{K_2Cr_2O_7+14HCl\to2KCl+2CrCl_3+7H_2O+3Cl_2\uparrow}\)

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

K2Cr2O7: other properties and uses

  • Orange-red crystalline solid; less hygroscopic than \(\mathrm{Na_2Cr_2O_7}\), so it is used as a primary standard.
  • On strong heating:\[\mathrm{4K_2Cr_2O_7\xrightarrow{\Delta}4K_2CrO_4+2Cr_2O_3+3O_2\uparrow}\]
  • Cold conc. \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) gives red crystals of chromium trioxide:\[\mathrm{K_2Cr_2O_7+2H_2SO_4\to2CrO_3+2KHSO_4+H_2O}\]and hot conc. \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) liberates oxygen:\[\mathrm{2K_2Cr_2O_7+8H_2SO_4\to2K_2SO_4+2Cr_2(SO_4)_3+8H_2O+3O_2\uparrow}\]
  • With \(\mathrm{KCl}\) and conc. \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\): deep red vapours of chromyl chloride \(\mathrm{CrO_2Cl_2}\) (chromyl chloride test for chloride).
  • With acidified \(\mathrm{H_2O_2}\): blue peroxide, extracted into ether:\[\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+4H_2O_2+2H^+\to2CrO_5+5H_2O}\]
  • Uses: volumetric analysis, leather tanning, dyeing and printing, chrome plating, photography, and as chromic acid cleaning mixture with conc. \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\).

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

f-block: lanthanoids and actinoids

Lanthanoids (4f)Actinoids (5f)
Configuration\([\mathrm{Xe}]4f^{1\text{–}14}5d^{0\text{–}1}6s^2\)\([\mathrm{Rn}]5f^{1\text{–}14}6d^{0\text{–}1}7s^2\)
Oxidation states+3 common; Ce +4, Eu +2, Yb +2, Tb +4+3 common; wider range, +4 to +7 (Np, Pu +7)
RadioactivityOnly PmAll radioactive
Magnetism\(\mu=\sqrt{n(n+2)}\) fails (spin–orbit coupling)Complex
  • Lanthanoid contraction: steady decrease in atomic and \(\mathrm{M^{3+}}\) radii from La to Lu because 4f electrons shield poorly. Consequences: Zr and Hf (and Nb–Ta, Mo–W) have nearly equal radii and are hard to separate; basicity of \(\mathrm{Ln(OH)_3}\) decreases from La to Lu; Ln3+ complexation strength increases.
  • \(\mathrm{Ce^{4+}}\) is a strong oxidant (\(E^\circ=+1.74\) V) used in volumetry; \(\mathrm{Eu^{2+},Yb^{2+}}\) are reductants. Misch metal (about 95% Ln, 5% Fe) is used in Mg alloys and lighter flints.
  • Actinoid contraction is larger than lanthanoid contraction (5f shields even more poorly).

Analysis of anions (acidic radicals): carbonate

(a) Dilute \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) / dilute HCl group. Carbonate ion, \(\mathrm{CO_3^{2-}}\).

Dilute \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) test: a colourless, odourless gas is evolved with brisk effervescence.

\[\mathrm{CaCO_3+H_2SO_4\to CaSO_4+H_2O+CO_2\uparrow}\]

Lime water / baryta water \(\mathrm{Ba(OH)_2}\) test:

\[\mathrm{CO_2+Ca(OH)_2\to CaCO_3\downarrow\,(\text{milky})+H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{CaCO_3+CO_2+H_2O\to Ca(HCO_3)_2}\ (\text{soluble})\qquad\mathrm{Ca(HCO_3)_2\xrightarrow{\Delta}CaCO_3\downarrow+H_2O+CO_2}\]

Sulphite ion, \(\mathrm{SO_3^{2-}}\)

Dilute \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) test: \(\mathrm{SO_2}\) is evolved, with the suffocating odour of burning sulphur.

\[\mathrm{CaSO_3+H_2SO_4\to CaSO_4+H_2O+SO_2\uparrow}\]

Acidified potassium dichromate test: filter paper dipped in acidified \(\mathrm{K_2Cr_2O_7}\) turns green.

\[\mathrm{Cr_2O_7^{2-}+2H^++3SO_2\to2Cr^{3+}\,(\text{green})+3SO_4^{2-}+H_2O}\]

Barium chloride / strontium chloride solution:

\[\mathrm{SO_3^{2-}+Ba^{2+}/Sr^{2+}\to BaSO_3/SrSO_3\downarrow\,(\text{white})}\]

The white precipitate dissolves in dilute HCl: \(\mathrm{BaSO_3+2H^+\to Ba^{2+}+SO_2\uparrow+H_2O}\).

Sulphide ion, \(\mathrm{S^{2-}}\)

Dilute \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) test: a pungent gas smelling like rotten eggs is obtained: \(\mathrm{S^{2-}+2H^+\to H_2S\uparrow}\).

Lead acetate test:

\[\mathrm{(CH_3COO)_2Pb+H_2S\to PbS\downarrow\,(\text{black})+2CH_3COOH}\]

Sodium nitroprusside test: purple coloration.

\[\mathrm{S^{2-}+[Fe(CN)_5(NO)]^{2-}\to[Fe(CN)_5NOS]^{4-}}\ (\text{violet})\]

Cadmium carbonate suspension / cadmium acetate solution:

\[\mathrm{Na_2S+CdCO_3\to CdS\downarrow\,(\text{yellow})+Na_2CO_3}\]

Nitrite ion, \(\mathrm{NO_2^-}\)

Dilute \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) test:

\[\mathrm{NO_2^-+H^+\to HNO_2}\qquad\mathrm{2HNO_2\to H_2O+N_2O_3}\]\[\mathrm{3HNO_2\to HNO_3+2NO+H_2O}\qquad\mathrm{2NO+O_2\to2NO_2}\ (\text{brown fumes})\]

Starch iodide test:

\[\mathrm{2NO_2^-+3I^-+4CH_3COOH\to I_3^-+2NO+4CH_3COO^-+2H_2O}\]\[\text{Starch}+\mathrm{I_3^-}\to\text{blue (starch–iodine adsorption complex)}\]

Acetate ion, \(\mathrm{CH_3COO^-}\)

Dilute \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) test: vinegar-like smell.

\[\mathrm{(CH_3COO)_2Ca+H_2SO_4\to2CH_3COOH+CaSO_4}\]

Neutral ferric chloride test:

\[\mathrm{6CH_3COO^-+3Fe^{3+}+2H_2O\to[Fe_3(OH)_2(CH_3COO)_6]^+\,(\text{deep red / blood red})+2H^+}\]

On boiling, the red complex is hydrolysed to a brownish-red precipitate:

\[\mathrm{[Fe_3(OH)_2(CH_3COO)_6]^++4H_2O\xrightarrow{\text{boil}}3Fe(OH)_2CH_3COO\downarrow\,(\text{brownish red})+3CH_3COOH+H^+}\]

(b) Conc. \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) group: chloride ion, \(\mathrm{Cl^-}\)

Concentrated \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) test: a colourless, pungent gas (HCl) is evolved; \(\mathrm{NH_4OH}\) brought near gives white fumes.

\[\mathrm{Cl^-+H_2SO_4\to HCl\uparrow+HSO_4^-}\qquad\mathrm{NH_4OH+HCl\to NH_4Cl\,(\text{white fumes})+H_2O}\]

Silver nitrate test: \(\mathrm{Cl^-+Ag^+\to AgCl\downarrow}\) (white). The precipitate is soluble in aqueous ammonia and reappears with \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\):

\[\mathrm{AgCl+2NH_4OH\to[Ag(NH_3)_2]Cl+2H_2O}\qquad\mathrm{[Ag(NH_3)_2]Cl+2H^+\to AgCl\downarrow+2NH_4^+}\]

Chromyl chloride test:

\[\mathrm{4Cl^-+Cr_2O_7^{2-}+6H^+(\text{conc.})\to2CrO_2Cl_2\,(\text{deep red vapours})+3H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{CrO_2Cl_2+4OH^-\to CrO_4^{2-}+2Cl^-+2H_2O}\qquad\mathrm{CrO_4^{2-}+Pb^{2+}\to PbCrO_4\downarrow\,(\text{yellow})}\]

Bromide ion, \(\mathrm{Br^-}\)

Concentrated \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) test:

\[\mathrm{2NaBr+H_2SO_4\to Na_2SO_4+2HBr}\qquad\mathrm{2HBr+H_2SO_4\to Br_2\,(\text{reddish brown})+2H_2O+SO_2\uparrow}\]

Silver nitrate test: \(\mathrm{NaBr+AgNO_3\to AgBr\downarrow\,(\text{pale yellow})+NaNO_3}\). The precipitate is partially soluble in dilute aqueous ammonia but readily dissolves in concentrated ammonia:

\[\mathrm{AgBr+2NH_4OH\to[Ag(NH_3)_2]Br+2H_2O}\]

Chlorine water test (organic layer test):

\[\mathrm{2Br^-+Cl_2\to2Cl^-+Br_2}\]

\(\mathrm{Br_2}\) dissolves in \(\mathrm{CHCl_3}\) / \(\mathrm{CCl_4}\) to give a reddish-brown colour in the organic layer.

Iodide ion, \(\mathrm{I^-}\)

Concentrated \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) test:

\[\mathrm{2NaI+H_2SO_4\to Na_2SO_4+2HI}\qquad\mathrm{2HI+H_2SO_4\to I_2\,(\text{pungent, dark violet})+2H_2O+SO_2\uparrow}\]

Starch paper test: iodides are readily oxidised in acid solution to free iodine, which gives a deep blue colour with starch.

\[\mathrm{3I^-+2NO_2^-+4H^+\to I_3^-+2NO\uparrow+2H_2O}\]

Silver nitrate test: \(\mathrm{I^-+Ag^+\to AgI\downarrow}\) (bright yellow); insoluble in dilute aqueous ammonia, partially soluble in concentrated ammonia.

Chlorine water test (organic layer test): \(\mathrm{2NaI+Cl_2\to2NaCl+I_2}\); \(\mathrm{I_2}\) dissolves in \(\mathrm{CHCl_3}\) to give a violet colour in the organic layer.

Nitrate ion, \(\mathrm{NO_3^-}\)

Concentrated \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) test: pungent, reddish-brown vapours are evolved.

\[\mathrm{4NO_3^-+2H_2SO_4\to4NO_2\uparrow+O_2+2SO_4^{2-}+2H_2O}\]

Bright copper turnings or paper pellets intensify the evolution of the reddish-brown gas:

\[\mathrm{2NO_3^-+4H_2SO_4+3Cu\to3Cu^{2+}+2NO\uparrow+4SO_4^{2-}+4H_2O}\qquad\mathrm{2NO+O_2\to2NO_2}\]\[\mathrm{C\,(\text{paper pellet})+4HNO_3\to CO_2+4NO_2+2H_2O}\]

Brown ring test:

\[\mathrm{2NO_3^-+4H_2SO_4+6Fe^{2+}\to6Fe^{3+}+2NO\uparrow+4SO_4^{2-}+4H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{Fe^{2+}+NO+5H_2O\to[Fe(H_2O)_5NO]^{2+}}\ (\text{brown ring})\]

(c) Miscellaneous group: sulphate ion, \(\mathrm{SO_4^{2-}}\)

Barium chloride test:

\[\mathrm{Na_2SO_4+BaCl_2\to BaSO_4\downarrow\,(\text{white})+2NaCl}\]

The white precipitate is insoluble in warm dilute \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\) as well as HCl, but moderately soluble in boiling concentrated hydrochloric acid.

Lead acetate test:

\[\mathrm{Na_2SO_4+(CH_3COO)_2Pb\to PbSO_4\downarrow\,(\text{white})+2CH_3COONa}\]

The white precipitate is soluble in excess hot ammonium acetate:

\[\mathrm{PbSO_4+2CH_3COONH_4\to(CH_3COO)_2Pb\,(\text{soluble})+(NH_4)_2SO_4}\]

Phosphate ion, \(\mathrm{PO_4^{3-}}\)

Ammonium molybdate test:

\[\mathrm{Na_2HPO_4(aq)+12(NH_4)_2MoO_4+23HNO_3\to(NH_4)_3PMo_{12}O_{40}\downarrow\,(\text{canary yellow})+2NaNO_3+21NH_4NO_3+12H_2O}\]

Analysis of cations: ammonium ion, \(\mathrm{NH_4^+}\)

\[\mathrm{2NH_3+Mn^{2+}+H_2O_2+H_2O\to MnO(OH)_2\downarrow\,(\text{brown})+2NH_4^+}\]

Nessler's reagent (alkaline solution of potassium tetraiodomercurate(II)):

\[\mathrm{NH_4^++2[HgI_4]^{2-}+4OH^-\to HgO\cdot Hg(NH_2)I\downarrow\,(\text{brown})+7I^-+3H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{3NH_4^++[Co(NO_2)_6]^{3-}\to(NH_4)_3[Co(NO_2)_6]\downarrow\,(\text{yellow})}\]\[\mathrm{2NH_4^++[PtCl_6]^{2-}\to(NH_4)_2[PtCl_6]\downarrow\,(\text{yellow})}\]\[\mathrm{NH_4^++HC_4H_4O_6^-\to NH_4HC_4H_4O_6\downarrow}\]

Cation groups: group reagents at a glance

Rebuilt summary — the original group schemes (pages 105–108) are flow charts. Group V and VI details below follow the surviving text.

Group I: confirmatory tests

  • \(\mathrm{Pb^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{PbCl_2}\) dissolves in hot water; \(\mathrm{Pb^{2+}+K_2CrO_4\to PbCrO_4\downarrow}\) (yellow); \(\mathrm{Pb^{2+}+2KI\to PbI_2\downarrow}\) (yellow, golden spangles on cooling).
  • \(\mathrm{Ag^+}\): \(\mathrm{AgCl}\) dissolves in \(\mathrm{NH_4OH}\) and is reprecipitated by \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\) (see chloride test above); with KI gives bright yellow \(\mathrm{AgI}\).
  • \(\mathrm{Hg_2^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{Hg_2Cl_2+2NH_4OH\to Hg(NH_2)Cl\downarrow+Hg\downarrow+NH_4Cl+2H_2O}\) (black).

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a flow chart.

Groups IIA and IIB: confirmatory tests

  • \(\mathrm{Hg^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{HgS}\) is black and insoluble in dilute \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\); dissolves in aqua regia.
  • \(\mathrm{Pb^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{PbS}\) black; \(\mathrm{PbSO_4}\) white (see sulphate test above); \(\mathrm{PbCrO_4}\) yellow.
  • \(\mathrm{Bi^{3+}}\): \(\mathrm{Bi_2S_3}\) is brown; \(\mathrm{Bi(OH)_3}\) with sodium stannite gives black metallic bismuth.
  • \(\mathrm{Cu^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{CuS}\) black; excess \(\mathrm{NH_4OH}\) gives deep blue \(\mathrm{[Cu(NH_3)_4]^{2+}}\); \(\mathrm{K_4[Fe(CN)_6]}\) gives a chocolate-brown precipitate.
  • \(\mathrm{Cd^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{CdS}\) is yellow; with excess \(\mathrm{NH_4OH}\) gives a colourless complex.
  • Group IIB sulphides (\(\mathrm{As_2S_3}\), \(\mathrm{Sb_2S_3}\), \(\mathrm{SnS_2}\)) dissolve in yellow ammonium sulphide, unlike group IIA.

Rebuilt summary — the original pages are flow charts.

Groups III and IV: confirmatory tests

  • \(\mathrm{Al^{3+}}\): \(\mathrm{Al(OH)_3}\) white gelatinous, soluble in excess NaOH; charcoal-cavity cobalt nitrate test gives blue (Thenard's blue).
  • \(\mathrm{Cr^{3+}}\): \(\mathrm{Cr(OH)_3}\) green; with NaOH / \(\mathrm{H_2O_2}\) gives yellow \(\mathrm{CrO_4^{2-}}\), which with lead acetate gives yellow \(\mathrm{PbCrO_4}\).
  • \(\mathrm{Fe^{3+}}\): \(\mathrm{Fe(OH)_3}\) reddish brown; \(\mathrm{K_4[Fe(CN)_6]}\) gives Prussian blue; KSCN gives blood-red colour.
  • \(\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{ZnS}\) white; \(\mathrm{K_4[Fe(CN)_6]}\) gives a white / bluish-white precipitate; cobalt nitrate gives green mass.
  • \(\mathrm{Mn^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{MnS}\) buff / flesh coloured; NaOH with \(\mathrm{H_2O_2}\) gives brown \(\mathrm{MnO(OH)_2}\) / \(\mathrm{MnO_2}\).
  • \(\mathrm{Ni^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{NiS}\) black; dimethylglyoxime in ammoniacal solution gives a rosy-red precipitate.
  • \(\mathrm{Co^{2+}}\): \(\mathrm{CoS}\) black; \(\mathrm{KNO_2}\) / acetic acid gives yellow \(\mathrm{K_3[Co(NO_2)_6]}\).

Rebuilt summary — the original pages are flow charts.

Group V: \(\mathrm{Ba^{2+},\ Sr^{2+},\ Ca^{2+}}\)

Group IV filtrate: boil off \(\mathrm{H_2S}\), then add \(\mathrm{(NH_4)_2CO_3}\) (aq), \(\mathrm{NH_4OH}\) and \(\mathrm{NH_4Cl}\) (s). White precipitate (\(\mathrm{BaCO_3}\), \(\mathrm{SrCO_3}\) or \(\mathrm{CaCO_3}\)); the filtrate is kept for group VI.

Dissolve the precipitate in \(\mathrm{CH_3COOH}\), divide into three parts and test in the sequence below.

PartReagentObservation
I\(\mathrm{K_2CrO_4}\)Yellow precipitate (\(\mathrm{BaCrO_4}\), insoluble in \(\mathrm{CH_3COOH}\))
II\(\mathrm{(NH_4)_2SO_4}\)White precipitate (\(\mathrm{SrSO_4}\))
III\(\mathrm{(NH_4)_2C_2O_4}\)White precipitate (\(\mathrm{CaC_2O_4}\))

Group VI: magnesium ion, \(\mathrm{Mg^{2+}}\)

\[\mathrm{Mg^{2+}+NH_3+HPO_4^{2-}\to Mg(NH_4)PO_4\downarrow\,(\text{white})}\]\[\mathrm{5Mg^{2+}+6CO_3^{2-}+7H_2O\to2MgCO_3\cdot Mg(OH)_2\cdot5H_2O\downarrow+2HCO_3^-}\]

Titan yellow (a water-soluble yellow dyestuff) is adsorbed by \(\mathrm{Mg(OH)_2}\), producing a deep red colour or precipitate.

  • If the suffix carbylamine is used, the carbon atom of \(\mathrm{-NC}\) is not counted.
  • If o-isonitrile is used, the carbon atom of \(\mathrm{-NC}\) is counted (e.g. \(\mathrm{CH_3-NC}\): acetoisonitrile, but methyl isocyanide / methyl carbylamine).

Parts of an IUPAC name

The IUPAC name of any organic compound consists of a maximum of five parts, in this sequence:

\[\text{Secondary prefix}+\text{Primary prefix}+\text{Word root}+\text{Primary suffix}+\text{Secondary suffix}\]

Word root, primary suffix and secondary suffix

CompoundWord rootPrimary suffixSecondary suffixIUPAC name
\(\mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH}\)Ethan(e)olEthanol
\(\mathrm{CH_3CH_2CH_2NH_2}\)Propan(e)aminePropanamine
\(\mathrm{CH_3CH_2CH_2COOH}\)Butan(e)oic acidButanoic acid
\(\mathrm{CH_3CH_2CN}\)Propan(e)nitrilePropanenitrile
\(\mathrm{CH_2{=}CHCHO}\)Propen(e)alPropenal
\(\mathrm{HC\equiv C-COOH}\)Propyn(e)oic acidPropynoic acid

Word root, primary prefix and secondary prefix

  1. Cyclohexane ring with \(\mathrm{-OH}\) on C-1 and \(\mathrm{-Br}\) on C-4: 4-Bromo (secondary prefix) + cyclo (primary prefix) + hex (word root) + an(e) (primary suffix) + 1-ol (secondary suffix) = 4-Bromocyclohexan-1-ol.
  2. Cyclopentane ring with \(\mathrm{-SO_3H}\) on C-1, together with iodo and methyl substituents: iodo, methyl (secondary prefixes) + cyclo (primary prefix) + pent (word root) + an(e) (primary suffix) + 1-sulphonic acid (secondary suffix).
  3. The position of the double bond is numbered 1, hence the name is 3-Methyl-3-propylhex-1-ene.

Organic chemistry

Structure Isomerism

Types of structural isomerism

IsomersCharacteristicsConditions
(1) Chain isomersDifferent size of main chain or side chainSame nature of locants
(2) Positional isomersDifferent position of locantsSame size of main chain and side chain, and same nature of locant
(3) Functional isomersDifferent nature of locantChain and positional isomerism are not considered
(4) MetamerismDifferent nature of alkyl groups along a polyvalent functional groupSame nature of functional group; chain and positional isomerism are ignored
(5) TautomerismDifferent position of hydrogen atomsThe two functional isomers remain in dynamic equilibrium with each other

Solved problem 1: naming and drawing

Q. Write the IUPAC name of the following compounds, or draw the structure for the name given.

  • (i) \(\mathrm{CH_3-CH_2-CH(OC_2H_5)-COOH}\)
  • (ii) 3-Bromocyclohexane-1-sulphonic acid (draw the structure)
  • (iii) A cyclopentane ring carrying three methyl groups (name it)
  • (iv) 3-Cyano-3-ethoxy-4-nitropentanoyl bromide (draw the structure)

Sol.

  • (i) 2-Ethoxybutanoic acid
  • (ii) Cyclohexane ring with \(\mathrm{-SO_3H}\) on C-1 and \(\mathrm{-Br}\) on C-3.
  • (iii) 1,1,2-Trimethylcyclopentane
  • (iv) \(\mathrm{BrOC-CH_2-C(CN)(OC_2H_5)-CH(NO_2)-CH_3}\)

Solved problem 2: drawing and naming

Q. Draw the structure of the following IUPAC names.

  • (i) The diyne \(\mathrm{HC\equiv C-CH(C_2H_5)-C\equiv CH}\) (name it)
  • (ii) 3-Methoxycarbonylpropanoic acid

Sol.

  • (i) 3-Ethylpenta-1,4-diyne
  • (ii) \(\mathrm{HOOC-CH_2-CH_2-COOCH_3}\)

Solved problem 3: dimethylcycloalkanes

Q. Find the total number of structural isomers of dimethylcyclopropane and dimethylcyclobutane respectively.

(A) 4, 6   (B) 3, 4   (C) 4, 5   (D) 2, 3

Ans. (D)

Sol. Dimethylcyclopropane (\(\mathrm{C_5H_{10}}\)): 1,1-dimethyl and 1,2-dimethyl, so 2 isomers. Dimethylcyclobutane (\(\mathrm{C_6H_{12}}\)): 1,1-dimethyl, 1,2-dimethyl and 1,3-dimethyl, so 3 isomers.

Solved problem 4: cycloalkanones of C5H8O

Q. How many structures of cycloalkanone are possible with molecular formula \(\mathrm{C_5H_8O}\)?

Ans. 6

Sol. The six cyclic ketones are:

  1. Cyclopentanone
  2. 2-Methylcyclobutanone
  3. 3-Methylcyclobutanone
  4. 2,2-Dimethylcyclopropanone
  5. 2,3-Dimethylcyclopropanone
  6. 2-Ethylcyclopropanone

Solved problem 5: cyclic isomers of C4H7Cl

Q. Find the total number of cyclic isomers of the compound X, \(\mathrm{C_4H_7Cl}\).

Ans. 4

Sol. Degree of unsaturation:

\[\text{DU}=C+1-\frac{H+X}{2}=5-\frac{8}{2}=1\]

so X is cyclic with one ring and no double bond. The cyclic isomers are:

  1. Chlorocyclobutane
  2. (Chloromethyl)cyclopropane
  3. 1-Chloro-1-methylcyclopropane
  4. 1-Chloro-2-methylcyclopropane

Total = 4.

22
Organic chemistry

General Organic Chemistry

Inductive effect

A C–C bond between two identical atoms is non-polar. In 1-chlorobutane the more electronegative Cl displaces the C–Cl bond pair towards itself, giving \(\delta^-\) on Cl and \(\delta^+\) on C1. Because C1 is now slightly positive it shifts the C1–C2 bond pair towards itself, so C2 acquires a smaller positive charge, then C3 – an induction of charge along the carbon chain.

\[\overset{\delta\delta\delta+}{\mathrm{C_4}}\ \to\ \overset{\delta\delta+}{\mathrm{C_3}}\ \to\ \overset{\delta+}{\mathrm{C_2}}\ \to\ \overset{\delta+}{\mathrm{C_1}}\ \to\ \overset{\delta-}{\mathrm{Cl}}\]

The arrow shows the electron-withdrawing nature of the \(\mathrm{-Cl}\) group.

Definition: the inductive effect is the permanent displacement of \(\sigma\)-bond pair electrons due to a dipole (polar bond).

Features of the inductive effect

  • Polarisation of one bond caused by the polarisation of an adjacent bond.
  • Also called the transmission effect.
  • It causes permanent polarisation in the molecule, hence it is a permanent effect.
  • The displacement of electrons arises from the difference in electronegativity of the two atoms in the covalent bond.
  • The electrons never leave their original atomic orbital.
  • Its magnitude decreases with distance and is almost negligible after the 3rd carbon atom.
  • It always operates through \(\sigma\) bonds; it does not involve \(\pi\)-bond electrons.

−I and +I groups

−I effect: a group that withdraws the electron cloud is a −I group. Decreasing −I strength:

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{-NR_3^+ \gt -NH_3^+ \gt -NO_2 \gt -SO_2R \gt -CN \gt -CHO \gt -COOH \gt -F}\\&\mathrm{\gt -Cl \gt -Br \gt -I \gt -OR \gt -OH \gt -C{\equiv}CH \gt -NH_2 \gt -C_6H_5 \gt -CH{=}CH_2 \gt -H}\end{aligned}\]

+I effect: a group that releases the electron cloud is a +I group. Decreasing +I strength:

\[\mathrm{-O^- \gt -COO^- \gt -C(CH_3)_3 \gt -CH(CH_3)_2 \gt -CH_2CH_3 \gt -CH_3 \gt -D \gt -H}\]

Hydrogen is the reference for both the +I and −I series; the inductive effect of hydrogen is assumed to be zero.

Inductive effect of −COOH and −COO⁻ on a carbon chain

Group on the chainNatureEffect on the carbon chain
(a)\(\mathrm{-COO^-}\)+I (electron donating)chain becomes partially negative (\(\delta^-\))
(b)\(\mathrm{-COOH}\)−I (electron withdrawing)chain becomes partially positive (\(\delta^+\))

Resonance

Resonance is the phenomenon in which two or more structures differing only in the position of electrons (identical positions of atoms) can be written for a particular species. All these possible structures are called resonating (canonical) structures.

  • Resonating structures are only hypothetical, but all of them contribute to the real structure, called the resonance hybrid.
  • The resonance hybrid is more stable than any single resonating structure.
  • The most stable resonating structure contributes the maximum to the resonance hybrid; a less stable resonating structure contributes the minimum.

Conjugation

A given atom or group is said to be in conjugation with an unsaturated system if:

  1. it is directly linked to one of the atoms of the multiple bond through a single bond, and
  2. it has a \(\pi\) bond, a positive charge, a negative charge, an odd electron or a lone pair of electrons.
TypeConjugation betweenExample
1\(\mathrm{C{=}C}\) and \(\mathrm{C{=}C}\)\(\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH{-}CH{=}CH_2}\)
2positive charge and \(\mathrm{C{=}C}\)\(\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH{-}\overset{+}{C}H_2}\)
3lone pair and \(\mathrm{C{=}C}\)\(\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH{-}\overset{\cdot\cdot}{Cl}{:}}\)
4odd electron and \(\mathrm{C{=}C}\)\(\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH{-}\dot{C}H_2 \leftrightarrow \dot{C}H_2{-}CH{=}CH_2}\)
5negative charge and \(\mathrm{C{=}C}\)\(\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH{-}\overset{-}{C}H_2}\)

Mesomeric (resonance) effect

The mesomeric effect is the permanent effect of \(\pi\)-electron shifting: from a multiple bond to an atom, from a multiple bond to a single bond, or from a lone pair to a single bond. It operates mainly in a conjugated system of double bonds, so it is also known as the conjugate effect.

  • +M effect: the group donates electrons to the conjugated system.
  • −M effect: the group withdraws electrons from the conjugated system.

+M effect

Relative order of +M groups (usually followed):

\[\mathrm{-O^- \gt -NH_2 \gt -NHR \gt -NR_2 \gt -OH \gt -OR \gt -NHCOR \gt -OCOR \gt -Ph \gt -F \gt -Cl \gt -Br \gt -I}\]

Example:

\[\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH{-}\overset{\cdot\cdot}{N}H_2 \leftrightarrow \overset{-}{C}H_2{-}CH{=}\overset{+}{N}H_2}\]

−M effect

Relative order of −M groups (usually followed):

\[\mathrm{-NO_2 \gt -CHO \gt C{=}O \gt -COOCOR \gt -COOR \gt -COOH \gt -CONH_2 \gt -COO^-}\]

\(\mathrm{C{=}O}\) in the order is the ketonic carbonyl group; \(\mathrm{-COOCOR}\) is the anhydride group.

Examples:

\[\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH{-}CH{=}O \leftrightarrow \overset{+}{C}H_2{-}CH{=}CH{-}O^-}\]\[\mathrm{H_2C{=}CH{-}C{\equiv}N \leftrightarrow \overset{+}{C}H_2{-}CH{=}C{=}\overset{-}{N}}\]

Hyperconjugation

Delocalisation of \(\sigma\) electrons into an adjacent p-orbital (or \(\pi\) system). Also known as \(\sigma\)–\(\pi\) conjugation or no-bond resonance. It takes place in alkenes, alkynes, carbocations, free radicals and the benzene nucleus.

Necessary condition: at least one hydrogen on a saturated carbon that is \(\alpha\) with respect to the alkene, alkyne, carbocation, free radical or benzene nucleus.

SpeciesHyperconjugation
Alkene (propene)\(\mathrm{H{-}CH_2{-}CH{=}CH_2 \leftrightarrow H^+\ \overset{}{C}H_2{=}CH{-}\overset{-}{C}H_2}\)
Carbocation (ethyl cation)\(\mathrm{H{-}CH_2{-}\overset{+}{C}H_2 \leftrightarrow H^+\ CH_2{=}CH_2}\)
Free radical (ethyl radical)\(\mathrm{H{-}CH_2{-}\dot{C}H_2 \leftrightarrow H\cdot\ CH_2{=}CH_2}\)

Aromatic character – Hückel's \((4n+2)\) rule

The following rules help to predict whether a compound is aromatic or non-aromatic:

  • Aromatic compounds are cyclic and planar.
  • Each atom in an aromatic ring is \(sp^2\) hybridised.
  • The cyclic \(\pi\) molecular orbital (formed by overlap of p-orbitals) must contain \((4n+2)\,\pi\) electrons, i.e. 2, 6, 10, 14, …, where \(n=0,1,2,3,\ldots\)

Aromatic compounds have a characteristic smell, extra stability, and burn with a sooty flame.

\[\text{Stability: Aromatic} \gt \text{Non-aromatic} \gt \text{Anti-aromatic}\]

Aromatic, anti-aromatic and non-aromatic compounds

CharacteristicAromatic (A)Anti-aromatic (B)Non-aromatic (C)
Examplebenzene, naphthalene, pyridine, cyclopentadienyl anion, tropylium cationcyclobutadiene, cyclopentadienyl cationcyclohexane, cyclopentadiene, cyclooctatetraene (non-planar)
1. Structurecyclic, planar; all ring atoms \(sp^2\) hybridisedcyclic, planar; all ring atoms \(sp^2\) hybridisedcyclic or acyclic; planar or non-planar; \(sp\) or \(sp^2\) or \(sp^3\)
2. \(\pi\) electrons\((4n+2)\,\pi\) electrons\(4n\,\pi\) electronsno such requirement
3. M.O. descriptionall \(\pi\) electrons paired in B.M.O.unpaired electrons in B.M.O. / non-bonding M.O.B.M.O. / non-bonding M.O.
4. Overlappingfavourable overlapping of p-orbitalsunfavourable overlapping of p-orbitalssimple overlapping, like alkenes
5. Resonance energy (R.E.)very high; R.E. \(\gt\) 20–25 kcal/molzero4–8 kcal/mol, like alkenes
6. Stabilityextra stabilityunstable; does not exist at room temperaturenormal stability, like a conjugated system
7. Characteristic reactionselectrophilic substitutiondimerisation to attain stabilityelectrophilic addition, like alkenes

Carbocation

Definition: a carbon intermediate that contains three bond pairs and a positive charge on carbon.

Hybridisation: carbocations may be \(sp^2\) or \(sp\) hybridised.

HybridisationExamples
\(sp^2\)\(\mathrm{CH_3^+}\), \(\mathrm{CH_3CH_2^+}\), \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_3C^+}\)
\(sp\)\(\mathrm{H_2C{=}\overset{+}{C}H}\), \(\mathrm{HC{\equiv}C^+}\)
  • Carbocations are electron deficient: they have only six electrons in their valence shell, so they act as Lewis acids.
  • Most carbocations are short-lived and highly reactive; they occur as intermediates in some organic reactions.
  • They react with Lewis bases or ions that can donate the electron pair they need to achieve a stable octet (the electronic configuration of a noble gas).
  • Being electron-seeking reagents, carbocations are electrophiles. All Lewis acids, including protons, are electrophiles. By accepting an electron pair a proton attains the valence-shell configuration of helium; carbocations attain that of neon.

Stability of carbocations

Carbocations are stabilised by (i) +I effect, (ii) +M effect, (iii) hyperconjugation, (iv) delocalisation of charge.

General stability order:

\[\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{(C_6H_5)_3C^+ \gt (C_6H_5)_2CH^+ \gt C_6H_5CH_2^+ \gt CH_2{=}CH{-}CH_2^+}\\&\mathrm{\gt (CH_3)_3C^+ \gt (CH_3)_2CH^+ \gt CH_3CH_2^+ \gt CH_3^+ \gt CH_2{=}CH^+}\end{aligned}\]

Carbanion

Definition: a carbon intermediate that contains three bond pairs and a negative charge (lone pair) on carbon.

Hybridisation: carbanions may be \(sp^3\), \(sp^2\) or \(sp\) hybridised.

HybridisationExamples
\(sp^3\)\(\mathrm{CH_3^-}\), \(\mathrm{CH_3CH_2^-}\), \(\mathrm{(CH_3)_3C^-}\)
\(sp^2\)\(\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH^-}\), \(\mathrm{CH_2{=}CH{-}CH_2^-}\), \(\mathrm{C_6H_5^-}\)
\(sp\)\(\mathrm{HC{\equiv}C^-}\)

Stability: carbanions are stabilised by electron-withdrawing effects: (i) −I effect, (ii) −M effect, (iii) delocalisation of charge.

\[\mathrm{CH_3^- \gt 1^\circ \gt 2^\circ \gt 3^\circ\ (alkyl)}\qquad \mathrm{HC{\equiv}C^- \gt CH_2{=}CH^- \gt CH_3CH_2^-}\ \ (sp \gt sp^2 \gt sp^3)\]

Free radical

Homolysis of a covalent bond gives free-radical intermediates that possess unpaired electrons. They are generated in the presence of sunlight, peroxides or high temperature.

Free radical: an uncharged intermediate with three bond pairs and an unpaired electron on carbon.

  • It is a neutral species with an odd electron.
  • It is paramagnetic in nature because of the odd electron.
  • No rearrangement is generally observed.
  • The carbon atom having the odd electron is in the \(sp^2\) hybridised state.
  • Any reaction carried out in the presence of sunlight, peroxide or high temperature generally proceeds via a free-radical intermediate.

Stability: a free radical is stabilised by resonance, hyperconjugation and +I groups.

\[\mathrm{(CH_3)_3\dot{C} \gt (CH_3)_2\dot{C}H \gt CH_3\dot{C}H_2 \gt \dot{C}H_3}\]

Carbenes (divalent carbon intermediates)

A group of intermediates in which carbon forms only two bonds. These neutral divalent carbon species are called carbenes. Most carbenes are highly unstable and capable of only fleeting existence; soon after they are formed they usually react with another molecule.

Methods of preparation:

\[\mathrm{CHCl_3 + KOH \to {:}CCl_2 + KCl + H_2O}\]\[\mathrm{CH_2I_2 + Zn \to {:}CH_2 + ZnI_2}\]

Nitrenes

The nitrogen analogues of carbenes are nitrenes, \(\mathrm{R{-}\overset{\cdot\cdot}{N}{:}}\). They are very reactive because the octet of nitrogen is incomplete: only one valency of N is satisfied.

Benzyne

Benzyne, \(\mathrm{C_6H_4}\), has one extra \(\mathrm{C{-}C}\) \(\pi\) bond in the benzene ring.

  • The newly formed \(\pi\) bond cannot enter into resonance with the other \(\pi\) orbitals of the ring, because it lies in a perpendicular plane (the plane of the ring).
  • The hybridisation of each carbon of the "benzynic bond" is \(sp^2\); the overlap between these \(sp^2\) hybrid orbitals is not very effective.
23
Organic chemistry

Alkane, Alkene & Alkyne

Wurtz reaction (alkanes)

Reagent: Na, dry ether. 1° and 2° alkyl halides give this reaction.

\[\mathrm{2R{-}X + 2Na \xrightarrow{\text{ether}} R{-}R + 2NaX}\]

Two different alkyl halides give a mixture of three alkanes:

\[\mathrm{R{-}X + R'{-}X + 2Na \xrightarrow{\text{ether}} R{-}R' + R{-}R + R'{-}R' + 2NaX}\]

A dihalide with the halogens on the terminal carbons reacts intramolecularly to give a cycloalkane:

\[\mathrm{Br{-}(CH_2)_3{-}Br + 2Na \xrightarrow{\text{ether}} \text{cyclopropane} + 2NaBr}\]

Electrophilic addition to alkenes and alkynes

The characteristic reaction of alkenes and alkynes is electrophilic addition.

Step 1 – attack of the electrophile on the \(\pi\) bond forms a carbocation; the positive charge is on the more substituted carbon.

\[\mathrm{C{=}C + E^+ \to \overset{+}{C}{-}C{-}E}\]

Step 2 – attack by a nucleophile on the carbocation gives the addition product.

\[\mathrm{\overset{+}{C}{-}C{-}E + Nu{:} \to Nu{-}C{-}C{-}E}\]

Addition of water and of hydrogen halides

(a) Addition of water (acid-catalysed, Markovnikov):

\[\mathrm{R{-}CH{=}CH_2 + H_2O \xrightarrow{H^+} R{-}CH(OH){-}CH_3}\]

(b) Addition of hydrogen halides (\(\mathrm{HX}\) = HCl, HBr, HI) to an alkyne (Markovnikov addition):

\[\mathrm{R{-}C{\equiv}C{-}R \xrightarrow{HX} R{-}CX{=}CH{-}R \xrightarrow{HX} R{-}CX_2{-}CH_2{-}R}\]

Stereochemistry of addition

When the electrophile is \(\mathrm{Cl^+}\), \(\mathrm{Br^+}\), \(\mathrm{I^+}\), \(\mathrm{NO_2^+}\) or \(\mathrm{Hg^{2+}}\), the stereochemistry is important and the major product is formed by anti addition.

24
Organic chemistry

Alkyl Halide

SN1 reaction of alkyl halides

Nucleophilic substitution (SN1, SN2) is the characteristic reaction of alkyl halides.

Mechanism: ionisation of R–X (assisted by \(\mathrm{Ag^+}\) from \(\mathrm{AgNO_3}\)) gives a carbocation, which is captured by water.

\[\mathrm{R{-}X \xrightarrow{AgNO_3} R^+ + AgX\downarrow}\]\[\mathrm{R^+ + H_2O \to R{-}OH + H^+}\ \ (\mathrm{R}\ \text{may rearrange})\]

Alkyl halides are hydrolysed to alcohols very slowly by water, but rapidly by silver oxide suspended in boiling water.

SN2 reaction of alkyl halides

Mechanism: the nucleophile attacks the carbon from the side opposite to the leaving group in a single step, through a transition state in which the nucleophile and the leaving group are both partly bonded to carbon.

\[\mathrm{HO^- + R{-}X \to \left[HO{\cdots}R{\cdots}X\right]^- \to HO{-}R + X^-}\]

For a carbon bearing groups a, b, d, the three groups flip over like an umbrella, so the configuration is inverted:

\[\mathrm{HO^- + X{-}C(a)(b)(d) \to \left[HO{\cdots}C(a)(b)(d){\cdots}X\right]^- \to HO{-}C(a)(b)(d) + X^-}\]
25
Organic chemistry

Alcohol

SN1 reaction of alcohols with HX

Mechanism: protonation of the –OH group, loss of water to give a carbocation, then attack by the halide ion (R may rearrange).

\[\mathrm{R{-}OH + H^+ \to R{-}\overset{+}{O}H_2 \to R^+ + H_2O \xrightarrow{X^-} R{-}X}\]
  • Reactivity of HX: \(\mathrm{HI \gt HBr \gt HCl}\)
  • Reactivity of ROH: allyl, benzyl \(\gt 3^\circ \gt 2^\circ \gt 1^\circ\) (order of carbocation stability)

Example: isopropyl alcohol \(\to\) isopropyl bromide

\[\mathrm{CH_3CH(OH)CH_3 + HBr \to CH_3CHBrCH_3 + H_2O}\]

SN2 reaction of alcohols

\[\mathrm{ROH + PCl_5 \to RCl + POCl_3 + HCl}\]\[\mathrm{3ROH + PCl_3 \to 3RCl + H_3PO_3}\]\[\mathrm{ROH + SOCl_2 \xrightarrow{\text{pyridine}} RCl + SO_2 + HCl}\]

Williamson's synthesis

Sodium or potassium alkoxide is heated with an alkyl halide (SN2). The method is particularly useful for preparing mixed ethers.

\[\mathrm{R_2O^- + R_1{-}X \to \left[R_2O{\cdots}R_1{\cdots}X\right]^- \to R_1{-}O{-}R_2 + X^-}\]

Nucleophilic aromatic substitution (SN2Ar)

An electron-withdrawing group at the ortho or para position with respect to a good leaving group is a necessary condition for SN2Ar.

  1. Step I (slow, rate-determining): the nucleophile adds to the ring carbon bearing X, giving a carbanion (cyclohexadienyl anion) intermediate in which X and Nu are on the same carbon.
  2. Step II (fast): the leaving group \(\mathrm{X^-}\) is expelled and aromaticity is restored.
\[\mathrm{Ar{-}X + Nu^- \xrightarrow{\text{Step I, slow (RDS)}} \left[Ar(X)(Nu)\right]^- \xrightarrow{\text{Step II, fast}} Ar{-}Nu + X^-}\]

The intermediate ion is stabilised by resonance; such ions are stable salts called Meisenheimer salts.

Effect of substituents on SN2Ar

Group G on the ringEffect on the carbanionEffect on Ar–SN2 reactionGroups
withdraws electronstends to neutralise the negative charge of the ring; this dispersal of charge stabilises the carbanionactivates\(\mathrm{-N^+(CH_3)_3}\), \(\mathrm{-NO_2}\), \(\mathrm{-CN}\), \(\mathrm{-SO_3H}\), \(\mathrm{-COOH}\), \(\mathrm{-CHO}\), \(\mathrm{-COR}\), \(\mathrm{-X}\)
releases electronstends to intensify the negative charge; destabilises the carbaniondeactivates (slows the reaction)\(\mathrm{-NH_2}\), \(\mathrm{-OH}\), \(\mathrm{-OR}\), \(\mathrm{-R}\)

Element effect

Reactivity order towards SN2Ar with different halogens:

\[\mathrm{Ar{-}F \gt Ar{-}Cl \gt Ar{-}Br \gt Ar{-}I}\]
26
Organic chemistry

Grignard Reagent

Grignard's reagent

Grignard's reagent: \(\mathrm{RMgX}\) (alkyl magnesium halide), prepared from an alkyl (or aryl) halide and magnesium in dry ether.

\[\mathrm{R{-}X + Mg \xrightarrow{\text{dry ether}} R{-}Mg{-}X}\]

The \(\mathrm{C{-}Mg}\) bond is strongly polar, \(\mathrm{R^{\delta-}{-}Mg^{\delta+}X}\), so the alkyl group behaves as a carbanion: RMgX is both a strong nucleophile and a strong base. Halide reactivity in the preparation: \(\mathrm{RI \gt RBr \gt RCl}\). Moisture must be excluded (anhydrous ether).

General course of its reactions: \(\mathrm{R^-}\) adds to (or displaces a group at) an electrophilic carbon to give a magnesium alkoxide or carboxylate, which is hydrolysed by \(\mathrm{H_3O^+}\) in the work-up.

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Active-H compounds: RMgX → R–H

\[\mathrm{R{-}MgX + H{-}Z \to R{-}H + Mg(X)Z}\]
Active-H compound \(\mathrm{H{-}Z}\)Other product
water, \(\mathrm{H{-}OH}\)\(\mathrm{Mg(OH)X}\)
alcohol or phenol, \(\mathrm{R'{-}OH}\)\(\mathrm{Mg(OR')X}\)
carboxylic acid, \(\mathrm{R'{-}COOH}\)\(\mathrm{R'{-}COOMgX}\)
ammonia / amine, \(\mathrm{NH_3}\), \(\mathrm{R'NH_2}\)\(\mathrm{Mg(NH_2)X}\), \(\mathrm{Mg(NHR')X}\)
terminal alkyne, \(\mathrm{R'{-}C{\equiv}C{-}H}\)\(\mathrm{R'{-}C{\equiv}C{-}MgX}\)
hydrogen halide, \(\mathrm{H{-}X}\)\(\mathrm{MgX_2}\)

Here RMgX acts as a base and the alkane R–H is evolved; so a Grignard reagent cannot be prepared or kept in the presence of water or any acidic hydrogen (–OH, –COOH, –NH2, –C≡C–H). With \(\mathrm{CH_3MgI}\) the volume of \(\mathrm{CH_4}\) evolved measures the number of active hydrogens (Zerewitinoff determination).

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Aldehydes and ketones → alcohols

Nucleophilic addition of R to the carbonyl carbon, followed by hydrolysis of the magnesium alkoxide.

Carbonyl compoundProduct (after \(\mathrm{H_3O^+}\))Type of alcohol
formaldehyde, \(\mathrm{HCHO}\)\(\mathrm{R{-}CH_2{-}OH}\)1° alcohol
other aldehydes, \(\mathrm{R'CHO}\)\(\mathrm{R'{-}CH(OH){-}R}\)2° alcohol
ketones, \(\mathrm{R'{-}CO{-}R''}\)\(\mathrm{R'R''C(OH){-}R}\)3° alcohol
\[\mathrm{R'R''C{=}O + R{-}MgX \to R'R''C(R){-}OMgX \xrightarrow{H_3O^+} R'R''C(R){-}OH + Mg(OH)X}\]

Examples:

\[\mathrm{CH_3CHO + CH_3MgBr \to CH_3CH(OH)CH_3}\qquad \mathrm{CH_3COCH_3 + CH_3MgBr \to (CH_3)_3C{-}OH}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Carbon dioxide → carboxylic acid

\[\mathrm{R{-}MgX + O{=}C{=}O \to R{-}COOMgX \xrightarrow{H_3O^+} R{-}COOH}\]

The acid has one carbon more than the alkyl group R (e.g. \(\mathrm{CH_3MgBr \to CH_3COOH}\)).

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Acid chlorides and esters: two RMgX are used

The first RMgX substitutes the leaving group to give a ketone (or aldehyde), which is more reactive than the starting compound and at once takes a second RMgX. Both alkyl groups on the carbinol carbon come from the Grignard reagent.

SubstrateOverall reaction with \(\mathrm{2RMgX}\), then \(\mathrm{H_3O^+}\)Product
acid chloride\(\mathrm{R'COCl + 2RMgX \to R'R_2C{-}OMgX + Mg(X)Cl}\) \(\to\) \(\mathrm{R'R_2C{-}OH}\)3° alcohol
ester\(\mathrm{R'COOR'' + 2RMgX \to R'R_2C{-}OMgX + R''OMgX}\) \(\to\) \(\mathrm{R'R_2C{-}OH + R''OH}\)3° alcohol
formic ester\(\mathrm{HCOOR'' + 2RMgX \to R_2CH{-}OMgX + R''OMgX}\) \(\to\) \(\mathrm{R_2CH{-}OH + R''OH}\)2° alcohol (two identical R)
acid anhydride\(\mathrm{(R'CO)_2O + 2RMgX \to R'R_2C{-}OMgX + R'COOMgX}\) \(\to\) \(\mathrm{R'R_2C{-}OH + R'COOH}\)3° alcohol + acid

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Nitriles → ketones

\[\mathrm{R'{-}C{\equiv}N + R{-}MgX \to R'{-}C(R){=}NMgX \xrightarrow{H_3O^+} R'{-}CO{-}R + NH_3}\]

The reaction stops at the ketone stage (the imine–magnesium salt is not attacked a second time). Example: \(\mathrm{C_6H_5CN + CH_3MgBr \to C_6H_5COCH_3}\) (acetophenone).

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Epoxides → alcohols with two more carbons

\[\mathrm{R{-}MgX + (CH_2)_2O \to R{-}CH_2CH_2{-}OMgX \xrightarrow{H_3O^+} R{-}CH_2CH_2{-}OH}\]

Ethylene oxide, \(\mathrm{(CH_2)_2O}\) (a three-membered cyclic ether), gives a 1° alcohol two carbons longer than R. In a substituted epoxide R attacks the less hindered carbon (SN2-type ring opening):

\[\mathrm{R{-}MgX + CH_3CH(O)CH_2 \to R{-}CH_2{-}CH(OH){-}CH_3}\]

\[\mathrm{Ph{-}CH{=}CH{-}COOH \xrightarrow{LiAlH_4} Ph{-}CH_2{-}CH_2{-}CH_2OH}\]

NaBH4, EtOH

\[\mathrm{Aldehyde \xrightarrow{NaBH_4,\ EtOH} 1^\circ\ alcohol}\]\[\mathrm{Ketone \xrightarrow{NaBH_4,\ EtOH} 2^\circ\ alcohol}\]\[\mathrm{Acid\ halide \xrightarrow{NaBH_4,\ EtOH} 1^\circ\ alcohol}\]

A milder reagent than \(\mathrm{LiAlH_4}\): it does not reduce \(\mathrm{C{=}C}\), \(\mathrm{{-}COOH}\) or \(\mathrm{{-}COOR}\).

The aluminium isopropoxide (Meerwein–Ponndorf–Verley) reduction is selective for the carbonyl group; \(\mathrm{C{=}C}\) is not affected.

Rosenmund's reduction

\[\mathrm{R{-}COCl + H_2 \xrightarrow{Pd/BaSO_4} R{-}CH{=}O + HCl}\]

The Pd/\(\mathrm{BaSO_4}\) catalyst is partially poisoned so that the reduction stops at the aldehyde stage (acid chloride \(\to\) aldehyde).

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Birch reduction (Li/Na/K + liquid NH3)

\[\mathrm{R{-}C{\equiv}C{-}R \xrightarrow{\text{Li/Na/K},\ \text{liq. }\mathrm{NH_3}} R{-}CH{=}CH{-}R}\ \ (\text{trans alkene})\]

Terminal alkynes are not reduced.

The same dissolving-metal reagent reduces benzene to cyclohexa-1,4-diene:

\[\mathrm{C_6H_6 \xrightarrow{Na,\ \text{liq. }NH_3,\ EtOH} \text{cyclohexa-1,4-diene}}\]

Stephen's reduction

\[\mathrm{R{-}C{\equiv}N \xrightarrow{SnCl_2,\ HCl} R{-}CH{=}NH{\cdot}HCl \xrightarrow{H_3O^+} R{-}CH{=}O + NH_4Cl}\]

Nitrile \(\to\) aldehyde (one carbon more than the alkyl halide from which the nitrile was made).

DIBAL-H is also used for the same conversion.

Clemmensen reduction

\[\mathrm{R{-}CO{-}R' \xrightarrow{Zn{-}Hg,\ \text{conc. HCl}} R{-}CH_2{-}R'}\qquad \mathrm{R{-}CHO \xrightarrow{Zn{-}Hg,\ \text{conc. HCl}} R{-}CH_3}\]

Carbonyl group (aldehyde or ketone) \(\to\) \(\mathrm{{-}CH_2{-}}\) in acidic medium.

Wolff–Kishner reduction

\[\mathrm{R{-}CO{-}R' \xrightarrow{NH_2NH_2} R{-}C(R'){=}N{-}NH_2 \xrightarrow{KOH,\ \text{ethylene glycol},\ \Delta} R{-}CH_2{-}R' + N_2\uparrow}\]

Carbonyl group \(\to\) \(\mathrm{{-}CH_2{-}}\) in basic medium, through the hydrazone.

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Lindlar catalyst

\[\mathrm{R{-}C{\equiv}C{-}R \xrightarrow{H_2,\ \text{Lindlar catalyst}} R{-}CH{=}CH{-}R}\ \ (\text{cis alkene})\]

Lindlar catalyst: Pd on \(\mathrm{CaCO_3}\) partially poisoned with quinoline (or \(\mathrm{Pb(OAc)_2}\)); hydrogen is added syn and the reduction stops at the alkene.

\(\mathrm{H_2}\), Pd, \(\mathrm{BaSO_4}\) (with quinoline) is also used for the same conversion.

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Red phosphorus and HI

Almost all functional-group-containing compounds are converted into the corresponding alkane by red P + HI.

\[\mathrm{R{-}CH_2OH \xrightarrow{\text{red P + HI},\ \Delta} R{-}CH_3}\]\[\mathrm{R{-}CHO \xrightarrow{\text{red P + HI},\ \Delta} R{-}CH_3}\]\[\mathrm{R_2C{=}O \xrightarrow{\text{red P + HI},\ \Delta} R_2CH_2}\]

DIBAL-H reduction

DIBAL-H = diisobutylaluminium hydride, \(\mathrm{[(CH_3)_2CHCH_2]_2AlH}\).

\[\mathrm{R{-}COOR' \xrightarrow[\text{cold}]{\text{DIBAL-H}} R{-}CH{=}O + R'OH}\]\[\mathrm{R{-}C{\equiv}N \xrightarrow{\text{DIBAL-H}} R{-}CH{=}O}\]

At ordinary temperature esters are reduced to alcohols, but at low temperature (cold) esters are reduced to aldehydes.

\[\mathrm{R{-}COOR' \xrightarrow[\text{ordinary temp.}]{\text{DIBAL-H}} R{-}CH_2OH + R'OH}\]

Organic chemistry

KMnO4 (both media) or K2Cr2O7 (acidic medium)

alkyne, \(\mathrm{R{-}C{\equiv}C{-}R'}\)\(\mathrm{RCOOH + R'COOH}\)
\[\mathrm{R{-}CH_2OH \xrightarrow{KMnO_4\ \text{or}\ K_2Cr_2O_7/H^+} R{-}COOH}\]\[\mathrm{R_2C{=}CHR' \xrightarrow{KMnO_4\ \text{or}\ K_2Cr_2O_7/H^+} R_2C{=}O + R'COOH}\]

KMnO4 works in both acidic and alkaline media; K2Cr2O7 only in acidic medium. A terminal \(\mathrm{{=}CH_2}\) is oxidised to \(\mathrm{CO_2 + H_2O}\). Cold, dilute, alkaline KMnO4 only hydroxylates the double bond (see Baeyer's reagent).

Oxidation of aromatic side chain

Any alkyl side chain that has at least one benzylic hydrogen is oxidised to \(\mathrm{{-}COOH}\) directly attached to the ring (the ring itself is not attacked).

\[\mathrm{Ar{-}R \xrightarrow{KMnO_4/OH^-,\ \Delta\ \text{or}\ K_2Cr_2O_7/H^+} Ar{-}COOH}\]

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

Baeyer–Villiger oxidation (m-CPBA or CH3CO3H)

A ketone is converted into an ester: an oxygen atom is inserted between the carbonyl carbon and one of the groups, which migrates to oxygen.

\[\mathrm{R{-}CO{-}R' + CH_3CO_3H \to R{-}CO{-}O{-}R' + CH_3COOH}\]

Priority of shift (migratory aptitude towards the electron-deficient oxygen):

\[\mathrm{R'} = \mathrm{Ph} \gt \mathrm{Ethyl} \gt \mathrm{Methyl}\]

Examples:

\[\mathrm{CH_3{-}CO{-}C_6H_5 \xrightarrow{CH_3CO_3H} CH_3{-}CO{-}O{-}C_6H_5}\ \ (\text{phenyl acetate; Ph migrates})\]\[\mathrm{CH_3{-}CO{-}C_2H_5 \xrightarrow{CH_3CO_3H} CH_3{-}CO{-}O{-}C_2H_5}\ \ (\text{ethyl acetate; ethyl migrates})\]\[\text{cyclohexanone} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{CH_3CO_3H}} \varepsilon\text{-caprolactone (cyclic ester)}\]

Fuller standard order of migration: 3° alkyl \(\gt\) 2° alkyl \(\gt\) phenyl \(\gt\) 1° alkyl \(\gt\) methyl; the migrating group retains its configuration. The cyclohexanone example is rebuilt — the original page is a diagram.

Prilezhaev reaction and anti-hydroxylation

A peroxy acid (MCPBA, m-chloroperbenzoic acid) converts an alkene into an epoxide; acid-catalysed opening of the epoxide ring by water gives the anti diol.

\[\mathrm{R{-}CH{=}CH{-}R \xrightarrow{\text{MCPBA}} \text{epoxide} \xrightarrow{H_3O^+} R{-}CH(OH){-}CH(OH){-}R}\ \ (\text{anti})\]
Alkenesyn (Baeyer's / \(\mathrm{OsO_4}\))anti (MCPBA, then \(\mathrm{H_3O^+}\))
cis-but-2-enemeso-diol(±)-diol
trans-but-2-ene(±)-diolmeso-diol
cyclohexenecis-cyclohexane-1,2-dioltrans-cyclohexane-1,2-diol

29
Organic chemistry

Aldehyde & Ketones

Aldol condensation

Carbonyl compounds having an acidic \(\alpha\)-hydrogen (on an \(sp^3\) carbon) show this reaction in the presence of dil. NaOH or dil. acid. The \(\beta\)-hydroxy carbonyl compound (aldol) loses water on heating to give an \(\alpha,\beta\)-unsaturated carbonyl compound.

\[\mathrm{2\,CH_3CHO \xrightarrow{\text{dil. NaOH}} CH_3CH(OH)CH_2CHO \xrightarrow[-\mathrm{H_2O}]{\mathrm{OH^-/H^+},\ \Delta} CH_3CH{=}CHCHO}\]

Crossed aldol condensation

(i) Acetaldehyde with formaldehyde:

\[\mathrm{CH_3CHO + HCHO \xrightarrow{\text{dil. NaOH}} HOCH_2CH_2CHO \xrightarrow[-\mathrm{H_2O}]{\mathrm{OH^-/H^+}} CH_2{=}CHCHO}\]

(ii) Acetone with formaldehyde:

\[\mathrm{CH_3COCH_3 + HCHO \xrightarrow{\text{dil. NaOH}} CH_3COCH_2CH_2OH \xrightarrow[-\mathrm{H_2O}]{\mathrm{OH^-/H^+}} CH_3COCH{=}CH_2}\]

Cannizzaro reaction

Carbonyl compounds not having \(\alpha\)-hydrogen (\(sp^3\) C–H) show the following disproportionation (one molecule is oxidised, one is reduced) with concentrated alkali.

\[\mathrm{2\,HCHO \xrightarrow{\text{NaOH (50\%)}} CH_3OH + HCOONa}\]\[\mathrm{2\,C_6H_5CHO \xrightarrow{\text{NaOH (50\%)}} C_6H_5CH_2OH + \underset{\text{sod. benzoate}}{C_6H_5COONa}}\]

Crossed Cannizzaro reaction

\[\mathrm{C_6H_5CHO + HCHO \xrightarrow{\text{NaOH (50\%)}} C_6H_5CH_2OH + \underset{\text{sod. formate}}{HCOONa}}\]

Formaldehyde is the one oxidised (to formate); the other aldehyde is reduced to the alcohol.

Formation of hydrazones and azines

\[\mathrm{R_2C{=}O + H_2N{-}NH_2 \to R_2C(OH)NHNH_2 \xrightarrow{-\mathrm{H_2O}} \underset{\text{hydrazone}}{R_2C{=}N{-}NH_2}}\]\[\mathrm{R_2C{=}N{-}NH_2 + O{=}CR_2 \xrightarrow{-\mathrm{H_2O}} \underset{\text{azine}}{R_2C{=}N{-}N{=}CR_2}}\]

Perkin reaction

When benzaldehyde (or any other aromatic aldehyde) is heated with the anhydride of an aliphatic acid (containing two \(\alpha\)-hydrogen atoms) in the presence of its sodium salt, condensation takes place to form a \(\beta\)-arylacrylic acid; e.g. with acetic anhydride and sodium acetate, cinnamic acid is formed.

\[\mathrm{C_6H_5CHO + (CH_3CO)_2O \xrightarrow{CH_3COONa} C_6H_5CH{=}CHCO_2H + CH_3CO_2H}\]

Mechanism

  1. Acetate ion removes an acidic \(\alpha\)-hydrogen of the anhydride to give a carbanion:\[\mathrm{CH_3COOCOCH_3 + CH_3CO_2^- \rightleftharpoons {}^{-}CH_2COOCOCH_3 + CH_3CO_2H}\]
  2. The carbanion attacks the carbonyl carbon of benzaldehyde:\[\mathrm{C_6H_5CHO + {}^{-}CH_2COOCOCH_3 \to C_6H_5CH(O^-)CH_2COOCOCH_3}\]
  3. The alkoxide is protonated:\[\mathrm{C_6H_5CH(O^-)CH_2COOCOCH_3 \xrightarrow{H^+} C_6H_5CH(OH)CH_2COOCOCH_3}\]
  4. Loss of water (dehydration) gives the unsaturated mixed anhydride:\[\mathrm{C_6H_5CH(OH)CH_2COOCOCH_3 \xrightarrow{-\mathrm{H_2O}} C_6H_5CH{=}CHCOOCOCH_3}\]
  5. Hydrolysis of the mixed anhydride gives cinnamic acid:\[\mathrm{C_6H_5CH{=}CHCOOCOCH_3 + H_2O \to C_6H_5CH{=}CHCO_2H + CH_3CO_2H}\]

Haloform reaction

Acetaldehyde and methyl alkyl ketones react rapidly with halogen (\(\mathrm{Cl_2}\), \(\mathrm{Br_2}\) or \(\mathrm{I_2}\)) in the presence of alkali to give a haloform and the salt of an acid.

\[\mathrm{R{-}CO{-}CH_3 \xrightarrow{\mathrm{NaOH/Br_2}} \underset{\text{bromoform}}{CHBr_3} + RCOONa}\]

The \(\mathrm{{-}CH_3}\) of the \(\mathrm{CH_3CO{-}}\) group is converted into the haloform because it contains acidic hydrogen atoms; the rest of the alkyl methyl ketone gives the salt of an acid having one carbon atom fewer than the ketone.

Preparation of the haloform from a methyl ketone involves two steps:

(a) Halogenation

\[\mathrm{R{-}CO{-}CH_3 \xrightarrow{Br_2} R{-}CO{-}CBr_3}\]

(b) Alkali hydrolysis

\[\mathrm{R{-}CO{-}CBr_3 \xrightarrow{NaOH} CHBr_3 + RCOONa}\]

This reaction is used to distinguish the presence of the \(\mathrm{CH_3CO{-}}\) group.

Nucleophilic addition to C=O

General carbonyl compound \(\mathrm{RR'C{=}O}\) (R, R′ = H, alkyl or aryl).

ReagentReaction

Oxidation tests: Tollens' and Fehling's

ReagentReaction
Tollens' reagent, \(\mathrm{[Ag(NH_3)_2]^+}\)\(\mathrm{RCHO + 2[Ag(NH_3)_2]^+ + 3OH^- \to RCOO^- + 2Ag\downarrow + 4NH_3 + 2H_2O}\) (silver mirror)
Fehling's solution, \(\mathrm{Cu^{2+}}\) (alkaline tartrate complex)\(\mathrm{RCHO + 2Cu^{2+} + 5OH^- \to RCOO^- + Cu_2O\downarrow + 3H_2O}\) (red-brown precipitate)

Aldehydes give both tests; ketones do not (they are oxidised only by strong oxidising agents, with C–C cleavage). Aromatic aldehydes (e.g. \(\mathrm{C_6H_5CHO}\)) respond to Tollens' reagent but not to Fehling's solution.

\(\mathrm{PCl_5}\)\(\mathrm{RCOOR' + PCl_5 \to RCOCl + R'Cl + POCl_3}\)
Claisen condensation (\(\mathrm{NaOC_2H_5}\), then \(\mathrm{H_3O^+}\))\(\mathrm{2\,CH_3COOC_2H_5 \xrightarrow{NaOC_2H_5} CH_3COCH_2COOC_2H_5 + C_2H_5OH}\) (ethyl acetoacetate)

Rebuilt summary — the original page is a diagram.

31
Organic chemistry

Aromatic Compounds

Electrophilic aromatic substitution

All the substitutions below follow one general mechanism: the \(\pi\) electrons of benzene attack an electrophile \(\mathrm{E^+}\), giving a resonance-stabilised carbocation (the \(\sigma\)-complex); loss of \(\mathrm{H^+}\) restores the aromatic ring.

\[\mathrm{C_6H_6 + E^+ \to [C_6H_6E]^+ \to C_6H_5E + H^+}\]
  1. Formation of a (stronger) electrophile.
  2. Electrophilic attack and formation of the \(\sigma\)-complex.
  3. Loss of a proton gives the product.

Bromination of benzene

Bromine itself is not sufficiently electrophilic to react with benzene, but a strong Lewis acid such as \(\mathrm{FeBr_3}\) catalyses the reaction.

Step 1: formation of a stronger electrophile.

\[\mathrm{Br{-}Br + FeBr_3 \to Br{-}Br^{+}{-}FeBr_3^{-}}\]

Step 2: electrophilic attack and formation of the sigma complex.

\[\mathrm{C_6H_6 + Br{-}Br^{+}{-}FeBr_3^{-} \to [C_6H_6Br]^+ + FeBr_4^-}\]

Step 3: loss of a proton gives the products.

\[\mathrm{[C_6H_6Br]^+ + FeBr_4^- \to \underset{\text{bromobenzene}}{C_6H_5Br} + HBr + FeBr_3}\]

Energy profile (reactants \(\mathrm{C_6H_6 + Br_2 + FeBr_3}\) to products \(\mathrm{C_6H_5Br + HBr + FeBr_3}\)): two transition states, T.S.1 and T.S.2, with the \(\sigma\)-complex \(\mathrm{[C_6H_6Br]^+FeBr_4^-}\) as the intermediate between them; the overall reaction is exothermic, \(\Delta H = -10.8\ \mathrm{kcal\,mol^{-1}}\).

Nitration

Step 1: formation of the nitronium ion (electrophile) from the nitrating mixture (conc. \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\) + conc. \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\)).

\[\mathrm{HNO_3 + 2H_2SO_4 \rightleftharpoons NO_2^+ + H_3O^+ + 2HSO_4^-}\]

Step 2: electrophilic attack gives the \(\sigma\)-complex.

\[\mathrm{C_6H_6 + NO_2^+ \to [C_6H_6NO_2]^+}\]

Step 3: loss of a proton.

\[\mathrm{[C_6H_6NO_2]^+ \to \underset{\text{nitrobenzene}}{C_6H_5NO_2} + H^+}\]

Sulphonation

The electrophilic reagent, \(\mathrm{SO_3}\), attacks the benzene ring to form the intermediate carbocation (\(\sigma\)-complex).

Step 1: formation of the electrophile.

\[\mathrm{2H_2SO_4 \rightleftharpoons SO_3 + H_3O^+ + HSO_4^-}\]

Step 2: attack of \(\mathrm{SO_3}\) on the ring gives the \(\sigma\)-complex \(\mathrm{[C_6H_6SO_3]}\) (ring carbocation carrying H and \(\mathrm{SO_3^-}\)).

Step 3: loss of \(\mathrm{H^+}\) and protonation of the sulphonate group give benzenesulphonic acid.

\[\mathrm{C_6H_6 + H_2SO_4 \rightleftharpoons \underset{\text{benzenesulphonic acid}}{C_6H_5SO_3H} + H_2O}\]

Steps 2 and 3 are rebuilt; the original shows only the generation of \(\mathrm{SO_3}\) and the attack on the ring.

Friedel–Crafts alkylation

Alkylation mechanism:

  1. Formation of the carbocation electrophile: \[\mathrm{R{-}Cl + AlCl_3 \to R^+ + AlCl_4^-}\]
  2. Electrophilic attack gives the \(\sigma\)-complex: \[\mathrm{C_6H_6 + R^+ \to [C_6H_6R]^+}\]
  3. Loss of a proton: \[\mathrm{[C_6H_6R]^+ + AlCl_4^- \to C_6H_5R + HCl + AlCl_3}\]

In contrast to acylation, alkylation can involve carbocation rearrangement and multiple substitution (rebuilt remark).

Friedel–Crafts acylation

Acylation of benzene may be brought about with acid chlorides or anhydrides in the presence of Lewis acids.

Step 1: formation of an acylium ion (acyl chloride + \(\mathrm{AlCl_3}\) gives a complex, which ionises).

\[\mathrm{R{-}CO{-}Cl + AlCl_3 \to [R{-}CO{-}Cl{-}AlCl_3] \to AlCl_4^- + [R{-}C{\equiv}O^+ \leftrightarrow R{-}C^+{=}O]}\]

Step 2: electrophilic attack forms the \(\sigma\)-complex.

\[\mathrm{C_6H_6 + R{-}C{\equiv}O^+ \to [C_6H_6COR]^+}\]

Step 3: loss of a proton; complexation of the product (the acylbenzene is held as its \(\mathrm{AlCl_3}\) complex and is freed on aqueous work-up).

\[\mathrm{[C_6H_6COR]^+ + AlCl_4^- \to \underset{\text{acylbenzene}}{C_6H_5COR}{\cdot}AlCl_3 + HCl}\]

Acylation example and limitations

\[\mathrm{\underset{\text{ethylbenzene}}{C_6H_5C_2H_5} + \underset{\text{acetyl chloride}}{CH_3COCl} \xrightarrow{AlCl_3} \underset{p\text{-ethylacetophenone (70\text{–}80\%)}}{p\text{-}C_2H_5C_6H_4COCH_3} + HCl}\]

Friedel–Crafts acylations are generally free from rearrangements and multiple substitution. They do not go on strongly deactivated rings (e.g. nitrobenzene).

Chemical reactions of benzene

ReactionEquation
Nitration\(\mathrm{C_6H_6 + HNO_3 \xrightarrow{\text{conc. }H_2SO_4} C_6H_5NO_2 + H_2O}\)
Sulphonation\(\mathrm{C_6H_6 + H_2SO_4 \xrightarrow{\text{conc. }H_2SO_4/SO_3} C_6H_5SO_3H + H_2O}\)
Chlorination\(\mathrm{C_6H_6 + Cl_2 \xrightarrow{FeCl_3} C_6H_5Cl + HCl}\)
Friedel–Crafts alkylation\(\mathrm{C_6H_6 + RCl \xrightarrow{AlCl_3} C_6H_5R + HCl}\)
Friedel–Crafts acylation\(\mathrm{C_6H_6 + RCOCl \xrightarrow{AlCl_3} C_6H_5COR + HCl}\)
H/D exchange\(\mathrm{C_6H_6 + D^+ \xrightarrow{D_2O/D^+} C_6H_5D + H^+}\)
Diazonium coupling\(\mathrm{C_6H_6 + ArN_2^+X^- \to C_6H_5{-}N{=}N{-}Ar + HX}\) (in practice the ring must be activated, e.g. phenol or aniline)

Organic chemistry

Polymers

Biodegradable polymers

A large number of polymers are quite resistant to the environmental degradation processes and are thus responsible for the accumulation of polymeric solid waste materials. These solid wastes cause acute environmental problems and remain undegraded for quite a long time. In view of the general awareness and concern for the problems created by polymeric solid wastes, certain new biodegradable synthetic polymers have been designed and developed. These polymers contain functional groups similar to the functional groups present in biopolymers.

Aliphatic polyesters are one of the important classes of biodegradable polymers. Some examples are given below.

PHBV – poly(β-hydroxybutyrate-co-β-hydroxyvalerate)

Obtained by the copolymerisation of 3-hydroxybutanoic acid and 3-hydroxypentanoic acid.

MonomerFormula
3-Hydroxybutanoic acid\(\mathrm{CH_3{-}CH(OH){-}CH_2{-}COOH}\)
3-Hydroxypentanoic acid\(\mathrm{CH_3CH_2{-}CH(OH){-}CH_2{-}COOH}\)
\[\begin{aligned}&n\,\mathrm{CH_3CH(OH)CH_2COOH} + n\,\mathrm{CH_3CH_2CH(OH)CH_2COOH}\\&\to \left[\mathrm{O{-}CH(CH_3){-}CH_2{-}CO{-}O{-}CH(C_2H_5){-}CH_2{-}CO}\right]_n + 2n\,\mathrm{H_2O}\end{aligned}\]

Uses: speciality packaging, orthopaedic devices and controlled release of drugs. PHBV undergoes bacterial degradation in the environment.

Nylon-2-nylon-6

An alternating polyamide copolymer of glycine (\(\mathrm{H_2N{-}CH_2{-}COOH}\)) and amino caproic acid (6-aminohexanoic acid, \(\mathrm{H_2N(CH_2)_5COOH}\)); it is also a biodegradable polymer.

\[n\,\mathrm{H_2N{-}CH_2{-}COOH} + n\,\mathrm{H_2N{-}(CH_2)_5{-}COOH} \to \left[\mathrm{NH{-}CH_2{-}CO{-}NH{-}(CH_2)_5{-}CO}\right]_n + 2n\,\mathrm{H_2O}\]

Organic chemistry

Stereochemistry

Chirality and optical isomerism

  • A carbon bonded to four different groups is a chiral (stereogenic) centre; a molecule is optically active if it has no plane, centre or alternating axis of symmetry.
  • Enantiomers: non-superimposable mirror images; equal and opposite specific rotation, identical physical properties otherwise. Diastereomers: stereoisomers that are not mirror images; different physical properties.
  • Racemic mixture (±): equimolar enantiomers, optically inactive by external compensation; separation is called resolution. A meso compound is inactive by internal compensation.
\[[\alpha]_\lambda^T=\frac{\alpha_{\text{obs}}}{l\,c}\quad(l\text{ in dm},\ c\text{ in g mL}^{-1})\]\[\text{Optical purity}=\frac{\text{observed }[\alpha]}{[\alpha]_{\text{pure}}}\times100=\text{enantiomeric excess (ee)}\]

Number of stereoisomers with n chiral centres

CaseOptically active formsMesoTotal
Unsymmetrical (all centres different)\(2^n\)0\(2^n\)
Symmetrical, \(n\) even\(2^{n-1}\)\(2^{\frac n2-1}\)\(2^{n-1}+2^{\frac n2-1}\)
Symmetrical, \(n\) odd\(2^{n-1}-2^{\frac{n-1}2}\)\(2^{\frac{n-1}2}\)\(2^{n-1}\)

Example: tartaric acid (n = 2) has 2 active forms + 1 meso = 3 stereoisomers. Geometrical isomers of a polyene \(R{-}(CH{=}CH)_n{-}R^{\prime}\): unsymmetrical \(2^n\); symmetrical, n even \(2^{n-1}+2^{\frac n2-1}\), n odd \(2^{n-1}\).

CIP rules: R/S and E/Z

  • Priority by atomic number at the first point of difference (I > Br > Cl > S > P > F > O > N > C > H); duplicate atoms are used for double/triple bonds, e.g. \(\mathrm{-CH{=}O}\) ranks above \(\mathrm{-CH_2OH}\).
  • R/S: view with the lowest priority group away; 1→2→3 clockwise = R, anticlockwise = S. If the lowest group is on a horizontal bond of a Fischer projection, reverse the result. Swapping any two groups inverts the descriptor.
  • E/Z: higher priority groups on opposite sides = E, same side = Z (cis/trans applies only when like groups are present).
  • Geometrical isomerism needs restricted rotation (C=C, C=N, N=N, rings) and two different groups on each doubly-bonded atom. Oximes, e.g. \(\mathrm{R_2C{=}N{-}OH}\), show syn/anti isomerism.

Fischer projection and relative configuration

  • Vertical bonds go back, horizontal bonds come forward. Rotating by \(180^\circ\) in the plane keeps the configuration; rotating by \(90^\circ\) or swapping one pair inverts it.
  • D/L relates to glyceraldehyde (OH on the right of the lowest chiral centre = D) and has no fixed relation to the sign of rotation (+/−). Erythro: like groups on the same side; threo: opposite sides.
  • Racemisation proceeds through planar carbocations (SN1) or enolates; SN2 gives inversion (Walden inversion); retention may occur by neighbouring-group participation or SNi.

Conformations

  • Ethane: staggered (most stable) and eclipsed; torsional strain \(\approx12\ \mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\) (3 × 4 kJ).
  • Butane (about C2–C3), stability: anti \(\gt\) gauche \(\gt\) partially eclipsed \(\gt\) fully eclipsed. Gauche is about \(3.8\ \mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\) above anti; fully eclipsed about \(19\ \mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}\). Intramolecular H-bonding can make gauche the most stable (ethylene glycol, 2-fluoroethanol).
  • Cyclohexane: chair (most stable, strain-free, 6 axial + 6 equatorial) \(\gt\) twist-boat \(\gt\) boat \(\gt\) half-chair. Large substituents prefer equatorial (avoid 1,3-diaxial strain); in cis-1,4 one group is axial, in trans-1,4 both equatorial (diequatorial) is most stable.
  • Baeyer strain theory is valid only for planar rings; cyclopentane is slightly puckered (envelope).

Degree of unsaturation

\[\text{DBE}=C+1-\frac{H+X-N}{2}\]

Each ring or double bond counts 1, a triple bond 2, benzene 4. O and S are ignored. For \(\mathrm{C_6H_6}\): DBE = 4.

34
Organic chemistry

Amines, Phenols, Ethers & Diazonium Salts

Preparation of amines

  • Reduction: \(\mathrm{R{-}NO_2,\ R{-}CN,\ R{-}CONH_2\xrightarrow{LiAlH_4\text{ or }H_2/Ni}R{-}CH_2NH_2}\) (nitrile and amide keep the carbon count; nitro arenes also give anilines with Sn/HCl or Fe/HCl).
  • Gabriel phthalimide synthesis: potassium phthalimide + R–X, then \(\mathrm{NaOH/H_2O}\) or hydrazine, gives pure 1° aliphatic amine (fails for aryl halides).
  • Hofmann bromamide degradation: \(\mathrm{R{-}CONH_2+Br_2+4NaOH\to R{-}NH_2+Na_2CO_3+2NaBr+2H_2O}\); amine has one carbon fewer; the intermediate is an isocyanate (retention of configuration).
  • Ammonolysis of R–X gives a mixture of 1°, 2°, 3° amines and quaternary salt.

Properties and tests of amines

  • Basicity (aqueous, aliphatic): ethyl series \((C_2H_5)_2NH\gt(C_2H_5)_3N\gt C_2H_5NH_2\gt NH_3\); methyl series: \((CH_3)_2NH\gt CH_3NH_2\gt(CH_3)_3N\gt NH_3\). Aniline is weaker than NH₃ (lone pair delocalised); \(-NO_2\) lowers and \(-OCH_3,-CH_3\) raise basicity of anilines (ortho effect lowers it).
  • Hinsberg test (\(\mathrm{C_6H_5SO_2Cl}\)): 1° gives a sulphonamide soluble in alkali; 2° gives an insoluble sulphonamide; 3° does not react.
  • Carbylamine test: 1° amine + \(\mathrm{CHCl_3}\) + KOH gives foul-smelling isocyanide.
  • Nitrous acid: aliphatic 1° gives \(\mathrm{N_2}\) + alcohol; aromatic 1° at 0–5 °C gives diazonium salt; 2° gives yellow N-nitrosoamine (Liebermann); 3° aliphatic gives salt, 3° aromatic gives C-nitroso product.
  • Aniline: acetylation protects –NH₂; bromine water gives 2,4,6-tribromoaniline; nitration in strongly acidic medium gives meta product (anilinium ion).

Diazonium salts

\[\mathrm{C_6H_5NH_2+NaNO_2+2HCl\xrightarrow{273-278\,K}C_6H_5N_2^+Cl^-+NaCl+2H_2O}\]
ReactionReagentProduct
SandmeyerCuCl/HCl, CuBr/HBr, CuCN/KCNAr–Cl, Ar–Br, Ar–CN
GattermannCu powder + HXAr–X
Balz–Schiemann\(\mathrm{HBF_4}\), heatAr–F
IodinationKIAr–I
Hydrolysis\(\mathrm{H_2O}\), warmAr–OH
Reduction to H\(\mathrm{H_3PO_2}\) or \(\mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH}\)Ar–H
Coupling (azo dyes)phenol (pH 9–10), aniline (pH 4–5)para azo compound

Aromatic diazonium salts are stable at 273–278 K because of resonance; aliphatic ones are unstable. Coupling uses the diazonium ion as a weak electrophile.

Phenols and ethers

  • Acidity: phenol \(\gt\) alcohols (phenoxide is resonance stabilised); \(-NO_2\) (o,p) increases and \(-CH_3\) decreases acidity. Phenol does not liberate \(\mathrm{CO_2}\) from \(\mathrm{NaHCO_3}\); picric acid (2,4,6-trinitrophenol) does.
  • Reimer–Tiemann: phenol + \(\mathrm{CHCl_3}\) + NaOH → salicylaldehyde (ortho; dichlorocarbene). With \(\mathrm{CCl_4}\) gives salicylic acid.
  • Kolbe (Kolbe–Schmitt): sodium phenoxide + \(\mathrm{CO_2}\) (4–7 atm, 400 K), then \(\mathrm{H^+}\) → salicylic acid.
  • Phenol: bromine water gives 2,4,6-tribromophenol; \(\mathrm{Br_2/CS_2}\) gives p-bromophenol; dil. \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\) gives o- + p-nitrophenol; neutral \(\mathrm{FeCl_3}\) gives violet colour; Zn dust gives benzene; cumene process gives phenol + acetone.
  • Ethers: cleavage by HI: \(\mathrm{R{-}O{-}R^{\prime}+HI\to R^{\prime}I+ROH}\); the smaller/less hindered alkyl forms the halide (SN2); 3° alkyl via SN1; aryl alkyl ether always gives phenol + alkyl iodide. Williamson synthesis needs 1° alkyl halide.

Haloarenes

  • Aryl halides are unreactive to SN1/SN2 because of resonance (partial double-bond character, C–X shorter), sp² carbon and unstable phenyl cation. Dipole moment of chlorobenzene (1.73 D) is less than that of cyclohexyl chloride (2.15 D).
  • Nucleophilic substitution needs ortho/para \(-NO_2\) (addition–elimination via Meisenheimer complex); Dow process (623 K, 300 atm) gives phenol; benzyne mechanism with \(\mathrm{NaNH_2/NH_3}\).
  • Halogens are deactivating but o,p-directing. Wurtz–Fittig: Ar–X + R–X + 2Na → Ar–R. Fittig: 2 Ar–X + 2Na → Ar–Ar.
35
Organic chemistry

Biomolecules

Carbohydrates

  • Glucose \(\mathrm{C_6H_{12}O_6}\) (aldohexose; 4 chiral centres, 16 stereoisomers); fructose (ketohexose; 3 chiral centres, 8 stereoisomers). Glucose is D-(+), fructose D-(−).
  • Cyclic pyranose (glucose) and furanose (fructose) forms; \(\alpha\)- and \(\beta\)-anomers differ at C1 (the anomeric carbon). Mutarotation: \(\alpha\)-D-glucose \([\alpha]=+111^\circ\) ⇌ \(\beta\)-D-glucose \(+19.2^\circ\), equilibrium \(+52.5^\circ\).
  • Evidence for open chain: forms oxime, cyanohydrin, gluconic acid (Br₂ water), saccharic acid (HNO₃), n-hexane (HI/red P), pentaacetate. It does not give the Schiff and NaHSO₃ tests, owing to the cyclic hemiacetal form.
  • Reducing sugars (Tollens, Fehling, Benedict): glucose, fructose (via enediol rearrangement), maltose, lactose. Sucrose (α-glucose C1–β-fructose C2) is non-reducing; hydrolysis (invert sugar) changes rotation from \(+66.5^\circ\) to \(-20^\circ\).
  • Osazone: glucose, fructose and mannose give the same osazone (3 mol phenylhydrazine; they differ only at C1, C2).

Polysaccharides

PolymerUnits and linksRemarks
Amylose (20%)α-D-glucose, C1–C4, unbranchedBlue with iodine, water soluble
Amylopectin (80%)α-D-glucose, C1–C4 and C1–C6 branchesInsoluble
Celluloseβ-D-glucose, C1–C4Linear, structural, not digested by humans
Glycogenα-D-glucose, highly branchedAnimal starch

Amino acids and proteins

  • α-Amino acids \(\mathrm{H_2N{-}CHR{-}COOH}\) exist as zwitterions in neutral solution; all natural ones except glycine are chiral (L-configuration). Essential (10): Val, Leu, Ile, Phe, Trp, Met, Thr, Lys, Arg, His.
\[\mathrm{pI}=\frac{\mathrm{p}K_{a1}+\mathrm{p}K_{a2}}{2}\ \ (\text{neutral amino acids});\quad\text{acidic: }\frac{\mathrm{p}K_{a1}+\mathrm{p}K_{aR}}{2};\ \text{basic: }\frac{\mathrm{p}K_{a2}+\mathrm{p}K_{aR}}{2}\]
  • Peptide bond: –CO–NH– (planar, partial double bond); a tripeptide has 2 peptide bonds; n different amino acids can form \(n!\) tripeptide sequences when n = 3.
  • Structure: primary (sequence), secondary (α-helix, β-pleated sheet; H-bonds), tertiary (folding; S–S, H-bond, ionic, hydrophobic), quaternary (subunits, e.g. haemoglobin). Denaturation destroys secondary and tertiary structure (primary retained). Fibrous (keratin, myosin) vs globular (albumin, insulin).
  • Tests: ninhydrin (violet, amino acids), biuret (violet, peptide bonds), xanthoproteic (yellow, aromatic), Millon’s. Enzymes are specific biocatalysts (lock and key; induced fit).

Vitamins

VitaminDeficiency diseaseSolubility
A (retinol)Night blindness, xerophthalmiaFat
B₁ (thiamine)Beri-beriWater
B₂ (riboflavin)CheilosisWater
B₆ (pyridoxine)ConvulsionsWater
B₁₂ (cobalamin, contains Co)Pernicious anaemiaWater
C (ascorbic acid)ScurvyWater
DRickets, osteomalaciaFat
EWeak RBC, muscular weaknessFat
KDelayed blood clottingFat

Nucleic acids

  • Nucleotide = phosphate + pentose + base; nucleoside = sugar + base. DNA: 2-deoxy-D-ribose; bases A, G (purines), C, T (pyrimidines). RNA: D-ribose; U replaces T.
  • Base pairing: A=T (2 H-bonds), G≡C (3 H-bonds) in the double helix (Chargaff: A = T, G = C). Linkage between nucleotides is a 3′–5′ phosphodiester bond.
  • RNA types: mRNA, tRNA, rRNA. DNA carries heredity (replication); RNA does protein synthesis (transcription, translation).

Lipids and hormones (overview)

  • Fats and oils are triglycerides (glycerol + long-chain fatty acids); saponification value, iodine value (degree of unsaturation) and acid value are used to characterise them. Phospholipids form cell membranes.
  • Hormones: insulin (peptide, from pancreas), thyroxine (iodinated amino acid), steroid hormones (cortisol, testosterone, oestrogen).
36
Physical · Solutions, solids & kinetics

Surface Chemistry

Adsorption

PhysisorptionChemisorption
Forcesvan der WaalsChemical bonds
\(\Delta H\)20–40 kJ/mol80–240 kJ/mol
Specificity, layersNon-specific, multilayerSpecific, monolayer
TemperatureDecreases as T risesRises, then falls
ReversibilityReversibleIrreversible

Adsorption is exothermic (\(\Delta H\lt0\)) with \(\Delta S\lt0\). Freundlich isotherm (valid at moderate pressure):

\[\frac xm=kP^{1/n}\quad(n\gt1)\qquad\log\frac xm=\log k+\frac1n\log P\]

Langmuir: \(\theta=\dfrac{KP}{1+KP}\). At low P, \(x/m\propto P\); at high P, \(x/m\) is constant (saturation).

Catalysis

  • Homogeneous (same phase, e.g. \(\mathrm{NO}\) in lead chamber) and heterogeneous (Fe in Haber, \(\mathrm{V_2O_5}\) in contact process, Ni in hydrogenation). A catalyst lowers \(E_a\), is not consumed and does not change \(\Delta G\) or \(K\). Promoters (Mo for Fe) raise activity; poisons (CO, As) reduce it.
  • Enzymes: sucrase (invertase), zymase, urease, pepsin; highly specific, work at 298–310 K and optimum pH. Zeolites (ZSM-5) are shape-selective catalysts that convert alcohols to gasoline.

Colloids

  • Particle size 1–1000 nm. Lyophilic (reversible, stable: starch, gum, gelatin) vs lyophobic (irreversible: metal sols, \(\mathrm{As_2S_3}\)). Types: sol (solid in liquid), gel (liquid in solid), emulsion (liquid in liquid), aerosol, foam.
  • Preparation: Bredig’s arc (metal sols), peptisation, double decomposition (\(\mathrm{As_2S_3}\)), oxidation (S sol), hydrolysis (\(\mathrm{Fe(OH)_3}\) from \(\mathrm{FeCl_3}\)). Purification: dialysis, electrodialysis, ultrafiltration.
  • Properties: Tyndall effect (scattering, needs size comparable to wavelength), Brownian motion (stability), electrophoresis, electro-osmosis. Charge: positive sols (\(\mathrm{Fe(OH)_3}\), \(\mathrm{Al(OH)_3}\), \(\mathrm{TiO_2}\)); negative sols (metals, \(\mathrm{As_2S_3}\), starch, clay). Origin: preferential adsorption of ions.
  • Coagulation, Hardy–Schulze rule: coagulating power \(\propto\) (charge of the oppositely charged ion)\(^n\): for a negative sol \(\mathrm{Al^{3+}\gt Ba^{2+}\gt Na^+}\); for a positive sol \(\mathrm{[Fe(CN)_6]^{4-}\gt PO_4^{3-}\gt SO_4^{2-}\gt Cl^-}\). Minimum concentration is the flocculation value; smaller flocculation value = greater coagulating power.
  • Gold number: mg of protective colloid that just prevents coagulation of 10 mL gold sol by 1 mL of 10% NaCl; smaller = greater protective power (gelatin 0.005–0.01, starch 25).

Emulsions and micelles

  • Oil-in-water (milk, vanishing cream) and water-in-oil (butter, cold cream); emulsifiers: soaps, proteins, gums. Soaps and detergents are surfactants.
  • Micelles form above the CMC (critical micelle concentration) and above the Kraft temperature; typical aggregation number about 100. Soap = sodium salt of long-chain fatty acid (hydrophilic head, hydrophobic tail); cleansing by trapping grease in the micelle core.
37
Organic chemistry

Purification & Practical Organic Analysis

Purification methods

MethodUse
CrystallisationSolid with temperature-dependent solubility (fractional for mixtures)
SublimationCamphor, naphthalene, \(\mathrm{NH_4Cl}\), benzoic acid
Simple distillation\(\Delta T_b\gt40\ \mathrm K\)
Fractional distillationClose boiling liquids (crude oil)
Distillation under reduced pressureLiquids decomposing at their b.p. (glycerol)
Steam distillationSteam-volatile, water-immiscible liquids (aniline, essential oils)
Differential extractionOrganic compound from water using immiscible solvent
ChromatographyAdsorption (column, TLC), partition (paper)
\[R_f=\frac{\text{distance moved by the substance}}{\text{distance moved by the solvent front}}\ (\le1)\]

Qualitative tests: Lassaigne’s extract

Fusion with sodium gives \(\mathrm{NaCN,\ Na_2S,\ NaX}\) (N, S, halogens).

  • N: \(\mathrm{FeSO_4}\), NaOH, boil, conc. \(\mathrm{H_2SO_4}\) gives Prussian blue \(\mathrm{Fe_4[Fe(CN)_6]_3}\).
  • S: sodium nitroprusside → violet; lead acetate → black PbS. If both N and S: blood-red \(\mathrm{[Fe(SCN)]^{2+}}\).
  • Halogens: boil with \(\mathrm{HNO_3}\) (to remove CN⁻, S²⁻) then \(\mathrm{AgNO_3}\): white (Cl, soluble in \(\mathrm{NH_3}\)), pale yellow (Br, sparingly soluble), yellow (I, insoluble).
  • Phosphorus: oxidise to phosphate, ammonium molybdate gives yellow precipitate. Beilstein test: green-blue flame for halogen (not specific).

Quantitative estimation

\[\%\mathrm C=\frac{12}{44}\frac{m_{\mathrm{CO_2}}}{m}\times100,\qquad\%\mathrm H=\frac{2}{18}\frac{m_{\mathrm{H_2O}}}{m}\times100\]\[\textbf{Dumas: }\%\mathrm N=\frac{28}{22400}\frac{V_0}{m}\times100\ (V_0\text{ mL of }\mathrm{N_2}\text{ at STP})\]\[\textbf{Kjeldahl: }\%\mathrm N=\frac{1.4\,N_{\text{acid}}\,V_{\text{acid}}}{m}\ \ (V\text{ mL of acid neutralised by the }\mathrm{NH_3})\]\[\textbf{Carius: }\%\mathrm X=\frac{\text{at. mass of X}}{\text{mol. mass of AgX}}\frac{m_{\mathrm{AgX}}}{m}\times100,\quad\%\mathrm S=\frac{32}{233}\frac{m_{\mathrm{BaSO_4}}}{m}\times100\]\[\%\mathrm P=\frac{62}{222}\frac{m_{\mathrm{Mg_2P_2O_7}}}{m}\times100\ \ (\text{or }\tfrac{31}{1877}\tfrac{m_{(\mathrm{NH_4})_3\mathrm{PO_4\cdot12MoO_3}}}{m}\times100)\]

Kjeldahl fails for nitro, azo compounds and nitrogen in a ring (pyridine). %O is found by difference or by direct method (\(\mathrm{CO_2}\) from \(\mathrm{CO}\) over \(\mathrm{I_2O_5}\)). Empirical formula: divide % by atomic mass, then by the smallest ratio.
